arXiv:math/0401180v1 [math.DG] 15 Jan 2004




Holonomy and parallel transport in the differential geometry of the space of loops and the groupoid of generalized gauge transformationsThanks: C. A. Rossi acknowledges partial support of Aly Kaufman Fellowship



Carlo A. Rossi

Address: Dept. of Mathematics — Technion —
32000 Haifa — Israel

Email address: crossi@techunix.technion.ac.il



Abstract.
    

The motivation for this paper stems [4] from the need to construct explicit isomorphisms of (possibly nontrivial) principal GG-bundles on the space of loops or, more generally, of paths in some manifold MM, over which I consider a fixed principal bundle PP; the aforementioned bundles are then pull-backs of PP w.r.t. evaluation maps at different points.
    

The explicit construction of these isomorphisms between pulled-back bundles relies on the notion of parallel transport.
I introduce and discuss extensively at this point the notion of generalized gauge transformation between (a priori) distinct principal GG-bundles over the same base MM; one can see immediately that the parallel transport can be viewed as a generalized gauge transformation for two special kind of bundles on the space of loops or paths; at this point, it is possible to generalize the previous arguments for more general pulled-back bundles.
    

Finally, I discuss how flatness of the reference connection, w.r.t. which I consider holonomy and parallel transport, is related to horizontality of the associated generalized gauge transformation.

1. Introduction




In the last section of [4], where the Topological Quantum Field theoretical background behind the higher-order cohomology groups of the space of knots in ℝm{\mathbb{R}}^{m}, for m≥3m\geq 3, of Cattaneo–Cotta-Ramusino–Longoni [2] was explicitly constructed, some hints were mentioned towards possible generalizations of the computations in [4], dealing with iterated Chen-type integrals, to a nontrivial principal GG-bundle PP; in fact, the main object throughout the paper was the so-called generalized holonomy, viewed as an iterated integral, on some fixed principal GG-bundle PP, which we assumed to be trivial in order to simplify the computations.




In the last section, we addressed informally the problem of defining iterated integrals, as our expression for the parallel transport, for a nontrivial bundle PP.
The same problem arises also in Section 2 of [3]: the authors discuss the notion of iterated Chen integrals in relationship with so-called “special connections” on the space of horizontal paths in a (not necessarily trivial) principal bundle over a 44-manifold MM, namely they need iterated Chen integrals of forms of the adjoint type on MM in order to compute the curvature of special connections.
An explicit formula is displayed there for the Chen bracket; still, as the authors themselves pointed out, it is without details, to which they planned to dedicate some further paper.
One of the main subtleties of the task in [3] and [4] lies therein, that we need to identify pulled-back bundles of PP on the space of loops (or, more generally, of paths) in a general manifold MM w.r.t. evaluation maps at different points.
We sketched without details therein some arguments leading to the answer; in particular, we pointed out the importance in this task of the holonomy and, more generally, of the parallel transport.
In fact, the parallel transport, which depends explicitly by its very construction on a connection AA on PP, defines an isomorphism between two particular type of pulled-back bundles on the space of loops or paths in MM.




In the present paper, I explain all details of this construction, performing the complete computations we hinted at in the last section of [4].
The paper is organised as follows: in Section 2, I recall the main notions, namely connections on principal GG-bundles over the manifold MM, and I discuss the two equivalent ways of defining a connection, since I will make use of them both in the subsequent computations.




In Section 3, I recall the notions of holonomy w.r.t. a chosen connection AA of a loop γ\gamma in MM and parallel transport w.r.t. AA of a path γ\gamma; I then state and prove two technical lemmata containing information about some sort of equivariance displayed by holonomy and parallel transport w.r.t. the actions of the structure group GG of PP and the gauge group 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}of PP.
The contents of the first two sections are standard facts in gauge theory; I will nonetheless review them in some detail to fix conventions and notations.




In Section 4, I discuss the notion of isomorphisms of GG-principal bundles (a priori distinct) over MM.It is well known that fibre-preserving, GG-equivariant automorphisms of a fixed GG-principal bundles are in one-to-one correspondence to maps from PP to GG, equivariant w.r.t. conjugation on GG; similarly, fibre-preserving, GG-equivariant (iso)morphisms between (a priori distinct) principal GG-bundles over the same base space MM are in one-to-one correspondence to what I call generalized gauge transformations.
These are maps from the fibred product of the aforementioned bundles (a concept somehow mimicking the notion of Whitney sum of vector bundles) with values in GG, equivariant w.r.t. an action of the product group G×GG\times G on GG, which, when restricted to the diagonal subgroup GG, restricts to the conjugation on GG.
The key element of the above correspondence lies in the canonical map from the fibred product of a bundle PP with itself associated to the identity map of PP; I also discuss its properties.
I then proceed with the discussion of the structure of the set of generalized gauge transformations; it turns out to be (obviously) a groupoid over the category of principal GG-bundles over MM; this notion generalizes that of gauge group, and I therefore speak of the groupoid of generalized gauge transformations of MM and GG.




In Section 5, I partly review, partly explain some general constructions for groupoids; in particular, I recall the concept of actions of groupoids on sets and construct what I call the generalized conjugation for groupoids, which is an action of the product of a groupoid with itself on the groupoid itself, which mimics somehow the conjugation of a group on itself, a notion which no longer makes sense for a groupoid.
I also discuss the notion of equivariant morphisms from (left or right) groupoid actions to other (left or right) groupoid actions.




In Section 6, I interpret holonomy and parallel transport as generalized gauge transformations for a particular type of pulled-back bundles over the space of loops or paths in MM of a fixed principal bundle PP over MM w.r.t. evaluation maps.
Thus, there is a map associating to a connection on PP a generalized gauge transformation; interpreting the gauge group of PP as a groupoid, hence the space of connections on PP as a 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}-space, the technical lemmata of Section 3 may be interpreted in the sense that there is an equivariant map of groupoid actions from the space of connections to the groupoid of generalized gauge transformations.




In Section 7, I discuss the important consequences of the restriction of the map discussed in Section 6 between the space of connections and the groupoid of generalized gauge transformations to the space of flat connections.
Namely, the flatness (which has a non-abelian cohomological interpretation) implies in a highly nontrivial way the horizontality of a the corresponding generalized gauge transformation; the key step for this achieving this result lies in the well-known fact that flat connections induce representations of the homotopy group of MM.



Acknowledgment.



I thank A. S. Cattaneo and G. Felder for many inspiring suggestions, mainly on the subject of groupoids and for constant support; I also acknowledge the pleasant atmosphere at the Department of Mathematics of the Technion, where this work was accomplished.






2. Background definitions




In this section, I introduce the main notions and notations I use throughout the paper; among them, I want to discuss in detail the notion of connection on principal bundles.
Let me notice that I work on a general principal bundle P​↦𝜋​MP\overset{\pi}{\mapsto}M over a real manifold MM (if not otherwise stated, MM is assumed to be connected and paracompact); I do not assume any particular property on the structure group GG.
By 𝔤\mathfrak{g} I denote the corresponding Lie algebra.



Definition 2.1.



A connection 11-form on the principal bundle PP is a 11-form AA on PP with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g}, satisfying the two following requirements:


i) 



(Equivariance)




(2.1)

Rg∗​A=Ad⁡(g−1)​A,\raction_{g}^{*}\!A=\Ad\!(g^{-1})A,



where by Rg\raction_{g} I have denoted the (free) right action of GG on PP.


ii) 



(Verticality) For any ξ∈𝔤\xi\in\mathfrak{g},




(2.2)

Ap​(Te⁡Lp⁡(ξ))=ξ,∀p∈P;A_{p}\!\left(\tange\!_{e}{\laction_{p}}\!(\xi)\right)=\xi,\quad\forall p\in P;



here, I have denoted by Rp\raction_{p} the fibre injection G→PG\to P given by g↦p​gg\mapsto pg.







For our purposes, it is better to introduce a slightly different characterisation of connections; later, I will discuss the relationship between them.




First of all, a tangent vector XX to PP at the point pp is said to be vertical, if it satisfies the equation





Tp⁡π⁡(X)=0.\tange\!_{p}{\pi}(X)=0.



The vertical space Vp⁡P\verti_{p}\!P, consisting of all vertical tangent vectors at pp, for any p∈Pp\in P, is isomorphic to the Lie-algebra 𝔤\mathfrak{g} via





ξ↦Te⁡Lp⁡(ξ),ξ∈𝔤.\xi\mapsto\tange\!_{e}{\laction_{p}}(\xi),\quad\xi\in\mathfrak{g}.



It turns out that the vertical spaces Vp⁡P\verti_{p}\!P can be glued together to give a smooth vector bundle, the so-called vertical bundle V⁡P\verti\!P (whose typical fibre is isomorphic to the Lie algebra 𝔤\mathfrak{g}); it is clearly a subbundle of T⁡P\tange\!\!P.




Alas, there is in principle no canonical way to define a complement of V⁡P\verti\!P w.r.t. Whitney sum, i.e. there is no canonical bundle H⁡P\coh\!P such that V⁡P⊕H⁡P=T⁡P\verti\!P\oplus\coh\!P=\tange\!\!P.
It turns out that the choice of such a complementary bundle relies on the choice of a connection 11-form AA on PP, as it is motivated by the following definition:



Definition 2.2.



Given a connection 11-form AA on PP, a tangent vector XpX_{p} to PP at pp is said to be AA-horizontal if the following equation holds:





Ap​(Xp)=0.A_{p}\!\left(X_{p}\right)=0.



(For the sake of brevity, when the connection 11-form AA is clear from the context, I simply speak of horizontal vectors.)





2.1. The bridge between connection 11-forms and horizontal bundle




I discuss, for the sake of completeness, how a connection 11-form AA on PP gives rise to a smooth assignment to any p∈Pp\in P of a subspace Hp⁡P⊂Tp⁡P\coh_{p}\!P\subset\tange\!_{p}{P}, such that the following two requirements hold:


i) 



Tp⁡P=Vp⁡P⊕Hp⁡P\tange\!_{p}{P}=\verti_{p}\!P\oplus\coh_{p}\!P;


ii) 



Hp​g⁡P=Tp⁡Rg⁡(Hp⁡P)\coh_{pg}\!P=\tange\!_{p}{\raction_{g}}\left(\coh_{p}\!P\right), for any g∈Gg\in G,



and vice versa.
In fact, the two requirements listed above are also an alternative definition of connection as a way of splitting the tangent bundle of PP into the Whitney sum of the vertical bundle, which is clearly GG-invariant as a consequence of the identity





Rg∘Lp=Lp​g∘c⁡(g−1),∀p∈P,g∈G,\raction_{g}\circ\!\laction_{p}=\laction_{pg}\circ\!\conj\!\left(g^{-1}\right),\quad\forall p\in P,g\in G,



and some horizontal bundle.




Given such an assignment, it is possible to define a corresponding connection 11-form via




(2.3)

Ap​(Xp):=ξXp,A_{p}\!\left(X_{p}\right)\colon=\xi_{X_{p}},



where ξXp\xi_{X_{p}} is the unique element of 𝔤\mathfrak{g} which corresponds to the vertical part of XpX_{p} w.r.t. the above splitting, i.e.





Te⁡Lp⁡(ξXp)=Xpv,\tange\!_{e}{\laction_{p}}\!\left(\xi_{X_{p}}\right)=X_{p}^{v},



where XpvX_{p}^{v} is the vertical part of XpX_{p} w.r.t. the above splitting.
It follows immediately that the horizontal space at p∈Pp\in P is exactly the kernel of ApA_{p}.
The very definition of vertical space and Equation (2.3) imply together that AA satisfies (2.2).
The invariance given by Condition ii) above ensures that both projections into vertical and horizontal subspace are GG-invariant, which in turn leads to (2.1).




On the other hand, given a connection 11-form AA on PP, the corresponding splitting of T⁡P\tange\!\!P at some point p∈Pp\in P is given by





Xp=(Xp−XpA)+XpA,XpA:=Te⁡Lp⁡(Ap​(Xp)),p∈P.X_{p}=\left(X_{p}-X_{p}^{A}\right)+X_{p}^{A},\quad X_{p}^{A}\colon=\tange\!_{e}{\laction_{p}}\!\left(A_{p}(X_{p})\right),\quad p\in P.



In fact, by (2.2), it is easy to prove that the linear operator Te⁡Lp∘Ap\tange\!_{e}{\laction_{p}}\!\circ\!A_{p} is the projection onto the vertical subspace; its kernel is therefore the horizontal subspace corresponding to the choice of AA.
Condition (2.1), in turn, alongside the identity





Lp​g∘c⁡(g−1)=Rg∘Lp,∀p∈P,g∈G,\laction_{pg}\circ\conj\!\left(g^{-1}\right)=\raction_{g}\circ\laction_{p},\quad\forall p\in P,g\in G,



ensures that the corresponding distribution is GG-equivariant (c\conj denotes the conjugation on GG).
Finally, let me spend some words on the concept of gauge transformation of a principal GG-bundle PP over MM.
A gauge transformation σ\sigma of PP is a (smooth) map from PP to PP, enjoying the following two properties:





π∘σ=π,σ⁡(p​g)=σ⁡(p)​g,∀p∈P,g∈G.\pi\circ\sigma=\pi,\quad\sigma(pg)=\sigma(p)g,\quad\forall p\in P,g\in G.



The first condition means that any gauge transformation respects the fibres of PP; the second one means that σ\sigma is equivariant w.r.t. the (right) GG-action on PP.
Later, we will see that there is another way of defining gauge transformations of a bundle PP, but, for the moment, let me skip the problem.




Another problem I will address to later is that, in fact, any gauge transformation is an isomorphism; the proof of this fact is easy, but I prefer to postpone it to Section 4, deserving it a treatment in a more general context.




Let me notice that this last fact that means that the set of gauge transformations is a group w.r.t. the product operation given by composition; hence, it makes sense to speak of the gauge group of a principal bundle PP, which I denote by 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}.




Finally, the gauge group 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} operates on the space of connections on PP, which I denote by 𝒜=𝒜P\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{P}, to make manifest the dependence on the chosen bundle PP; the (right) action is given explicitly by





Aσ:=σ∗​A,A∈𝒜P,σ∈𝒢P.A^{\sigma}\colon=\sigma^{*}A,\quad A\in\mathcal{A}_{P},\sigma\in\mathcal{G}_{P}.








3. Holonomy and parallel transport




Let me consider a curve γ\gamma on MM; by the word “curve”, I mean in this context (if not otherwise stated) a piecewise smooth map from the unit interval I\mathrm{I} to MM.



Definition 3.1.



Given a connection 11-form AA on PP, a horizontal lift of γ\gamma based at p∈Pp\in P is a curve on PP, lying over γ\gamma, based at the point pp and such that all its tangent directions are (AA-)horizontal.





I quote without proof from [7], Chapter 2, Section 3, the following Theorem, which is the main ingredient of many of the subsequent constructions:



Theorem 3.2.



Given a connection 11-form AA on PP and a curve γ\gamma in MM, there is a unique horizontal lift of γ\gamma based at the point pp, which I denote by γ~A,p\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}.





Now, it is possible to display two important consequences of Theorem 3.2.




3.1. Holonomy: definition and main properties




I consider a loop γ\gamma, i.e. a curve in MM satisfying γ⁡(0)=γ⁡(1)\gamma(0)=\gamma(1); I choose additionally a point p∈Pp\in P lying over γ⁡(0)=γ⁡(1)\gamma(0)=\gamma(1).
Given a connection AA, by Theorem 3.2, there is a unique horizontal lift γ~A,p\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p} of γ\gamma based at pp.
Since γ~A,p\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p} is a lift of γ\gamma, γ~A,p​(1)\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(1) also lies over γ⁡(0)\gamma(0), and, as GG acts transitively on each fibre of PP, it makes sense to propose the following



Definition 3.3.



The holonomy of γ\gamma w.r.t. the connection AA and base point pp over γ⁡(0)\gamma(0) is the unique element of GG, usually denoted by H⁡(A,γ,p)\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right), satisfying




(3.1)

γ~A,p​(1)=p​H⁡(A,γ,p),\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(1)=p\!\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right),



where γ~A,p\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p} is the unique horizontal lift based at p∈Pp\in P over γ⁡(0)\gamma(0), of the loop γ\gamma.





The structure group GG and the gauge group 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} operate from the right on PP, resp. on the space of connections 𝒜\mathcal{A}.
The holonomy depends by its very construction on a given loop γ\gamma, a connection AA on PP and a base point p∈Pγ⁡(0)p\in P_{\gamma(0)}; hence, it makes sense to consider it as a function on the cartesian product of the space of connections 𝒜\mathcal{A} on PP, the space of loops with values in the structure group GG and the bundle PP itself; later, we will see that it is more precisely a section of some principal bundle over it.




The next Lemma shows how the holonomy behaves w.r.t. the action of GG, resp. of 𝒢\mathcal{G}, on PP, resp. 𝒜\mathcal{A}.



Lemma 3.4.



I assume gg, resp. σ\sigma, to be an element of GG, resp. a gauge transformation; I denote by gσg_{\sigma} the function from PP with values in GG canonically associated to σ\sigma via the formula





σ⁡(p)=p​gσ​(p).\sigma(p)=pg_{\sigma}(p).



(This formula makes sense by the transitivity of the action of GG on any fibre of PP.)




Then , the following formulae hold:




(3.2)

H⁡(A,γ,p​g)\displaystyle\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;pg\right)
=c⁡(g−1)​H⁡(A,γ,p);\displaystyle=\conj\!\left(g^{-1}\right)\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right);


(3.3)

H⁡(Aσ,γ,p)\displaystyle\partr\!\left(A^{\sigma};\gamma;p\right)
=c⁡(gσ​(p)−1)​H⁡(A,γ,p).\displaystyle=\conj\!\left(g_{\sigma}(p)^{-1}\right)\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right).






Proof.



By Definition 3.3, the identity holds





γ~A,p​g​(1)=p​g​H⁡(A,γ,p​g),\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,pg}(1)=pg\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;pg\right),



where γ~A,p​g\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,pg} is the unique horizontal lift of γ\gamma based at p​gpg given by Theorem 3.2.
I consider the curve Rg⁡(γ~A,p)\raction_{g}\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}\right): it is clearly based at p​gpg, and (2.1) implies that





ARg⁡(γ~A,p​(t))​(dd​t⁡Rg⁡(γ~A,p​(t)))\displaystyle A_{\raction_{g}\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(t)\right)}\left(\ddtt\raction_{g}\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(t)\right)\right)
=ARg⁡(γ~A,p​(t))​[Tγ~A,p​(t)⁡Rg⁡(dd​t⁡γ~A,p​(t))]=\displaystyle=A_{\raction_{g}\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(t)\right)}\left[\tange\!_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(t)}{\raction_{g}}\left(\ddtt\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(t)\right)\right]=




=Ad⁡(g−1)​[Aγ~A,p​(t)​(dd​t⁡γ~A,p​(t))]=0.\displaystyle=\Ad\left(g^{-1}\right)\left[A_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(t)}\left(\ddtt\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(t)\right)\right]=0.



Hence, the curve Rg⁡(γ~A,p)\raction_{g}\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}\right) is also horizontal, it lies over γ\gamma (by the GG-invariance of π\pi) and is based at p​gpg.
By the uniqueness in Theorem 3.2, it follows Rg⁡(γ~A,p)=γ~A,p​g\raction_{g}\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}\right)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,pg}, whence





γ~A,p​g​(1)=γ~A,p​(1)​g=p​H⁡(A,γ,p)​g.\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,pg}(1)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(1)g=p\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)g.



Finally, the freeness of the action of GG implies (3.2).




Similarly, again by Definition 3.3, it holds





γ~Aσ,p​(1)=p​H⁡(Aσ,γ,p).\widetilde{\gamma}_{A^{\sigma},p}(1)=p\ \partr\!\left(A^{\sigma};\gamma;p\right).



I claim now




(3.4)

σ⁡(γ~Aσ,p)=γ~A,σ⁡(p).\sigma\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A^{\sigma},p}\right)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,\sigma(p)}.



Both curves σ⁡(γ~Aσ,p)\sigma\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A^{\sigma},p}\right) and γ~A,σ⁡(p)\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,\sigma(p)} lie over γ\gamma, as σ\sigma is a gauge transformation, and both are clearly based at σ⁡(p)\sigma(p).
To prove equation (3.4), it suffices to show that both curves are AA-horizontal by uniqueness of horizontal lifts.
A direct computation shows:





Aσ​(γ~Aσ,p​(t))​(dd​t⁡σ⁡(γ~Aσ,p​(t)))\displaystyle A_{\sigma\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A^{\sigma},p}(t)\right)}\left(\ddtt\sigma\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A^{\sigma},p}(t)\right)\right)
=(Aσ)γ~Aσ,p​(t)​(dd​t⁡γ~Aσ,p​(t))=\displaystyle=\left(A^{\sigma}\right)_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{A^{\sigma},p}(t)}\left(\ddtt\widetilde{\gamma}_{A^{\sigma},p}(t)\right)=




=0,\displaystyle=0,



where the last identity is a consequence of the AσA^{\sigma}-horizontality of γ~Aσ,p\widetilde{\gamma}_{A^{\sigma},p}.
Hence, Identity (3.4) holds true.
Therefore, one gets





σ⁡(p)​H​(Aσ,γ,p)​=By (3.4)​σ​(p)​H​(A,γ,σ⁡(p))\sigma(p)\ \partr\!\left(A^{\sigma};\gamma;p\right)\overset{\text{By (\ref{eq-gaugehor})}}{=}\sigma(p)\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;\sigma(p)\right)



Since the action of GG is free, it follows





H⁡(Aσ,γ,p)\displaystyle\partr\!\left(A^{\sigma};\gamma;p\right)
=H⁡(A,γ,σ⁡(p))=H⁡(A,γ,p​gσ​(p))​=By (3.2)\displaystyle=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;\sigma(p)\right)=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;pg_{\sigma}(p)\right)\overset{\text{By (\ref{eq-liehol})}}{=}




=c⁡(gσ​(p)−1)​H⁡(A,γ,p).\displaystyle=\conj\left(g_{\sigma}(p)^{-1}\right)\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right).



∎






3.2. Parallel transport: definition and main properties




The next object I want to define is the parallel transport w.r.t. a connection AA along a general curve γ\gamma.



Definition 3.5.



Let γ\gamma be a curve in MM, not necessarily closed, and let t∈It\in I and p∈Pp\in P, resp. q∈Pq\in P, be such that





π⁡(p)=γ⁡(0),resp.π⁡(q)=γ⁡(t).\pi(p)=\gamma(0),\quad\text{resp.}\quad\pi\left(q\right)=\gamma(t).



I define the parallel transport from pp to qq along γ\gamma from 00 to tt w.r.t. AA, usually denoted by H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right), as the unique element of GG obeying the rule




(3.5)

γ~A,p​(t)=q​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q),\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(t)=q\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right),



where γ~A,p\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p} is the horizontal lift of γ\gamma.





The parallel transport defined by equation (3.5) satisfies two identities similar in spirit to (3.2) and (3.3).



Lemma 3.6.



Given a connection AA on PP, a curve γ\gamma in MM, p∈Pγ⁡(0)p\in P_{\gamma(0)} and q∈Pγ⁡(t)q\in P_{\gamma(t)}, for some tt in the unit interval, general elements hh and kk of GG and a gauge transformation σ∈𝒢\sigma\in\mathcal{G}, the parallel transport of γ\gamma from pp to qq along γ\gamma from 00 to tt satisfies the two following identities:




(3.6)

H⁡(A,γ,t,p​g,q​h)\displaystyle\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;pg,qh\right)
=h−1​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)​g;\displaystyle=h^{-1}\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)g;


(3.7)

H⁡(Aσ,γ,t,p,q)\displaystyle\partr\!\left(A^{\sigma};\gamma;t;p,q\right)
=gσ​(q)−1​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)​gσ​(p).\displaystyle=g_{\sigma}\!\left(q\right)^{-1}\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)g_{\sigma}(p).






Proof.



By Definition 3.5, one has





γ~A,p​(t)=q​h​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q​h)=q​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q).\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(t)=qh\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,qh\right)=q\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right).



By the freeness of the action of GG on PP, one gets




(3.8)

H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q​h)=h−1​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q),∀h∈G.\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,qh\right)=h^{-1}\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right),\quad\forall h\in G.



On the other hand, the identity holds





γ~A,p​g​(t)\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,pg}(t)
=q​H⁡(A,γ,t,p​g,q)=\displaystyle=q\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;pg,q\right)=




=γ~A,p​(t)​g=\displaystyle=\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(t)g=




=q​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)​g,\displaystyle=q\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)g,



where the second identity was shown in the proof of Lemma 3.4.
It follows therefore




(3.9)

H⁡(A,γ,t,p​g,q)=H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)​g,∀g∈G.\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;pg,q\right)=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)g,\quad\forall g\in G.



Combining (3.8) and (3.9), one gets (3.6).




As for the second identity, I make use again of (3.4): it then holds





γ~A,σ⁡(p)​(t)\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,\sigma(p)}\!(t)
=σ⁡(q)​H⁡(A,γ,t,σ⁡(p),σ⁡(q))=\displaystyle=\sigma\!\left(q\right)\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;\sigma(p),\sigma\left(q\right)\right)=




=σ​(γ~Aσ,p​(t))=\displaystyle=\sigma\!\left(\widetilde{\gamma}_{A^{\sigma},p}(t)\right)=




=σ⁡(q​H⁡(Aσ,γ,t,p,q))=\displaystyle=\sigma\left(q\partr\!\left(A^{\sigma};\gamma;t;p,q\right)\right)=




=σ⁡(q)​H⁡(A,γ,t,σ⁡(p),σ⁡(q)).\displaystyle=\sigma\left(q\right)\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;\sigma(p),\sigma\left(q\right)\right).



The freeness of the action of GG implies then





H⁡(Aσ,γ,t,p,q)\displaystyle\partr\!\left(A^{\sigma};\gamma;t;p,q\right)
=H⁡(A,γ,t,σ⁡(p),σ⁡(q))=\displaystyle=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;\sigma(p),\sigma\left(q\right)\right)=




=H⁡(A,γ,t,p​gσ⁡(p),q​gσ​(q))=\displaystyle=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;pg_{\sigma(p)},qg_{\sigma}\left(q\right)\right)=




=gσ​(q)−1​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)​gσ​(p),\displaystyle=g_{\sigma}\!\left(q\right)^{-1}\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)g_{\sigma}(p),



where the last identity is a consequence of (3.6).
∎





After having introduced the parallel transport of a curve γ\gamma from 00 to a point tt w.r.t. AA and having discussed some of its properties, I may also introduce another object, namely the parallel transport of the curve γ\gamma from ss to tt, where s,t∈Is,t\in\mathrm{I} satisfy s<ts<t.




First of all, given a curve γ\gamma from the unit interval I\mathrm{I} to MM, and given s∈Is\in\mathrm{I}, let me define a new curve γs\gamma_{s} from the interval [0,1−s]\left[0,1-s\right] by the assignment





γs​(t):=γ⁡(t+s),∀t∈[0,1−s].\gamma_{s}\!(t)\colon=\gamma(t+s),\quad\forall t\in\left[0,1-s\right].





Definition 3.7.



Let γ\gamma be a curve in MM, defined on the unit interval I\mathrm{I}; let then s<ts<t two points in I\mathrm{I}, and pp and qq two points in PP satisfying





π⁡(p)=γ⁡(s),π⁡(q)=γ⁡(t).\pi(p)=\gamma(s),\quad\pi(q)=\gamma(t).






The parallel transport of γ\gamma from pp to qq w.r.t. the connection AA, which I denote by H⁡(A,γ,s,t,p,q)\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;s,t;p,q\right), is the unique element of GG obeying the rule




(3.10)

γs~A,p​(t−s)=q​H⁡(A,γ,s,t,p,q).\widetilde{\gamma_{s}}_{A,p}(t-s)=q\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;s,t;p,q\right).



Recalling Equation (3.5), Equation (3.10) is equivalent to




(3.11)

H⁡(A,γ,s,t,p,q)=H⁡(A,γs,t−s,p,q).\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;s,t;p,q\right)=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{s};t-s;p,q\right).







Lemma 3.6 together with Equation (3.11) implies the useful



Corollary 3.8.



Given a connection AA on PP, a curve γ\gamma in MM, two points s,t∈Is,t\in\mathrm{I} obeying s<ts<t, p∈Pγ⁡(s)p\in P_{\gamma(s)} and q∈Pγ⁡(t)q\in P_{\gamma(t)}, general elements hh and kk of GG and a gauge transformation σ∈𝒢\sigma\in\mathcal{G}, the parallel transport of γ\gamma from pp to qq along γ\gamma from ss to tt satisfies the following identities:





H⁡(A,γ,s,t,p​g,q​h)\displaystyle\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;s,t;pg,qh\right)
=h−1​H⁡(A,γ,s,t,p,q)​g;\displaystyle=h^{-1}\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;s,t;p,q\right)g;



H⁡(Aσ,γ,s,t,p,q)\displaystyle\partr\!\left(A^{\sigma};\gamma;s,t;p,q\right)
=gσ​(q)−1​H⁡(A,γ,s,t,p,q)​gσ​(p).\displaystyle=g_{\sigma}\!\left(q\right)^{-1}\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;s,t;p,q\right)g_{\sigma}(p).









4. Isomorphisms of principal bundles and generalized gauge transformations




Let P​→𝜋​MP\overset{\pi}{\to}M, P~​→π~​M\widetilde{P}\overset{\widetilde{\pi}}{\to}M two principal bundles over the same manifold MM and with the same structure group GG; the right action of GG on PP, resp. P~\widetilde{P}, is denoted by R∙\raction_{\bullet}, resp. R~∙\widetilde{\raction}_{\bullet}, or simply by (p,g)↦p​g(p,g)\mapsto pg, resp. (p~,g)↦p~​g(\widetilde{p},g)\mapsto\widetilde{p}g, given the case.



Definition 4.1.



Given two principal bundles PP, P~\widetilde{P} as above, a GG-equivariant map σ\sigma from PP to P~\widetilde{P} is a smooth map from PP to P~\widetilde{P} satisfying the two properties:




(4.1)

π~∘σ\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}\circ\sigma
=π,\displaystyle=\pi,



σ∘Rg\displaystyle\sigma\circ\raction_{g}
=R~g∘σ,∀g∈G.\displaystyle=\widetilde{\raction}_{g}\circ\sigma,\quad\forall g\in G.



The set of all such maps is denoted by 𝒢P,P~\mathcal{G}_{P,\widetilde{P}}.




Remark 4.2.



The set 𝒢P,P\mathcal{G}_{P,P}, for a given principal bundle PP, is the set of gauge transformations of PP.





Given two principal bundles PP, P~\widetilde{P} over MM as above, it is possible to form out of them a manifold as follows:



Definition 4.3.



Given two principal GG-bundles PP and P~\widetilde{P} over the same base space MM, their fibred product, denoted usually by P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P}, is defined as





P⊙P~:={(p,p~)∈P×P~:π⁡(p)=π~​(p~)}.P\odot\widetilde{P}\colon=\left\{\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\in P\times\widetilde{P}\colon\pi(p)=\widetilde{\pi}\left(\widetilde{p}\right)\right\}.







There is a natural map π¯\overline{\pi} from the fibred product P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} to MM, which is simply





π¯​(p,p~):=π⁡(p)=π~​(p~),(p,p~)∈P⊙P~.\overline{\pi}\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\colon=\pi(p)=\widetilde{\pi}\left(\widetilde{p}\right),\quad\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\in P\odot\widetilde{P}.



Additionally, P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} receives a right G×GG\times G-action:





(p,p~,(g,h))↦(p​g,p~​h),∀(p,p~)∈P⊙P~,(g,h)∈G×G.\left(p,\widetilde{p};(g,h)\right)\mapsto\left(pg,\widetilde{p}h\right),\quad\forall\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\in P\odot\widetilde{P},(g,h)\in G\times G.



It is clear that the above action is free, as both actions of GG on PP and P~\widetilde{P} are free; moreover, considering the fibre





(P⊙P~)x:=π¯−1​({x}),\left(P\odot\widetilde{P}\right)_{x}\colon=\overline{\pi}^{-1}\left(\left\{x\right\}\right),



it follows immediately that the action of G×GG\times G is transitive on it.




These two facts are not incidental, because of the following



Proposition 4.4.



The fibred product P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} is a principal G×GG\times G-bundle over MM, with projection π¯\overline{\pi}.




Proof.



If UU is an open set of MM, let me denote by φU\varphi_{U}, resp. φ~U\widetilde{\varphi}_{U}, the trivialization of PP over UU, resp. of P~\widetilde{P} over UU.
A trivialization φ¯U\overline{\varphi}_{U} of P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} over UU may thus be defined via





φ¯:π¯−1​(U)\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}\colon\overline{\pi}^{-1}\!\left(U\right)
⟶U×(G×G)\displaystyle\longrightarrow U\times\left(G\times G\right)



(p,p~)\displaystyle\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)
⟼(π⁡(p),(pr2∘φU)​(p),(pr2∘φ~U)​(p~)).\displaystyle\longmapsto\left(\pi(p);\left(\pr_{2}\circ\varphi_{U}\right)(p),\left(\pr_{2}\circ\widetilde{\varphi}_{U}\right)\left(\widetilde{p}\right)\right).\,



where pr2\pr_{2} denotes the projection from U×GU\times G onto GG.
These maps are invertible, their inverses being given by





φ¯U−1:U×(G×G)\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}_{U}^{-1}\colon U\times\left(G\times G\right)
⟶π¯−1​(U)\displaystyle\longrightarrow\overline{\pi}^{-1}\!\left(U\right)



(x,g,h)\displaystyle\left(x;g,h\right)
⟼(φU−1​(x,g),φ~U−1​(x,h)).\displaystyle\longmapsto\left(\varphi_{U}^{-1}\left(x,g\right),\widetilde{\varphi}_{U}^{-1}\left(x,h\right)\right).



It is clear from their definition that the maps φ¯U\overline{\varphi}_{U} and their inverses are smooth.
Hence, I have obtained a trivialization of the fibred product P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P}.




For the sake of completeness, let me write down explicitly the transition maps of the G×GG\times G-principal bundle P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P}:





φ¯U,V:U∩V\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}_{U,V}\colon U\cap V
⟶Diff⁡(G×G)\displaystyle\longrightarrow\diff\!(G\times G)



x\displaystyle x
⟼RφU,V​(x)×R~φ~U,V​(x),\displaystyle\longmapsto\raction_{\varphi_{U,V}(x)}\times\widetilde{\raction}_{\widetilde{\varphi}_{U,V}(x)},



where φU,V\varphi_{U,V}, resp. φ~U,V\widetilde{\varphi}_{U,V}, are the transition maps of PP, resp. P~\widetilde{P} w.r.t. the trivializations φU\varphi_{U}, φV\varphi_{V}, resp. φ~U\widetilde{\varphi}_{U}, φ~V\widetilde{\varphi}_{V}, for UU, VV any two open subsets of MM with nontrivial intersection.
∎




Remark 4.5.



Let me notice that the fibred product may be also seen as a principal GG-bundle over PP: in fact, by its very definition,





P⊙P~=π∗​(P~),P\odot\widetilde{P}=\pi^{*}\!(\widetilde{P}),



and the latter manifold inherits clearly a principal GG-bundle structure over PP.
(Equivalently, the fibred product may be also viewed as a GG-bundle over P~\widetilde{P}, as P⊙P~=π~∗​(P)P\odot\widetilde{P}=\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\!(P).)




Remark 4.6.



The fibred product of two principal bundles over the same base space and with the same structure group may be seen as an analogue of the Whitney sum of vector bundles for principal bundles.




Furthermore, it is clear that there is a canonical isomorphism





P⊙P~≅P~⊙P,P\odot\widetilde{P}\cong\widetilde{P}\odot P,



for any two principal bundles PP, P~\widetilde{P} over the same base space MM.





Now, there is a left action of G×GG\times G on GG specified by the rule:





c:G×G\displaystyle\conj\colon G\times G
⟶Diff⁡(G)\displaystyle\longrightarrow\diff\!(G)



(g,h)\displaystyle(g,h)
⟼c⁡(g,h)​k:=h​k​g−1.\displaystyle\longmapsto\conj\!\left(g,h\right)k\colon=hkg^{-1}.



It is quite evident that the restriction of c\conj to the diagonal subgroup GG of G×GG\times G gives the usual conjugation of GG on itself; therefore, one can speak of the above action as of the generalized conjugation in GG.



Definition 4.7.



Under the same hypotheses as in Definition 4.3, the set of smooth G×GG\times G-equivariant maps from P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} to GG, denoted by C∞​(P⊙P~,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!\!\left(P\odot\widetilde{P},G\right)^{G\times G}, is the subset of C∞​(P⊙P~,G)C^{\infty}\!\!\left(P\odot\widetilde{P},G\right) of those maps KK satisfying the equivariance w.r.t. the generalized conjugation




(4.2)

K⁡(p​g,p~​h)=c⁡(g−1,h−1)​K​(p,p~),∀(p,p~)∈P⊙P~,(g,h)∈G×G.K\!\left(pg,\widetilde{p}h\right)=\conj\!\left(g^{-1},h^{-1}\right)K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right),\quad\forall\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\in P\odot\widetilde{P},(g,h)\in G\times G.







To a given principal GG-bundle PP over MM, one can associate the canonical map ϕP\phi_{P} on P⊙PP\odot P with values in GG by the rule




(4.3)

q=p​ϕP​(p,q),q=p\phi_{P}\!(p,q),



for any pair (p,q)(p,q) in P⊙PP\odot P.
Notice that OPENi)i) the definition makes sense, because pp and qq lie in the same fibre and GG operates transitively on each fibre, and OPENi​i)ii) the element ϕP​(p,q)\phi_{P}\!(p,q) is uniquely defined since the action of GG on PP is free.




The following proposition illustrates the main properties of ϕP\phi_{P}.



Proposition 4.8.



The map ϕP\phi_{P} defined by (4.3) is smooth, and satisfies the two identities




(4.4)

ϕP​(p,p)\displaystyle\phi_{P}\!(p,p)
=e,∀p∈P;\displaystyle=e,\forall p\in P;


(4.5)

ϕP​(p,q)\displaystyle\phi_{P}\!\left(p,q\right)
=ϕP​(q,p)−1,∀p,q∈P⊙P;\displaystyle=\phi_{P}\!\left(q,p\right)^{-1},\quad\forall p,q\in P\odot P;


(4.6)

ϕP​(p​g,q​h)\displaystyle\phi_{P}\!(pg,qh)
=g−1​ϕP​(p,q)​h,∀(p,q)∈P⊙P,∀(g,h)∈G×G.\displaystyle=g^{-1}\phi_{P}\!(p,q)h,\quad\forall(p,q)\in P\odot P,\quad\forall(g,h)\in G\times G.






Proof.



By considering a local trivialization φ¯U\overline{\varphi}_{U} of P⊙PP\odot P over U⊂MU\subset M open, one can find a local expression for the map ϕP\phi_{P}:





(x,g,h)​↝ϕP,U​g−1​h,(x;g,h)\overset{\phi_{P,U}}{\leadsto}g^{-1}h,



whence it follows immediately that ϕP\phi_{P} is a smooth map.




Moreover, by its very definition, ϕP\phi_{P} is uniquely determined by (4.3).
Taking therefore the pair (p,p)(p,p) in P⊙PP\odot P, by the freeness of the action of GG on PP, both (4.4) and (4.5) follow immediately.




On the other hand, again by (4.3), one gets





p~​h\displaystyle\widetilde{p}h
=p​g​ϕP​(p​g,p~​h)=\displaystyle=pg\phi_{P}\!(pg,\widetilde{p}h)=




=p​ϕP​(p,p~)​h,\displaystyle=p\phi_{P}\!(p,\widetilde{p})h,



whence (4.6) follows again by the freeness of the action of GG on any fibre.
∎




Remark 4.9.



The canonical map ϕP\phi_{P} was called by MacKenzie in [8] the division map of PP; the origin of the name lies obviously in its local description, or, equivalently, in its shape for the trivial bundle.
On the other hand, Mœrdijk [9] called the division map ϕP\phi_{P} (although working in the more general context of principal bundles with structure groupoids) a cocycle on PP with values in GG; I will use the former name for ϕP\phi_{P}.





I have all elements now to state and prove the following Theorem, which gives an alternative characterisation of the maps introduced in Definition 4.1



Theorem 4.10.



There is a one-to-one correspondence between the set 𝒢P,P~\mathcal{G}_{P,\widetilde{P}} of bundle morphisms from PP to P~\widetilde{P}, as in Definition 4.1, and the set C∞​(P⊙P~,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!\!\left(P\odot\widetilde{P},G\right)^{G\times G} of smooth G×GG\times G-equivariant maps from the fibred product P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} to GG, as in Definition 4.7.




Proof.



Consider a bundle morphism σ\sigma from PP to P~\widetilde{P} and take a pair (p,p~)∈P⊙P~\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\in P\odot\widetilde{P}.
By the first property in (4.1), it follows that the pair (p~,σ⁡(p))(\widetilde{p},\sigma(p)) belongs to the fibred product of P~\widetilde{P} with itself.
Therefore, one can consider the function on P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} with values in GG given by





Kσ​(p,p~):=ϕP~​(p~,σ⁡(p)).K_{\sigma}\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\colon=\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p},\sigma(p)\right).



By its very definition, Kσ​(p,p~)K_{\sigma}\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right) is uniquely determined by the equation





σ⁡(p)=p~​Kσ​(p,p~).\sigma(p)=\widetilde{p}K_{\sigma}\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right).



By Proposition 4.8, it follows immediately that KσK_{\sigma} is smooth, since ϕP~\phi_{\widetilde{P}} is smooth and σ\sigma also.
Equation (4.6) implies





Kσ​(p​g,p~​h)\displaystyle K_{\sigma}\!\left(pg,\widetilde{p}h\right)
=ϕP~​(p~​h,σ⁡(p​g))=\displaystyle=\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p}h,\sigma(pg)\right)=




=by (4.1)​ϕP~​(p~​h,σ⁡(p)​g)=\displaystyle\overset{\text{by (\ref{eq-princbunisom})}}{=}\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p}h,\sigma(p)g\right)=




=h−1​ϕP~​(p~,σ⁡(p))​g=\displaystyle=h^{-1}\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p},\sigma(p)\right)g=




=h−1​Kσ​(p,p~)​g.\displaystyle=h^{-1}K_{\sigma}\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)g.



Conversely, given an element KK of C∞​(P⊙P~,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!\left(P\odot\widetilde{P},G\right)^{G\times G}, it is possible to construct a map σK\sigma_{K} from PP to P~\widetilde{P} as follows:





σK​(p):=p~​K​(p,p~),\sigma_{K}\!\left(p\right)\colon=\widetilde{p}\ K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right),



p~\widetilde{p} being any element of P~\widetilde{P}, such that the pair (p,p~)\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right) lies in P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P}.




First of all, one has to show that the map σK\sigma_{K} is well-defined: taking q~\widetilde{q} to be another point in P~\widetilde{P}, such that π~​(p~)=π~​(q~)\widetilde{\pi}\left(\widetilde{p}\right)=\widetilde{\pi}\left(\widetilde{q}\right).
Since p~\widetilde{p} and q~\widetilde{q} belong to the same fibre, the identity holds





q~=p~​ϕP~​(p~,q~).\widetilde{q}=\widetilde{p}\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}\right).



It then follows





σK​(p)\displaystyle\sigma_{K}\left(p\right)
=q~​K​(p,q~)=\displaystyle=\widetilde{q}\ K\!\left(p,\widetilde{q}\right)=




=p~​ϕP~​(p~,q~)​K​(p,p~​ϕP~​(p~,q~))=\displaystyle=\widetilde{p}\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}\right)\ K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}\right)\right)=




=By equivariance​p~​K​(p,p~).\displaystyle\overset{\text{By equivariance}}{=}\widetilde{p}\ K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right).



Therefore, σK\sigma_{K} is well-defined.
It also satisfies π~∘σK=π\widetilde{\pi}\circ\sigma_{K}=\pi by its very definition, and the GG-equivariance follows from





σK​(p​g)\displaystyle\sigma_{K}(pg)
=p~​K​(p​g,p~)=\displaystyle=\widetilde{p}K\!\left(pg,\widetilde{p}\right)=




=By equivariance​p~​K​(p,p~)​g=\displaystyle\overset{\text{By equivariance}}{=}\widetilde{p}K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\ g=




=σK​(p)​g,∀g∈G,\displaystyle=\sigma_{K}(p)g,\quad\forall g\in G,



hence σK\sigma_{K} is a bundle morphism from PP to P~\widetilde{P}.
It remains to show that the assignments





σ↝KσandK↝σK\sigma\leadsto K_{\sigma}\quad\text{and}\quad K\leadsto\sigma_{K}



are inverse to each other.
Namely, one has





σKσ​(p)\displaystyle\sigma_{K_{\sigma}}(p)
=p~​Kσ​(p,p~)=\displaystyle=\widetilde{p}K_{\sigma}\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)=




=p~​ϕP~​(p~,σ⁡(p))=\displaystyle=\widetilde{p}\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p},\sigma(p)\right)=




=by (4.3)​σ​(p),\displaystyle\overset{\text{by (\ref{eq-canbundle})}}{=}\sigma(p),



where p~∈P~\widetilde{p}\in\widetilde{P} is chosen so that the pair (p,p~)∈P⊙P~(p,\widetilde{p})\in P\odot\widetilde{P}.
On the other hand,





KσK​(p,p~)\displaystyle K_{\sigma_{K}}\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)
=ϕP~​(p~,σK​(p))=\displaystyle=\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p},\sigma_{K}(p)\right)=




=ϕP~​(p~,p~​K​(p,p~))=\displaystyle=\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{p}K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\right)=




=by (4.6)​ϕP~​(p~,p~)​K​(p,p~)=\displaystyle\overset{\text{by (\ref{eq-canequiv})}}{=}\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{p}\right)K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)=




=by (4.4)​K​(p,p~).\displaystyle\overset{\text{by (\ref{eq-diagonal})}}{=}K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right).



∎





Notice that the bundle morphism σK\sigma_{K} canonically associated to K∈C∞​(P⊙P~,G)G×GK\in C^{\infty}\!\left(P\odot\widetilde{P},G\right)^{G\times G} is invertible; namely, an explicit inverse map can be defined via





σK−1​(p~):=p​K​(p,p~)−1,\sigma_{K}^{-1}(\widetilde{p})\colon=p\ K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)^{-1},



where p∈Pp\in P is chosen so that the pair (p,p~)(p,\widetilde{p}) belongs to P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P}, and K−1K^{-1} denotes the inverse in GG of KK.
As in the proof of Theorem 4.10, one can show that σK−1\sigma_{K}^{-1} is well-defined, that it is GG-equivariant and satisfies π∘σK−1=π~\pi\circ\sigma_{K}^{-1}=\widetilde{\pi}; in fact,





(σK−1∘σK)​(p)\displaystyle\left(\sigma_{K}^{-1}\circ\sigma_{K}\right)(p)
=σK−1​(p~​K​(p,p~))=\displaystyle=\sigma_{K}^{-1}\!\left(\widetilde{p}K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\right)=




=σK−1​(p~)​K​(p,p~)=\displaystyle=\sigma_{K}^{-1}\!\left(\widetilde{p}\right)K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)=




=p​K​(p,p~)−1​K​(p,p~)=\displaystyle=pK\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)^{-1}\!K\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)=




=p.\displaystyle=p.



It follows analogously that σK∘σK−1=idP~\sigma_{K}\circ\sigma_{K}^{-1}=\id_{\widetilde{P}}.
Notice that I made use of the fact that both definitions of σK\sigma_{K} and σK−1\sigma_{K}^{-1} do not depend on the choice of points in P~\widetilde{P}, resp. PP.




This fact finds its motivation in the following



Lemma 4.11.



Any element σ\sigma of 𝒢P,P~\mathcal{G}_{P,\widetilde{P}} is a bijection.




Proof.



First of all, any σ\sigma in 𝒢P,P~\mathcal{G}_{P,\widetilde{P}} is injective: in fact, assuming





σ⁡(p)=σ⁡(q)\sigma(p)=\sigma(q)



for pp and qq in PP, one gets immediately





(π~∘σ)​(p)=π⁡(p)=π⁡(q)=(π~∘σ)​(q)⇒q=p​ϕP​(p,q).(\widetilde{\pi}\circ\sigma)(p)=\pi(p)=\pi(q)=(\widetilde{\pi}\circ\sigma)(q)\Rightarrow q=p\phi_{P}\!\left(p,q\right).



Hence, it follows





σ⁡(q)\displaystyle\sigma(q)
=σ⁡(p​ϕP​(p,q))=\displaystyle=\sigma(p\phi_{P}\!\left(p,q\right))=




=σ⁡(p)​ϕP​(p,q)=\displaystyle=\sigma(p)\phi_{P}\!\left(p,q\right)=




=!​σ​(p).\displaystyle\overset{!}{=}\sigma(p).



Therefore, by the freeness of the action of GG, it follows





ϕP​(p,q)=e⇒p=q,\phi_{P}\!\left(p,q\right)=e\Rightarrow p=q,



yielding injectivity of σ\sigma.




It remains to prove surjectivity of σ\sigma; for this purpose, it suffices to show that σ\sigma is surjective on any fibre, since the equation σ⁡(p)=p~\sigma(p)=\widetilde{p} implies, by the first equation in (4.1), that (p,p~)∈P⊙P~(p,\widetilde{p})\in P\odot\widetilde{P}.
Hence, taking in any fibre PxP_{x} of π\pi a point px∈Pp_{x}\in P (whose existence is guaranteed by the surjectivity of π\pi), consider its image σ⁡(px)\sigma(p_{x}).
Consequently, taking a general p~∈P~\widetilde{p}\in\widetilde{P}, consider pπ~​(p~)∈Pp_{\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{p})}\in P.
Defining





q:=pπ~​(p~)​ϕP~​(σ⁡(pπ~​(p~)),p~),q\colon=p_{\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{p})}\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\sigma(p_{\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{p})}),\widetilde{p}\right),



a direct computation shows





σ⁡(q)\displaystyle\sigma(q)
=σ⁡(pπ~​(p~)​ϕP~​(σ⁡(pπ~​(p~)),p~))=\displaystyle=\sigma(p_{\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{p})}\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\sigma(p_{\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{p})}),\widetilde{p}\right))=




=by equivariance​σ​(pπ~​(p~))​ϕP~​(σ⁡(pπ~​(p~)),p~)=\displaystyle\overset{\text{by equivariance}}{=}\sigma(p_{\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{p})})\phi_{\widetilde{P}}\!\left(\sigma(p_{\widetilde{\pi}(\widetilde{p})}),\widetilde{p}\right)=




=by (4.3)​p~.\displaystyle\overset{\text{by (\ref{eq-canbundle})}}{=}\widetilde{p}.



∎





The inverse of any bundle morphism σ∈𝒢P,P~\sigma\in\mathcal{G}_{P,\widetilde{P}} is automatically smooth; this can be checked locally or can be viewed as a consequence of Theorem 4.10.



Remark 4.12.



If one considers the case P=P~P=\widetilde{P}, the proof of Lemma 4.11 implies the well-known fact that a gauge transformation, i.e. a bundle endomorphism of PP, is invertible, whence it follows that the set of gauge transformations is a group w.r.t. composition.





4.1. The groupoid of generalized gauge transformations




I consider now the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M} of (smooth) principal bundles over MM with structure group GG.
In order to avoid cumbersome notations, a general object of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M} is labelled by PiP_{i}, for some index ii; the surjective submersion from PiP_{i} to MM is labelled by πi\pi_{i}, and general elements of PiP_{i} are always labelled by the index ii, e.g. pip_{i}, p~i\widetilde{p}_{i}, qiq_{i}.
Theorem 4.10 is a general version of the well-known correspondence between bundle endomorphisms of PP (also called gauge transformations) and (smooth) maps on PP with values in GG, equivariant w.r.t. the conjugation action of GG on itself.
Moreover, this correspondence can be proved to be an isomorphism of groups, because the set of GG-equivariant maps from PP to GG w.r.t. conjugation inherits a group structure from the multiplication in GG.
Obviously, such a group structure is in principle not anymore available, if one considers, instead of a fixed principal GG-bundle PP over MM and its gauge group, all objects of 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M} and its set of morphisms, i.e. GG-equivariant, fibre-preserving maps from an object PP to another object P~\widetilde{P}, which must not be a priori identical.
Lemma 4.11 implies that any morphism of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M} in the above sense is invertible; hence, the morphisms of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M} build a groupoid over the objects of the same category.




Let me deal with the following replacement:





P∈𝖮𝖻⁡(𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M)\displaystyle P\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M})
↝(P1,P2)∈𝖮𝖻⁡(𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M2),\displaystyle\leadsto\left(P_{1},P_{2}\right)\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}^{2}),



C∞​(P,G)G\displaystyle C^{\infty}\!(P,G)^{G}
↝C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×G.\displaystyle\leadsto C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}.



(Here, I have denoted by 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M2\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}^{2} the product category of 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M} with itself.)
It is clear that one can consider the “diagonal” in 𝖮𝖻⁡(𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M2)\mathsf{Ob}\left(\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}^{2}\right), consisting of pairs of the form (P,P)\left(P,P\right), for P∈𝖡𝗎𝗇G,MP\in\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}.
The GG-equivariant, fibre-preserving isomorphisms of PP (gauge transformations) correspond, on the one hand, to the group C∞​(P,G)GC^{\infty}\!(P,G)^{G}, on the other hand, by Theorem 4.10, to the set C∞​(P⊙P,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P\odot P,G)^{G\times G}.




It can be therefore expected that both sets are in bijection; this is in fact true in virtue of the following



Lemma 4.13.



The set C∞​(P,G)GC^{\infty}\!(P,G)^{G} of GG-equivariant maps on PP with values in GG is canonically bijective to the set C∞​(P⊙P,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P\odot P,G)^{G\times G} of G×GG\times G-equivariant maps on the fibred product of PP with itself with values in GG.




Proof.



First of all, define a canonical map from C∞​(P⊙P,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P\odot P,G)^{G\times G} to C∞​(P,G)C^{\infty}\!(P,G) via the assignment





K∈C∞​(P⊙P,G)G×G↝C∞​(P,G)∋gK​(p):=K⁡(p,p),K\in C^{\infty}\!(P\odot P,G)^{G\times G}\leadsto C^{\infty}\!(P,G)\ni g_{K}\!(p)\colon=K\!(p,p),



i.e., consider simply the restriction to the diagonal of P⊙PP\odot P of any element of C∞​(P⊙P,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P\odot P,G)^{G\times G}.
One has to prove that such a map gKg_{K}, which is smooth by construction, belongs to C∞​(P,G)GC^{\infty}\!(P,G)^{G}, i.e. one has to prove its GG-equivariance.
This is an immediate consequence of the fact that the generalized conjugation of G×GG\times G restricts to the usual conjugation, when taking its restriction from G×GG\times G to its diagonal subgroup.




Consider on the other hand the map ϕP\phi_{P} in (4.3), and to a general element kk of C∞​(P,G)GC^{\infty}\!(P,G)^{G}, associate the map KK defined via the rule




(4.7)

K⁡(p,p~):=ϕK​(p,p~)−1​k​(p),K\!(p,\widetilde{p})\colon=\phi_{K}\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)^{-1}k(p),



for any pair (p,p~)(p,\widetilde{p}) in P⊙PP\odot P.
It is clear that the map KK in (4.7) is smooth by Proposition 4.8 and the smoothness of kk; hence, it remains to show that it is G×GG\times G-equivariant.
This is a consequence of the following computation, which follows again from Proposition 4.8:





K⁡(p​g,p~​h)\displaystyle K\!(pg,\widetilde{p}h)
=ϕP​(p​g,p~​h)−1​k​(p​g)=\displaystyle=\phi_{P}\!(pg,\widetilde{p}h)^{-1}k(pg)=




=By equivariance of k and ϕK​(g−1​ϕK​(p,p~)​h)−1​g−1​k​(p)​g=\displaystyle\overset{\text{By equivariance of $k$ and $\phi_{K}$}}{=}\left(g^{-1}\phi_{K}(p,\widetilde{p})h\right)^{-1}g^{-1}k(p)g=




=h−1​ϕK​(p,p~)−1​g​g−1​k​(p)​g=\displaystyle=h^{-1}\phi_{K}(p,\widetilde{p})^{-1}gg^{-1}k(p)g=




=c~​(g−1,h−1)​K​(p,p~).\displaystyle=\widetilde{\conj}\!\left(g^{-1},h^{-1}\right)K\!(p,\widetilde{p}).






It remains to show that the assignments





k↦KandK↦gKk\mapsto K\quad\text{and}\quad K\mapsto g_{K}



are inverse to each other.
In fact, one gets






k⁡(p)↝K⁡(p,p~)=ϕK​(p,p~)−1​k​(p)↝\displaystyle k(p)\leadsto K(p,\widetilde{p})=\phi_{K}\!(p,\widetilde{p})^{-1}k(p)\leadsto




↝gK​(p)=K⁡(p,p)=ϕK​(p,p)−1​k​(p)​=by the properties of ϕK​k​(p).\displaystyle\leadsto g_{K}(p)=K(p,p)=\phi_{K}\!(p,p)^{-1}k(p)\overset{\text{by the properties of $\phi_{K}$}}{=}k(p).



On the other hand,






K⁡(p,p~)↝gK​(p)=K⁡(p,p)↝ϕK​(p,p~)−1​gK​(p)=ϕK​(p,p~)−1​K​(p,p)=\displaystyle K\!(p,\widetilde{p})\leadsto g_{K}(p)=K\!(p,p)\leadsto\phi_{K}\!(p,\widetilde{p})^{-1}g_{K}(p)=\phi_{K}\!(p,\widetilde{p})^{-1}K\!(p,p)=




=by G×G-equivariance of K​K​(p,p​ϕK​(p,p~))​=By the definition of ϕK​K​(p,p~).\displaystyle\overset{\text{by $G\times G$-equivariance of $K$}}{=}K\!(p,p\phi_{K}\!(p,\widetilde{p}))\overset{\text{By the definition of $\phi_{K}$}}{=}K(p,\widetilde{p}).



∎




Remark 4.14.



It is easy to prove that the inverse in GG of the map ϕP\phi_{P} yields an element of C∞​(P⊙P,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P\odot P,G)^{G\times G}, which, by Theorem 4.10, corresponds uniquely to a fibre-preserving isomorphism of PP.
By the very arguments of the proof of Lemma 4.13, it follows immediately that ϕP\phi_{P} corresponds to the identity isomorphism of PP; namely,





idP↝gidP​(p)=e,∀p∈P↝ϕP​(p,p~)−1​e=ϕP​(p,p~)−1,∀(p,p~)∈P⊙P.\id_{P}\leadsto g_{\id_{P}}(p)=e,\quad\forall p\in P\leadsto\phi_{P}\!(p,\widetilde{p})^{-1}e=\phi_{P}\!(p,\widetilde{p})^{-1},\quad\forall(p,\widetilde{p})\in P\odot P.







Motivated by the fact that the correspondence 𝒢P↔C∞​(P,G)G\mathcal{G}_{P}\leftrightarrow C^{\infty}\!(P,G)^{G} is an isomorphism of groups, I construct a “product” on any set of morphisms from P1P_{1} to P2P_{2} in the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}.




Let me just begin with a notational remark:


A general bundle morphism from PiP_{i} to PjP_{j} is denoted by σi​j\sigma_{ij}.

Given three principal bundles P1P_{1}, P2P_{2} and P3P_{3}, and bundle morphisms σ12\sigma_{12} and σ23\sigma_{23}, since σ23∘σ12\sigma_{23}\circ\sigma_{12} is obviously a bundle morphism from P1P_{1} to P3P_{3}, there is a unique element in C∞​(P1⊙P3,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{3},G)^{G\times G}, given by





(σ23∘σ12)↝Kσ23∘σ12​(p1,p3)=ϕP3​(p3,(σ23∘σ12)​(p1)).\left(\sigma_{23}\circ\sigma_{12}\right)\leadsto K_{\sigma_{23}\circ\sigma_{12}}\!\left(p_{1},p_{3}\right)=\phi_{P_{3}}\!\left(p_{3},\left(\sigma_{23}\circ\sigma_{12}\right)(p_{1})\right).



A direct computation gives





(σ23∘σ12)​(p1)\displaystyle\left(\sigma_{23}\circ\sigma_{12}\right)(p_{1})
=σ23​(p2​Kσ12​(p1,p2))=\displaystyle=\sigma_{23}\left(p_{2}K_{\sigma_{12}}\!\left(p_{1},p_{2}\right)\right)=




=σ23​(p2)​Kσ12​(p1,p2)=\displaystyle=\sigma_{23}(p_{2})K_{\sigma_{12}}\!\left(p_{1},p_{2}\right)=




=p3​Kσ23​(p2,p3)​Kσ12​(p1,p2),\displaystyle=p_{3}K_{\sigma_{23}}\!\left(p_{2},p_{3}\right)K_{\sigma_{12}}\!\left(p_{1},p_{2}\right),



where p2∈P2p_{2}\in P_{2} such that π2​(p2)=π1​(p1)=π3​(p3)\pi_{2}(p_{2})=\pi_{1}(p_{1})=\pi_{3}(p_{3}).
The freeness of the action of GG on P3P_{3} and (4.4) imply finally




(4.8)

K13​(p1,p3):=Kσ23∘σ12​(p1,p3)=Kσ23​(p2,p3)​Kσ12​(p1,p2).K_{13}\!\left(p_{1},p_{3}\right)\colon=K_{\sigma_{23}\circ\sigma_{12}}\!\left(p_{1},p_{3}\right)=K_{\sigma_{23}}\!\left(p_{2},p_{3}\right)K_{\sigma_{12}}\!\left(p_{1},p_{2}\right).



(In order to avoid cumbersome notations, I simply abbreviate Kσ12K_{\sigma_{12}} by K12K_{12} and so on.)




Let me make some comments on Equation (4.8).
Since K12K_{12} belongs to C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G} and K23K_{23} to C∞​(P2⊙P3,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P_{2}\odot P_{3},G)^{G\times G}, K13K_{13} as defined in (4.8) does not depend on the choice of p2∈P2p_{2}\in P_{2} such that π1​(p1)=π2​(p2)=π3​(p3)\pi_{1}(p_{1})=\pi_{2}(p_{2})=\pi_{3}(p_{3}): namely, for another representative q2=p2​ϕP2​(p2,q2)q_{2}=p_{2}\phi_{P_{2}}\!\left(p_{2},q_{2}\right), one gets





K13​(p1,p3)\displaystyle K_{13}\!(p_{1},p_{3})
=K23​(p2​ϕP2​(p2,q2),p3)​K12​(p1,p2​ϕP2​(p2,q2))=\displaystyle=K_{23}\!(p_{2}\phi_{P_{2}}\!\left(p_{2},q_{2}\right),p_{3})K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2}\phi_{P_{2}}\!\left(p_{2},q_{2}\right))=




=K23​(p2,p3)​ϕP2​(p2,q2)​ϕP2​(p2,q2)−1​K12​(p1,p2)=\displaystyle=K_{23}\!(p_{2},p_{3})\phi_{P_{2}}\!\left(p_{2},q_{2}\right)\phi_{P_{2}}\!\left(p_{2},q_{2}\right)^{-1}K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})=




=K23​(p2,p3)​K12​(p1,p2).\displaystyle=K_{23}\!(p_{2},p_{3})K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2}).



On the other hand, K13K_{13} as in (4.8) is also G×GG\times G-equivariant w.r.t. the action c~\widetilde{\conj}





K13​(p1​g,p3​h)\displaystyle K_{13}\!(p_{1}g,p_{3}h)
=K23​(p2,p3​h)​K12​(p1​g,p2)=\displaystyle=K_{23}\!(p_{2},p_{3}h)K_{12}\!(p_{1}g,p_{2})=




=h−1​K23​(p2,p3)​K12​(p1,p2)​g=\displaystyle=h^{-1}K_{23}\!(p_{2},p_{3})K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})g=




=c⁡(g−1,h−1)​K13​(p1,p3),∀g,h∈G.\displaystyle=\conj\!\left(g^{-1},h^{-1}\right)K_{13}\!(p_{1},p_{3}),\quad\forall g,h\in G.






For any two objects P1P_{1}, P2P_{2} of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}, consider the set C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G} of generalized gauge transformations from P1P_{1} to P2P_{2}.
For any triple (P1,P2,P3)(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}) of objects of 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}, consider then the following operation





⋆:C∞​(P2⊙P3,G)G×G×C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×G\displaystyle\star\colon C^{\infty}\!(P_{2}\odot P_{3},G)^{G\times G}\times C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}
→C∞​(P1⊙P3,G)G×G\displaystyle\to C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{3},G)^{G\times G}



(K23,K12)\displaystyle\left(K_{23},K_{12}\right)
↦K23⋆K12,\displaystyle\mapsto K_{23}\star K_{12},



where K23⋆K12K_{23}\star K_{12} is defined by (4.8).
Consider then a 44-tuple (P1,P2,P3,P4)\left(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4}\right) of objects of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}, and the three respective sets of generalized gauge transformations:





C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×G,C∞​(P2⊙P3,G)G×GandC∞​(P3⊙P4,G)G×G.C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G},\quad C^{\infty}\!(P_{2}\odot P_{3},G)^{G\times G}\quad\text{and}\quad C^{\infty}\!(P_{3}\odot P_{4},G)^{G\times G}.



It thus make sense to compute iterated operations of the map ⋆\star as follows





K34⋆(K23⋆K12)and(K34⋆K23)⋆K12,K_{34}\star\left(K_{23}\star K_{12}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\left(K_{34}\star K_{23}\right)\star K_{12},



for any K12∈C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×GK_{12}\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}, K23∈C∞​(P2⊙P3,G)G×GK_{23}\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{2}\odot P_{3},G)^{G\times G} and K34∈C∞​(P3⊙P4,G)G×GK_{34}\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{3}\odot P_{4},G)^{G\times G}.
Then, explicit computations give





(K34⋆(K23⋆K12))​(p1,p4)\displaystyle\left(K_{34}\star\left(K_{23}\star K_{12}\right)\right)\!(p_{1},p_{4})
=K34(p3,p4)(K23⋆K12)(p1,p3)=(π3​(p3)=π1​(p1))\displaystyle=K_{34}\!(p_{3},p_{4})\left(K_{23}\star K_{12}\right)\!(p_{1},p_{3})=\quad\text{$(\pi_{3}(p_{3})=\pi_{1}(p_{1}))$}




=K34(p3,p4)K23(p2,p3)K12(p1,p2)=(π2​(p2)=π1​(p1))\displaystyle=K_{34}\!(p_{3},p_{4})K_{23}\!(p_{2},p_{3})K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})=\quad\text{$(\pi_{2}(p_{2})=\pi_{1}(p_{1}))$}




=(K34⋆K23)​(p2,p4)​K12​(p1,p2)=\displaystyle=\left(K_{34}\star K_{23}\right)\!(p_{2},p_{4})K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})=




=((K34⋆K23)⋆K12)​(p1,p4),\displaystyle=\left(\left(K_{34}\star K_{23}\right)\star K_{12}\right)\!(p_{1},p_{4}),



which proves associativity of the operation ⋆\star, whenever it makes sense.




On the other hand, one may consider a pair (P1,P2)\left(P_{1},P_{2}\right) of objects of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}.
As was already proved in Lemma 4.13, any gauge transformation σ∈𝒢P1=𝒢P1,P1\sigma\in\mathcal{G}_{P_{1}}=\mathcal{G}_{P_{1},P_{1}} corresponds uniquely to an element of C∞​(P1⊙P1,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{1},G)^{G\times G}.
In particular, the unique element associated to the identity map on P1P_{1} is simply ϕP1−1\phi_{P_{1}}^{-1}.
It is interesting to compute an explicit expression for K12⋆ϕP1−1K_{12}\star\phi_{P_{1}}^{-1}, for any K12∈C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×GK_{12}\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}:





(K12⋆ϕP1−1)​(p1,p2)\displaystyle\left(K_{12}\star\phi_{P_{1}}^{-1}\right)\!(p_{1},p_{2})
=K12​(q1,p2)​ϕP1​(p1,q1)−1=\displaystyle=K_{12}\!(q_{1},p_{2})\phi_{P_{1}}\!(p_{1},q_{1})^{-1}=




=by independence of the choice of q1​K12​(p1,p2)​ϕP1​(p1,p1)−1=\displaystyle\overset{\text{by independence of the choice of $q_{1}$}}{=}K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})\phi_{P_{1}}\!(p_{1},p_{1})^{-1}=




=K12​(p1,p2),\displaystyle=K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2}),



where π1​(p1)=π1​(q1)\pi_{1}(p_{1})=\pi_{1}(q_{1}).
On the other hand, using the same notations as before, it is possible to compute explicitly ϕP2−1⋆K12\phi_{P_{2}}^{-1}\star K_{12}:





(ϕP2−1⋆K12)​(p1,p2)\displaystyle\left(\phi_{P_{2}}^{-1}\star K_{12}\right)\!(p_{1},p_{2})
=ϕP2​(q2,p2)−1​K12​(p1,q2)=\displaystyle=\phi_{P_{2}}\!(q_{2},p_{2})^{-1}K_{12}\!(p_{1},q_{2})=




=by independence of the choice of q2​ϕP2​(p2,p2)−1​K12​(p1,p2)=\displaystyle\overset{\text{by independence of the choice of $q_{2}$}}{=}\phi_{P_{2}}\!(p_{2},p_{2})^{-1}K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})=




=K12​(p1,p2),\displaystyle=K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2}),



where π2​(p2)=π2​(q2)\pi_{2}(p_{2})=\pi_{2}(q_{2}).
Hence, for any object P1P_{1} of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}, there is an element ϕP1−1\phi_{P_{1}}^{-1}, which corresponds to the identity for the operation ⋆\star.




Finally, for any pair (P1,P2)\left(P_{1},P_{2}\right) of objects of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M} and any bundle morphism between them represented by the generalized gauge transformation K12∈C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×GK_{12}\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}, one defines (see also the last part of the proof of Theorem 4.10) an element K~12\widetilde{K}_{12} as follows





K~12​(p2,p1):=K12​(p1,p2)−1,(p2,p1)∈P2⊙P1.\widetilde{K}_{12}\!(p_{2},p_{1})\colon=K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})^{-1},\quad(p_{2},p_{1})\in P_{2}\odot P_{1}.



It is almost immediate to check that K~12\widetilde{K}_{12} belongs to C∞​(P2⊙P1,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P_{2}\odot P_{1},G)^{G\times G}.
Let us compute explicitly the product K~12⋆K12\widetilde{K}_{12}\star K_{12}:





(K~12⋆K12)​(p1,q1)\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{K}_{12}\star K_{12}\right)\!(p_{1},q_{1})
=K~12​(p2,q1)​K12​(p1,p2)=\displaystyle=\widetilde{K}_{12}\!(p_{2},q_{1})K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})=




=K12​(q1,p2)−1​K12​(p1,p2)=\displaystyle=K_{12}\!(q_{1},p_{2})^{-1}K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})=




=by definition of ϕP1​K12​(p1​ϕP1​(p1,q1),p2)−1​K12​(p1,p2)=\displaystyle\overset{\text{by definition of $\phi_{P_{1}}$}}{=}K_{12}\!(p_{1}\phi_{P_{1}}\!(p_{1},q_{1}),p_{2})^{-1}K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})=




=by G×G-equivariance of K12​ϕP1​(p1,q1)−1​K12​(p1,p2)−1​K12​(p1,p2)=\displaystyle\overset{\text{by $G\times G$-equivariance of $K_{12}$}}{=}\phi_{P_{1}}\!(p_{1},q_{1})^{-1}K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})^{-1}K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})=




=ϕP1​(p1,q1)−1,\displaystyle=\phi_{P_{1}}\!(p_{1},q_{1})^{-1},



where p2∈P2p_{2}\in P_{2} is such that π2​(p2)=π1​(p1)\pi_{2}(p_{2})=\pi_{1}(p_{1}).
On the other hand, similar computations yield





K12⋆K~12=ϕP2,K_{12}\star\widetilde{K}_{12}=\phi_{P_{2}},



whence the assignment





K12∈C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×G↝C∞​(P2⊙P1,G)G×G∋K~12K_{12}\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}\leadsto C^{\infty}\!(P_{2}\odot P_{1},G)^{G\times G}\ni\widetilde{K}_{12}



gives an inverse for the operation ⋆\star.




To any pair of objects (P1,P2)\left(P_{1},P_{2}\right) of 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}, one can associate the set





(P1,P2)↝C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×G≅𝒢P1,P2.\left(P_{1},P_{2}\right)\leadsto C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}\cong\mathcal{G}_{P_{1},P_{2}}.



There are then maps ss, tt, the source and target respectively, from the set of generalized gauge transformations C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}, for any two objects P1P_{1}, P2P_{2} of 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}, to the objects of 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}; the identity map ι\iota from the objects of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}, to sets of generalized gauge transformations of the form C∞​(P⊙P,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P\odot P,G)^{G\times G}, for some object PP of 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}, defined respectively via





s⁡(K12)\displaystyle s\left(K_{12}\right)
:=P1,K12∈C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×G,\displaystyle\colon=P_{1},\quad K_{12}\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G},



t⁡(K12)\displaystyle t\left(K_{12}\right)
:=P2,K12∈C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×G,\displaystyle\colon=P_{2},\quad K_{12}\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G},



i⁡(P)\displaystyle i\left(P\right)
:=ϕP−1∈C∞​(P⊙P,G)G×G≅C∞​(P,G)G.\displaystyle\colon=\phi_{P}^{-1}\in C^{\infty}\!(P\odot P,G)^{G\times G}\cong C^{\infty}\!(P,G)^{G}.



There is a partially defined, associative product of the set of sets of the form C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×GC^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}:





⋆:C∞​(P2⊙P3,G)G×G×C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×G\displaystyle\star:C^{\infty}\!(P_{2}\odot P_{3},G)^{G\times G}\times C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}
→C∞​(P1⊙P3,G)G×G\displaystyle\to C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{3},G)^{G\times G}



(K23,K12)\displaystyle\left(K_{23},K_{12}\right)
↦K23⋆K12.\displaystyle\mapsto K_{23}\star K_{12}.



It is obvious that





s⁡(K23⋆K12)\displaystyle s\left(K_{23}\star K_{12}\right)
=P1=s⁡(K12),\displaystyle=P_{1}=s\left(K_{12}\right),



t⁡(K23⋆K12)\displaystyle t\left(K_{23}\star K_{12}\right)
=P3=t⁡(K23),\displaystyle=P_{3}=t\left(K_{23}\right),



s⁡(i⁡(P))\displaystyle s\left(i\left(P\right)\right)
=P=t⁡(i⁡(P));\displaystyle=P=t\left(i\left(P\right)\right);



i⁡(t⁡(K12))⋆K12\displaystyle i\left(t\left(K_{12}\right)\right)\star K_{12}
=K12,K12⋆i⁡(s⁡(K12))=K12,∀K12∈C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×G.\displaystyle=K_{12},\quad K_{12}\star i\left(s\left(K_{12}\right)\right)=K_{12},\quad\forall K_{12}\in C^{\infty}\!\left(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G\right)^{G\times G}.



It was also proved that there exists, for any K12∈C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×GK_{12}\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G)^{G\times G}, a unique element, previously denoted by K~12∈C∞​(P2⊙P1,G)G×G\widetilde{K}_{12}\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{2}\odot P_{1},G)^{G\times G}, which satisfies the property





K12⋆K~12=ϕP2=i⁡(t⁡(K12)),K~12⋆K12=ϕP1=i⁡(s⁡(K12)).K_{12}\star\widetilde{K}_{12}=\phi_{P_{2}}=i\left(t\left(K_{12}\right)\right),\quad\widetilde{K}_{12}\star K_{12}=\phi_{P_{1}}=i\left(s\left(K_{12}\right)\right).



Hence, from now on, I switch to the notation K~12:=K12−1\widetilde{K}_{12}\colon=K^{-1}_{12}, meaning not the inverse of K12K_{12} w.r.t. the multiplication in GG, but w.r.t. the operation ⋆\star.






5. Some general constructions for groupoids




In this Section, I discuss some general facts about groupoids; I briefly discuss the concept of product groupoid, and, in more details, the concept of left and right 𝒢\mathcal{G}-spaces, for a general groupoid 𝒢\mathcal{G}.
In particular, I introduce the notion of generalized conjugation for groupoids, since a natural notion of conjugation action for groupoids is in some sense elusive.
I discuss later the concept of morphisms of groupoids, and later, of equivariant maps between left (and right) groupoid spaces, where the actions may come from distinct groupoids.




To begin with, a general groupoid 𝒢\mathcal{G}, without any assumption on smooth structures, consists of a six-tuple (𝒢,X𝒢,s𝒢,t𝒢,ι𝒢,j𝒢)\left(\mathcal{G},X_{\mathcal{G}},s_{\mathcal{G}},t_{\mathcal{G}},\iota_{\mathcal{G}},j_{\mathcal{G}}\right), whose elements are respectively two sets 𝒢\mathcal{G} (the set of arrows) and X𝒢X_{\mathcal{G}} (the set of points or the set of arrows), two surjective maps s𝒢s_{\mathcal{G}} and t𝒢t_{\mathcal{G}} from 𝒢\mathcal{G} to XX, the source and target of the groupoid, a map ι𝒢\iota_{\mathcal{G}} from XX to 𝒢\mathcal{G}, the identity, and a map j𝒢j_{\mathcal{G}} from 𝒢\mathcal{G} to itself, the inversion of 𝒢\mathcal{G}; these maps satisfy a series of axioms, for which see e.g. [10].
Moreover, there is a well-defined partial product on the set of arrows, which is defined as follows: the set 𝒢2⊂𝒢×𝒢\mathcal{G}_{2}\subset\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G} consists of all pairs of elements (g1,g2)\left(g_{1},g_{2}\right) of arrows, such that





s𝒢​(g1)=t𝒢​(g2);s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{1})=t_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2});



then, there is an operation from 𝒢2\mathcal{G}_{2} to 𝒢\mathcal{G}, which is denoted as follows





𝒢2\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}
→𝒢\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G}



(g1,g2)\displaystyle(g_{1},g_{2})
↦g1​g2.\displaystyle\mapsto g_{1}g_{2}.



The product has to be associative, whenever it makes sense:





(g1​g2)​g3=g1​(g2​g3),s𝒢​(g1)=t𝒢​(g2),s𝒢​(g2)=t𝒢​(g3).(g_{1}g_{2})g_{3}=g_{1}(g_{2}g_{3}),\quad s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{1})=t_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2}),\quad s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2})=t_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{3}).



The identity ι𝒢\iota_{\mathcal{G}} and the inversion j𝒢j_{\mathcal{G}} have to satisfy additional identities involving the product, for which I refer to [10].




5.1. The product groupoid of two groupoids 𝒢\mathcal{G}, ℋ\mathcal{H}




Let me introduce briefly the concept of product groupoid of two groupoids 𝒢\mathcal{G} and ℋ\mathcal{H}, which plays a central rôle in the definition of the generalized conjugation for a groupoid 𝒢\mathcal{G} (see Subsection 5.5).
Given two groupoids 𝒢\mathcal{G} and ℋ\mathcal{H}, with respective sources, targets, identities and inversions, one may form the product groupoid of 𝒢\mathcal{G} and ℋ\mathcal{H} by putting


i) 



the product set 𝒢×ℋ\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H} as the set of arrows of the product groupoid;


ii) 



the product set X𝒢×XℋX_{\mathcal{G}}\times X_{\mathcal{H}} as the set of objects of the product groupoid;


iii) 



the map





s𝒢×ℋ​(g,h):=(s𝒢​(g),sℋ​(h)),∀(g,h)∈𝒢×ℋ,s_{\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H}}\!\left(g,h\right)\colon=\left(s_{\mathcal{G}}(g),s_{\mathcal{H}}(h)\right),\quad\forall(g,h)\in\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H},



as the source map of the product groupoid;


iv) 



the map





t𝒢×ℋ​(g,h):=(t𝒢​(g),tℋ​(h)),∀(g,h)∈𝒢×ℋ,t_{\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H}}\!\left(g,h\right)\colon=\left(t_{\mathcal{G}}(g),t_{\mathcal{H}}(h)\right),\quad\forall(g,h)\in\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H},



as the target map of the product groupoid;


v) 



the map





ι𝒢×ℋ​(x,y):=(ι𝒢​(x),ιℋ​(y)),∀(x,y)∈X𝒢×Xℋ,\iota_{\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H}}\!(x,y)\colon=\left(\iota_{\mathcal{G}}(x),\iota_{\mathcal{H}}(y)\right),\quad\forall(x,y)\in X_{\mathcal{G}}\times X_{\mathcal{H}},



as the identity of the product groupoid;


vi) 



the map





j𝒢×ℋ​(g,h):=(j𝒢​(g),jℋ​(h)),∀(g,h)∈𝒢×ℋ,j_{\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H}}\!(g,h)\colon=\left(j_{\mathcal{G}}(g),j_{\mathcal{H}}(h)\right),\quad\forall(g,h)\in\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H},



as the inversion of the product groupoid;


vii) 



the partial product of the product groupoid is defined by the assignment





(g1,h1)​(g2,h2):=(g1​g2,h1​h2),s𝒢×ℋ​(g1,h1)=t𝒢×ℋ​(g2,h2).(g_{1},h_{1})(g_{2},h_{2})\colon=(g_{1}g_{2},h_{1}h_{2}),\quad s_{\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H}}\!\left(g_{1},h_{1}\right)=t_{\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{H}}\!\left(g_{2},h_{2}\right).





Notice that the definition of product makes sense by the very definition of the source and target map in the product groupoid.
It is immediate to check that all axioms of groupoid are satisfied.





5.2. Morphisms of groupoids




Given two groupoids 𝒢\mathcal{G} and ℋ\mathcal{H}, I want to clarify the notion of morphisms of groupoids.



Definition 5.1.



A morphism from the groupoid 𝒢\mathcal{G} to the groupoid ℋ\mathcal{H} is a pair of maps (Φ,φ)\left(\Phi,\varphi\right), such that the map Φ:𝒢→ℋ\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H} is a map between the respective sets of arrows and the map φ:X𝒢→Xℋ\varphi:X_{\mathcal{G}}\to X_{\mathcal{H}} is a map between the respective sets of objects.




Moreover, the following two conditions must hold:


i) 



the three diagrams must commute




(5.1)

𝒢→Φℋs𝒢↓↓sℋX𝒢→φXℋ,𝒢→Φℋt𝒢↓↓tℋXℋ→φXℋandX𝒢→φXℋι𝒢↓↓ιℋℋ→Φℋ;\begin{CD}\mathcal{G}@>{\Phi}>{}>\mathcal{H}\\
@V{s_{\mathcal{G}}}V{}V@V{}V{s_{\mathcal{H}}}V\\
X_{\mathcal{G}}@>{\varphi}>{}>X_{\mathcal{H}}\end{CD}\quad,\quad\begin{CD}\mathcal{G}@>{\Phi}>{}>\mathcal{H}\\
@V{t_{\mathcal{G}}}V{}V@V{}V{t_{\mathcal{H}}}V\\
X_{\mathcal{H}}@>{\varphi}>{}>X_{\mathcal{H}}\end{CD}\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{CD}X_{\mathcal{G}}@>{\varphi}>{}>X_{\mathcal{H}}\\
@V{\iota_{\mathcal{G}}}V{}V@V{}V{\iota_{\mathcal{H}}}V\\
\mathcal{H}@>{\Phi}>{}>\mathcal{H}\end{CD}\quad;




ii) 



the identity holds




(5.2)

Φ⁡(g1​g2)=Φ⁡(g1)​Φ​(g2),s𝒢​(g1)=t𝒢​(g2).\Phi(g_{1}g_{2})=\Phi(g_{1})\Phi(g_{2}),\quad s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{1})=t_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2}).








Remark 5.2.



Notice that the commutativity of the first two diagrams in (5.1) implies that both sides of (5.2) are well-defined.




Moreover, it is immediate to check the identity





Φ∘j𝒢=jℋ∘Φ.\Phi\circ j_{\mathcal{G}}=j_{\mathcal{H}}\circ\Phi.








5.3. The opposite groupoid




I discuss now the notion of opposite groupoid of a groupoid 𝒢\mathcal{G}, which is denoted by 𝒢o​p\mathcal{G}^{\op}.




Set


i) 



the set of arrows 𝒢\mathcal{G} of the initial groupoid as the set of arrows of the opposite groupoid;


ii) 



the set of objects X𝒢X_{\mathcal{G}} of the initial groupoid as the set of objects of the opposite groupoid;


iii) 



the map





s𝒢o​p:=t𝒢s_{\mathcal{G}}^{\op}\colon=t_{\mathcal{G}}



as the source map of the opposite groupoid;


iv) 



the map





t𝒢o​p:=s𝒢t_{\mathcal{G}}^{\op}\colon=s_{\mathcal{G}}



as the target map of the opposite groupoid;


v) 



the map





ι𝒢o​p:=ι𝒢\iota_{\mathcal{G}}^{\op}\colon=\iota_{\mathcal{G}}



as the identity of the opposite groupoid;


vi) 



the map





j𝒢o​p:=j𝒢j_{\mathcal{G}}^{\op}\colon=j_{\mathcal{G}}



as the inversion of the opposite groupoid;


vii) 



the product ⋅o​p\cdot_{\op}, defined via





g1⋅o​pg2:=g2​g1,s𝒢o​p​(g1)=t𝒢o​p​(g2),g_{1}\cdot_{\op}g_{2}\colon=g_{2}g_{1},\quad s_{\mathcal{G}}^{\op}(g_{1})=t_{\mathcal{G}}^{\op}(g_{2}),



as the product on the opposite groupoid.



It is easy to check that the opposite groupoid, as defined above, satisfies all groupoid axioms; moreover, the pair (j𝒢,idX𝒢)\left(j_{\mathcal{G}},\id_{X_{\mathcal{G}}}\right) is an isomorphisms of groupoids from 𝒢\mathcal{G} to the opposite groupoid 𝒢o​p\mathcal{G}^{\op}.





5.4. Left- and right 𝒢\mathcal{G} actions for the groupoid 𝒢\mathcal{G}




Given a groupoid 𝒢\mathcal{G} and a set MM, the next task is to define a natural notion of left 𝒢\mathcal{G}-action on the set MM.



Definition 5.3.



A left 𝒢\mathcal{G}-action of the groupoid 𝒢\mathcal{G} on the set MM consists of a 33-tuple (M,JM,ΨM)\left(M,J_{M},\Psi_{M}\right), where OPENi)i) JMJ_{M} is a map from MM to the set of objects X𝒢X_{\mathcal{G}} of 𝒢\mathcal{G} (which is called the momentum map or shortly the momentum of the action) and OPENi​i)ii) a map ΨM\Psi_{M} from 𝒢×JMM\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{M}}M to MM, where





𝒢×JMM:={(g,m)∈𝒢×M:s𝒢​(g)=JM​(m)}.\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{M}}M\colon=\left\{(g,m)\in\mathcal{G}\times M\colon s_{\mathcal{G}}(g)=J_{M}(m)\right\}.



It is customary to write





ΨM​(g,m):=g​m\Psi_{M}\!\left(g,m\right)\colon=gm






Moreover, the following requirements must hold


i) 







JM​(g​m)=t𝒢​(g),∀(g,m)∈𝒢×JMM;J_{M}\!\left(gm\right)=t_{\mathcal{G}}(g),\quad\forall(g,m)\in\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{M}}M;




ii) 







g1​(g2​m)=(g1​g2)​m,∀(g1,g2)∈𝒢2,(g1​g2,m)∈𝒢×JMM;g_{1}\left(g_{2}m\right)=\left(g_{1}g_{2}\right)m,\quad\forall(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathcal{G}_{2},(g_{1}g_{2},m)\in\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{M}}M;



observe that Condition OPENi)\mathnormal{i}) implies that the previous identity is well-defined.


iii) 







ι𝒢​(JM​(m))​m=m,∀m∈M.\iota_{\mathcal{G}}\left(J_{M}(m)\right)m=m,\quad\forall m\in M.








Remark 5.4.



The definition of right 𝒢\mathcal{G}-action is similar, the only difference being that one has to switch the rôles of the source and target maps; consequently, the map ΨM\Psi_{M} goes from the product M×JM𝒢M\times_{J_{M}}\mathcal{G} to MM, and is denoted by





ΨM​(m,g):=m​g.\Psi_{M}(m,g)\colon=mg.



Equivalently, a right 𝒢\mathcal{G}-action is a left 𝒢o​p\mathcal{G}^{\op}-action, and the switch between the two actions is simply given by the inversion j𝒢j_{\mathcal{G}}.






5.5. The generalized conjugation of 𝒢\mathcal{G}




It is well-known that there is in general no natural notion of conjugation action on a groupoid.
Nonetheless, as it is discussed in [11], the conjugation action on an abstract group GG may be viewed as a specialization of the so-called generalized conjugation discussed at the beginning of Section 4; the latter action admits an easy generalization to the context of groupoids, which I am now going to discuss in detail.




First of all, consider the product groupoid 𝒢2\mathcal{G}^{2} of 𝒢\mathcal{G} with itself; the aim is to construct a right action of 𝒢2\mathcal{G}^{2} on 𝒢\mathcal{G}.
The first ingredient one needs is a map from 𝒢\mathcal{G} to the product of the set of objects X𝒢X_{\mathcal{G}} with itself, the momentum of the (right) generalized conjugation: namely, I define





Jc​(g):=(s𝒢​(g),t𝒢​(g)),∀g∈𝒢.J_{\conj}\!\left(g\right)\colon=\left(s_{\mathcal{G}}(g),t_{\mathcal{G}}(g)\right),\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{G}.



Consequently, the set 𝒢×Jc𝒢2\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{\conj}}\mathcal{G}^{2} has the form





𝒢×Jc𝒢2={(g3,g1,g2)∈𝒢3:{t𝒢​(g1)=s𝒢​(g3)t𝒢​(g2)=t𝒢​(g3)}.\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{\conj}}\mathcal{G}^{2}=\left\{\left(g_{3};g_{1},g_{2}\right)\in\mathcal{G}^{3}\colon\begin{cases}t_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{1})&=s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{3})\\
t_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2})&=t_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{3})\end{cases}\right\}.



It makes thus sense to define the map Ψc\Psi_{\conj} from 𝒢×Jc𝒢2\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{\conj}}\mathcal{G}^{2} to 𝒢\mathcal{G} by the rule




(5.3)

Ψc​(g3,g1,g2):=g2−1​g3​g1,\Psi_{\conj}\!\left(g_{3};g_{1},g_{2}\right)\colon=g_{2}^{-1}g_{3}g_{1},



where, for the sake of simplicity, g2−1:=j𝒢​(g2)g_{2}^{-1}\colon=j_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2}).




These data fit into a right 𝒢2\mathcal{G}^{2}-action on 𝒢\mathcal{G} in the sense of Definition 5.3, as shown in the following



Proposition 5.5.



The triple (𝒢2,Jc,Ψc)\left(\mathcal{G}^{2},J_{\conj},\Psi_{\conj}\right) defines a right 𝒢2\mathcal{G}^{2}-action on 𝒢\mathcal{G}, which is called the generalized conjugation of 𝒢\mathcal{G}.




Proof.



First of all, let me compute, for any triple (g3,g1,g2)\left(g_{3};g_{1},g_{2}\right) in 𝒢×Jc𝒢2\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{\conj}}\mathcal{G}^{2}, the following expression:





(Jc∘Ψc)​(g3,g1,g2)\displaystyle\left(J_{\conj}\circ\Psi_{\conj}\right)\!\left(g_{3};g_{1},g_{2}\right)
=Jc​(g2−1​g3​g1)=\displaystyle=J_{\conj}\!\left(g_{2}^{-1}g_{3}g_{1}\right)=




=by definition of Jc​(s𝒢​(g2−1​g3​g1),t𝒢​(g2−1​g3​g1))=\displaystyle\overset{\text{by definition of $J_{\conj}$}}{=}\left(s_{\mathcal{G}}\!\left(g_{2}^{-1}g_{3}g_{1}\right),t_{\mathcal{G}}\!\left(g_{2}^{-1}g_{3}g_{1}\right)\right)=




=(s𝒢​(g1),t𝒢​(g2−1))=\displaystyle=\left(s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{1}),t_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2}^{-1})\right)=




=(s𝒢​(g1),s𝒢​(g2))=\displaystyle=\left(s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{1}),s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2})\right)=




=s𝒢2​(g1,g2),\displaystyle=s_{\mathcal{G}^{2}}\!\left(g_{1},g_{2}\right),



which proves the first requirement for (𝒢2,Jc,Ψc)\left(\mathcal{G}^{2},J_{\conj},\Psi_{\conj}\right) to be a right 𝒢2\mathcal{G}^{2} action.




Second, I compute explicitly





Ψc​(Ψc​(g3,g1,g2),h1,h2)\displaystyle\Psi_{\conj}\!\left(\Psi_{\conj}\!\left(g_{3};g_{1},g_{2}\right);h_{1},h_{2}\right)
=Ψc​(g2−1​g3​g1,h1,h2)=\displaystyle=\Psi_{\conj}\!\left(g_{2}^{-1}g_{3}g_{1};h_{1},h_{2}\right)=




=h2−1​(g2−1​g3​g1)​h1=\displaystyle=h_{2}^{-1}\left(g_{2}^{-1}g_{3}g_{1}\right)h_{1}=




=(g2​h2)−1​g3​(g1​h1)=\displaystyle=\left(g_{2}h_{2}\right)^{-1}g_{3}(g_{1}h_{1})=




=Ψc​(g3,g1​h1,g2​h2),\displaystyle=\Psi_{\conj}\!\left(g_{3};g_{1}h_{1},g_{2}h_{2}\right),



whenever the identity makes sense.




Finally, one has to compute





Ψc(g;ι𝒢2(Jc(g)))\displaystyle\Psi_{\conj}\!\left(g_{;}\iota_{\mathcal{G}^{2}}\!\left(J_{\conj}(g)\right)\right)
=Ψc​(g,ι𝒢​(s𝒢​(g)),ι𝒢​(t𝒢​(g)))=\displaystyle=\Psi_{\conj}\!\left(g;\iota_{\mathcal{G}}(s_{\mathcal{G}}(g)),\iota_{\mathcal{G}}(t_{\mathcal{G}}(g))\right)=




=ι𝒢​(t𝒢​(g))−1​g​ι𝒢​(s𝒢​(g))=\displaystyle=\iota_{\mathcal{G}}(t_{\mathcal{G}}(g))^{-1}g\iota_{\mathcal{G}}(s_{\mathcal{G}}(g))=




=g,∀g∈𝒢,\displaystyle=g,\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{G},



whence the claim follows.
∎




Remark 5.6.



There is another right 𝒢2\mathcal{G}^{2}-action on 𝒢\mathcal{G}; in fact, one can consider the map J¯c\overline{J}_{\conj} from 𝒢\mathcal{G} to X𝒢×X𝒢X_{\mathcal{G}}\times X_{\mathcal{G}} given by





J¯c​(g):=(t𝒢​(g),s𝒢​(g)),\overline{J}_{\conj}(g)\colon=\left(t_{\mathcal{G}}(g),s_{\mathcal{G}}(g)\right),



and the map Ψ¯c\overline{\Psi}_{\conj} from 𝒢×J¯c𝒢2\mathcal{G}\times_{\overline{J}_{\conj}}\mathcal{G}^{2} to 𝒢\mathcal{G} via





Ψ¯c​(g3,g1,g2):=g1−1​g3​g2.\overline{\Psi}_{\conj}\!\left(g_{3};g_{1},g_{2}\right)\colon=g_{1}^{-1}g_{3}g_{2}.







The action of Proposition 5.5, as well as the action of Remark 5.6, are called both generalized conjugations of 𝒢\mathcal{G}, because they are the natural analogues of the generalized conjugation of a group GG, which I have briefly discussed at the beginning of Section 4.




Notice that there are also two left generalized conjugations of 𝒢\mathcal{G}: namely, take
