{JcL​(g):=Jc(g),ΨcL(g1,g2;g3):=g2g3g1−1,J¯cL​(g):=J¯c(g),Ψ¯cL(g1,g2;g3):=g1g3g2−1,\begin{cases}J_{\conj}^{L}(g)&\colon=J_{\conj}(g),\quad\Psi_{\conj}^{L}\!\left(g_{1},g_{2};g_{3}\right)\colon=g_{2}g_{3}g_{1}^{-1},\\
\overline{J}_{\conj}^{L}(g)&\colon=\overline{J}_{\conj}(g),\quad\overline{\Psi}_{\conj}^{L}\!\left(g_{1},g_{2};g_{3}\right)\colon=g_{1}g_{3}g_{2}^{-1},\end{cases}



for (g1,g2,g3)\left(g_{1},g_{2};g_{3}\right) in 𝒢2×JcL𝒢\mathcal{G}^{2}\times_{J_{\conj}^{L}}\mathcal{G}, resp. 𝒢2×J¯cL𝒢\mathcal{G}^{2}\times_{\overline{J}_{\conj}^{L}}\mathcal{G}.
The proof that both triples (𝒢,JcL,ΨcL)\left(\mathcal{G},J_{\conj}^{L},\Psi_{\conj}^{L}\right) and (𝒢,J¯cL,Ψ¯cL)\left(\mathcal{G},\overline{J}_{\conj}^{L},\overline{\Psi}_{\conj}^{L}\right) define both left 𝒢2\mathcal{G}^{2}-actions is immediate.





5.6. Equivariant maps between groupoid-spaces




I want to define and discuss the concept of equivariant map between groupoid-spaces; here, by groupoid-space, I mean a set MM acted on (from the left or from the right) by a groupoid 𝒢\mathcal{G}.




For our purposes, let me consider the following general situation: given a left 𝒢\mathcal{G}-space (M,JM,ΨM)\left(M,J_{M},\Psi_{M}\right) and a left ℋ\mathcal{H}-space (N,JN,ΨN)\left(N,J_{N},\Psi_{N}\right) respectively, where 𝒢\mathcal{G}, ℋ\mathcal{H} are any two groupoids and MM, NN any two sets.



Definition 5.7.



A (twisted) equivariant map between the left 𝒢\mathcal{G}-space MM and the left ℋ\mathcal{H}-space NN is a triple (Θ,Φ,φ)\left(\Theta,\Phi,\varphi\right), where Θ\Theta is a map from the set MM to the set NN, and the pair (Φ,φ)\left(\Phi,\varphi\right) is a morphism from the groupoid 𝒢\mathcal{G} to the groupoid ℋ\mathcal{H} in the sense of Definition 5.1.




Moreover, one imposes the commutativity of the following diagrams:


i) 







M→ΘNJM↓↓JNX𝒢→φXℋ;\begin{CD}M@>{\Theta}>{}>N\\
@V{J_{M}}V{}V@V{}V{J_{N}}V\\
X_{\mathcal{G}}@>{\varphi}>{}>X_{\mathcal{H}}\end{CD}\quad;




ii) 







𝒢×JMM→Φ×Θℋ×JNNΨM↓↓ΨNM→ΘN\begin{CD}\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{M}}M@>{\Phi\times\Theta}>{}>\mathcal{H}\times_{J_{N}}N\\
@V{\Psi_{M}}V{}V@V{}V{\Psi_{N}}V\\
M@>{\Theta}>{}>N\end{CD}








Remark 5.8.



The first commutative diagram in Definition 5.7 implies that Φ×Θ\Phi\times\Theta maps 𝒢×JMM\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{M}}M to ℋ×JNN\mathcal{H}\times_{J_{N}}N, as the following explicit computation shows:





JN​(Θ​(m))\displaystyle J_{N}\left(\Theta(m)\right)
=φ​(JM​(m))=\displaystyle=\varphi(J_{M}(m))=




=φ​(s𝒢​(g))=\displaystyle=\varphi(s_{\mathcal{G}}(g))=




=sℋ​(Φ⁡(g)),∀(g,m)∈𝒢×JMM.\displaystyle=s_{\mathcal{H}}(\Phi(g)),\quad\forall(g,m)\in\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{M}}M.



Usually, the second diagram is encoded in the following identity:





Θ⁡(g​m)=Φ⁡(g)​Θ​(m),∀(g,m)∈𝒢×JMM,\Theta(gm)=\Phi(g)\Theta(m),\quad\forall(g,m)\in\mathcal{G}\times_{J_{M}}M,



which corresponds clearly to the usual definition of (twisted by Φ\Phi) equivariance of a map Θ\Theta from a left GG-space to a left HH-space, for GG, HH usual groups.






5.7. Some explicit computations for the groupoid of generalized gauge transformations




Finally, I want to compute an explicit expression for the generalized conjugation of the groupoid of generalized gauge transformations.




For a general element K12∈C∞​(P1⊙P2,G)G×GK_{12}\in C^{\infty}\!\left(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G\right)^{G\times G}, the generalized conjugation of it makes sense, whenever one considers the action of pairs (Ki​1,Kj​2)\left(K_{i1},K_{j2}\right), where Ki​k∈C∞​(Pi⊙Pk,G)G×GK_{ik}\in C^{\infty}\!\left(P_{i}\odot P_{k},G\right)^{G\times G}, for some principal GG-bundle PiP_{i} over MM, and k=1,2k=1,2.
The explicit formula takes the form





(Kj​2−1⋆K12⋆Ki​1)​(pi,pj)\displaystyle\left(K_{j2}^{-1}\star K_{12}\star K_{i1}\right)\!(p_{i},p_{j})
=Kj​2​(pj,p2)−1​K12​(p1,p2)​Ki​1​(pi,p1),\displaystyle=K_{j2}\!(p_{j},p_{2})^{-1}K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})K_{i1}\!(p_{i},p_{1}),



where p1∈P1p_{1}\in P_{1} and p2∈P2p_{2}\in P_{2} are such that π1​(p1)=π2​(p2)=πi​(pi)=πj​(pj)\pi_{1}(p_{1})=\pi_{2}(p_{2})=\pi_{i}(p_{i})=\pi_{j}(p_{j}).
The previous equation simplifies considerably, when considering the generalized conjugation of K12K_{12} by a special pair (g,h)(g,h), where g∈C∞​(P1,G)Gg\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{1},G)^{G} and h∈C∞​(P2,G)Gh\in C^{\infty}\!(P_{2},G)^{G}, i.e. when considering the action of a pair of gauge transformations on the source and on the target of K12K_{12}.
A direct computation shows immediately





(Kh−1⋆K12⋆Kg)​(p1,p2)\displaystyle\left(K_{h}^{-1}\star K_{12}\star K_{g}\right)\!(p_{1},p_{2})
=Kh​(q2,p2)−1​K12​(q1,q2)​Kg​(p1,q1)=\displaystyle=K_{h}\!(q_{2},p_{2})^{-1}K_{12}\!(q_{1},q_{2})K_{g}\!(p_{1},q_{1})=




=h​(p2)−1​ϕP2​(p2,q2)​K12​(q1,q2)​ϕP1​(p1,q1)−1​g​(p1)=\displaystyle=h(p_{2})^{-1}\phi_{P_{2}}\!(p_{2},q_{2})K_{12}\!(q_{1},q_{2})\phi_{P_{1}}\!(p_{1},q_{1})^{-1}g(p_{1})=




=h​(p2)−1​K12​(q1​ϕP1​(p1,q1)−1,q2​ϕP2​(p2,q2)−1)​g​(p1)=\displaystyle=h(p_{2})^{-1}K_{12}\!(q_{1}\phi_{P_{1}}\!(p_{1},q_{1})^{-1},q_{2}\phi_{P_{2}}\!(p_{2},q_{2})^{-1})g(p_{1})=




=by (4.3)​h​(p2)−1​K12​(p1,p2)​g​(p1),\displaystyle\overset{\text{by (\ref{eq-canbundle})}}{=}h(p_{2})^{-1}K_{12}\!(p_{1},p_{2})g(p_{1}),



whenever π1​(p1)=π1​(q1)\pi_{1}(p_{1})=\pi_{1}(q_{1}) and π2​(p2)=π2​(q2)\pi_{2}(p_{2})=\pi_{2}(q_{2}).






6. Applications to bundles on the space of loops or paths




In the present section, I interpret the holonomy as a gauge transformation of a bundle over the space of loops in the manifold MM, and similarly the parallel transport as a generalized gauge transformation on bundles over the space of loops or, more generally, paths in MM.




Consider first the space of free loops in MM, which is denoted by L​M\mathrm{L}M; by the word “free”, one means loops in MM without a specified base point.
There are canonical, smooth maps from the product manifold L​M×I\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I} to MM, the evaluation map and the evaluation map at 00, defined respectively via




(6.1)

ev⁡(γ,t):\displaystyle\ev\left(\gamma;t\right)\colon
=γ⁡(t);\displaystyle=\gamma(t);


(6.2)

ev0⁡(γ,t):\displaystyle\ev_{0}\left(\gamma;t\right)\colon
=γ⁡(0).\displaystyle=\gamma(0).



Using the maps (6.1) and (6.2), one can construct two principal GG-bundles on the cylinder L​M×I\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I} as follows: consider a fixed principal GG-bundle PP over MM, and take the pull-backs





ev∗⁡P\displaystyle\ev^{*}P
={(γ,t,p)∈L​M×I×P:π⁡(p)=γ⁡(t)};\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\gamma;t;p\right)\in\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I}\times P\colon\pi(p)=\gamma(t)\right\};



ev0∗​P\displaystyle\ev_{0}^{*}P
={(γ,t,p)∈L​M×I×P:π⁡(p)=γ⁡(0)}.\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\gamma;t;p\right)\in\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I}\times P\colon\pi(p)=\gamma(0)\right\}.



The canonical projections from both bundles onto L​M×I\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I} map any 33-tuple to the first two arguments; this allows us to write the fibred product of ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P and ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P as





ev0∗P⊙ev∗P={(γ;t;p,p~)∈LM×I×P×P:π(p)=γ(0),π(p~)=γ(t)}.\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P=\left\{\left(\gamma;t;p,\widetilde{p}\right)\in\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I}\times P\times P\colon\pi(p)=\gamma(0),\quad\pi\left(\widetilde{p}\right)=\gamma(t)\right\}.






Choosing a background connection AA on PP, one can define (see Section 3) the holonomy w.r.t. AA and the parallel transport w.r.t. AA.
The holonomy is an element of GG, depending on a loop γ\gamma and on a chosen base point p∈Pp\in P, satisfying π⁡(p)=γ⁡(0)=ev0⁡(γ,t)\pi(p)=\gamma(0)=\ev_{0}(\gamma,t).
Hence, it may be viewed as a map on the pulled-back bundle ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P with values in GG, which depends additionally on the choice of a connection AA on PP.
Moreover, Equation (3.2) of Lemma 3.4 implies that the holonomy is a GG-equivariant map from ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P to GG; thus, it may be viewed as a gauge transformation of ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P.
Similarly, the parallel transport w.r.t. a chosen connection AA depends on a curve γ\gamma in MM, some t∈It\in\mathrm{I}, and two points in PP, (p,q)(p,q), satisfying π⁡(p)=γ⁡(0)\pi(p)=\gamma(0) and π⁡(q)=γ⁡(t)\pi(q)=\gamma(t); hence, the parallel transport may be viewed as a function on the fibred product of ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P and ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P with values in GG.
Moreover, Equation (3.6) of Lemma 3.6 guarantees that the parallel transport is G×GG\times G-equivariant w.r.t. the generalized conjugation introduced at the beginning of Section 4.
Thus, parallel transport defines a generalized gauge transformation between the bundle ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P and ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P, depending on the choice of a connection AA.




From now on, the holonomy H(A)|∙∙\partr(A)\arrowvert_{\bullet}^{\bullet}, viewed as a gauge transformation of ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P, is denoted simply by H(A)|01\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}, whereas the parallel transport H⁡(A,∙,∙,∙,∙)\partr\!\left(A;\bullet;\bullet;\bullet,\bullet\right) as a generalized gauge transformation is denoted by H(A)|0∙\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}.
Theorem 4.10 of Section 4 implies that the parallel transport H(A)|0∙\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet} induces a bundle (iso)morphism from ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P to ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P, which is denoted by ΦA\Phi_{A} in order to make explicit its dependence on the chosen connection AA.




I now want to inspect now more carefully the dependence on the chosen connection AA of the isomorphism ΦA\Phi_{A}, in view of the computations done in Subsection 5.7 of Section 5.
First, write down the isomorphism ΦA\Phi_{A} explicitly:





ΦA​(γ,t,p)=(γ,t,q​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)),\Phi_{A}\!\left(\gamma;t;p\right)=\left(\gamma;t;q\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)\right),



where π⁡(p)=γ⁡(0)\pi(p)=\gamma(0), and q∈Pq\in P is any point satisfying π⁡(q)=γ⁡(t)\pi\left(q\right)=\gamma(t).




It is well-known that the gauge group 𝒢\mathcal{G} operates on the space of connections 𝒜\mathcal{A} by pull-back.
Considering now a gauge transformation σ\sigma and taking the pull-back of the connection AA w.r.t. σ\sigma, the morphism ΦA\Phi_{A} changes as follows:




(6.3)

Φσ∗​A​(γ,t,p)\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma^{*}A}\left(\gamma;t;p\right)
=(γ,t,p~​H⁡(σ∗​A,γ,t,p,p~))=\displaystyle=(\gamma;t;\widetilde{p}\ \partr\!\left(\sigma^{*}A;\gamma;t;p,\widetilde{p}\right))=




=(γ,t,p~​gσ​(p~)−1​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,p~)​gσ​(p))=\displaystyle=(\gamma;t;\widetilde{p}\ g_{\sigma}\!\left(\widetilde{p}\right)^{-1}\!\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,\widetilde{p}\right)\!g_{\sigma}(p))=




=(γ,t,σ−1​(p~)​H⁡(A,γ,t,p​gσ​(p),p~))=\displaystyle=\left(\gamma;t;\sigma^{-1}\!\left(\widetilde{p}\right)\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p\ g_{\sigma}(p),\widetilde{p}\right)\right)=




=(γ,t,σ−1​(p~​H⁡(A,γ,t,σ⁡(p),p~)))=\displaystyle=\left(\gamma;t;\sigma^{-1}\!\left(\widetilde{p}\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;\sigma(p),\widetilde{p}\right)\right)\right)=




=σ−1​(ΦA​(γ,t,σ⁡(p))).\displaystyle=\sigma^{-1}\left(\Phi_{A}\left(\gamma;t;\sigma(p)\right)\right).



The main argument used in the proof of the above equation is Identity (3.7) of Lemma 3.6; this identity admits an interpretation in terms of equivariant maps between groupoid-spaces, as introduced in Subsection 5.6 of Section 5.




I need some technical arguments to make the statement more precise.
Assume ff to be a smooth map from some manifold LL to MM; then



Lemma 6.1.



Every gauge transformation of PP induces a gauge transformation of the pulled-back bundle f∗​Pf^{*}P.




Proof.



Consider a general gauge transformation σ∈𝒢P\sigma\in\mathcal{G}_{P}.
By definition,





f∗​P={(l,p)∈L×P:f⁡(l)=π⁡(p)};f^{*}P=\left\{\left(l,p\right)\in L\times P\colon f(l)=\pi(p)\right\};



consider then a general point (l,p)(l,p) in f∗​Pf^{*}P.
Define a morphism σf\sigma_{f} from f∗​Pf^{*}P to itself as follows:





σf​(l,p):=(l,σ⁡(p)).\sigma_{f}\!\left(l,p\right)\colon=\left(l,\sigma(p)\right).



One has then to show that σf\sigma_{f} is well-defined, that it respects the fibres of f∗​Pf^{*}P and that it is GG-equivariant; bijectivity, and hence invertibility, follows from Lemma 4.11 of Section 4.




Since π∘σ=π\pi\circ\sigma=\pi, it follows that the image of a point in f∗​Pf^{*}P still belongs to f∗​Pf^{*}P.
By its very definition, σf\sigma_{f} is fibre-preserving.
The GG-equivariance of σf\sigma_{f} is immediate.




Notice that the map taking σ\sigma to σf\sigma_{f} is clearly a homomorphism of groups from 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} to 𝒢f∗​P\mathcal{G}_{f^{*}P}.
∎





The gauge group of PP, 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}, can be viewed as a groupoid, where the set of objects X𝒢PX_{\mathcal{G}_{P}} is simply a point, namely, one can consider this point as PP, an object of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}; source map and target map are simply





s𝒢P​(σ)=t𝒢P​(σ)=P;s_{\mathcal{G}_{P}}(\sigma)=t_{\mathcal{G}_{P}}(\sigma)=P;



identity is simply





ιP​(P)=idP.\iota_{P}(P)=\id_{P}.



Finally, the inversion is clearly





j𝒢P​(σ)=σ−1.j_{\mathcal{G}_{P}}(\sigma)=\sigma^{-1}.



Moreover, as already seen at the end of Section 2, the gauge group 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} operates on the space of connections on PP, 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P}; it is easy to see that 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P} is a right 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}-space in the sense explained in Subsection 5.4 of Section 5.




I want to construct a morphism of groupoids between 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} and the product groupoid of the groupoid of generalized gauge transformations of the space of loops (or paths) in MM with itself.
The groupoid of generalized gauge transformations, for a manifold MM and group GG, is denoted by 𝒢G,M\mathcal{G}_{G,M} (hence, it follows 𝒢G,M,P1,P2=C∞!​(P1⊙P2,G)G×G\mathcal{G}_{G,M,P_{1},P_{2}}=C^{\infty}!\left(P_{1}\odot P_{2},G\right)^{G\times G}, P1P_{1} and P2P_{2} being two principal GG-bundles over MM).



Lemma 6.2.



The pair (ΦP,φP)\left(\Phi_{P},\varphi_{P}\right), where





{φP​(P):=(ev0∗​P,ev∗⁡P)ΦP​(σ):=(σev0,σev),\begin{cases}\varphi_{P}(P)&\colon=\left(\ev_{0}^{*}P,\ev^{*}P\right)\\
\Phi_{P}(\sigma)&\colon=\left(\sigma_{\ev_{0}},\sigma_{\ev}\right)\end{cases},



is a morphism in the sense of Definition 5.1 from the groupoid 𝒢G,L​M2\mathcal{G}_{G,\mathrm{L}M}^{2}, for any manifold MM and any Lie group GG..




Proof.



According to Definition 5.1, one has to show first the commutativity of the three diagrams of (5.1); this is immediate, because of the definition of source map, target map and identity for the product groupoid of the groupoid of generalized gauge transformations of L​M\mathrm{L}M (see Subsection 5.1 for more details).




Property (5.2) follows immediately from Lemma 6.1 and from the definition of product of a product groupoid.
∎





Using now all arguments sketched in Section 4 and 5, one can restate Identity (3.7) of Lemma 3.6 as follows:



Proposition 6.3.



The triple (H(∙)|0∙,ΦP,φP)\left(\partr(\bullet)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet},\Phi_{P},\varphi_{P}\right) defines an equivariant map from the space of connections 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P}, viewed as a right 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}-space, to the groupoid 𝒢G,L​M\mathcal{G}_{G,\mathrm{L}M}, resp. 𝒢G,P​M\mathcal{G}_{G,\mathrm{P}\!M} of generalized gauge transformations of the space of loops (resp. paths) in MM, viewed as a right 𝒢G,L​M2\mathcal{G}_{G,\mathrm{L}M}^{2}-, resp. 𝒢G,P​M2\mathcal{G}_{G,\mathrm{P}\!M}^{2}-,space via the generalized conjugation introduced in Subsection 5.5.
(By H(∙)|0∙\partr(\bullet)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}, I mean the map associating to a connection AA the corresponding parallel transport as a generalized gauge transformation.)




Proof.



First of all, the momentum J𝒜PJ_{\mathcal{A}_{P}} is simply given by





J𝒜P​(A):=P,∀A∈𝒜P.J_{\mathcal{A}_{P}}\!(A)\colon=P,\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{A}_{P}.



With this definition in mind, and recalling the arguments of Subsection 5.5, one gets immediately the commutativity of the first diagram in Definition 5.7.




It remains to show the commutativity of the second diagram.
This is an immediate consequence of the explicit computation done in Subsection 5.7 together with Identity (3.7) of Lemma 3.6.
∎





An immediate consequence of Proposition 6.3, together with Identity (3.3) of Lemma 3.4, is given by



Corollary 6.4.



The holonomy map H(∙)|01\partr(\bullet)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}, assigning to any connection AA in 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P} the gauge transformation of the bundle ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P defined by the holonomy w.r.t. AA, is an equivariant map from the space of connections on PP to the gauge group of ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P w.r.t. the conjugation.




6.0.1. A consequence of Corollary 6.4: the Wilson loop map




In this subsubsection, I discuss an important consequence of Corollary 6.4, namely the construction of the so-called Wilson loop map; this map plays an important rôle in the framework of Topological Quantum Field Theories, in particular Chern–Simons Theory.




To begin with, notice that the holonomy map H(A)|01\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}, for any given connection AA, defines immediately a section of the associated bundle Ad⁡(ev0∗​P)\Ad\!(\ev_{0}^{*}P) on L​M×I\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I} w.r.t. conjugation.
For our purposes, it is better to view the holonomy map H(A)|01\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1} as a map from the pulled-back bundle ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P over L​M\mathrm{L}M, where ev⁡(0)\ev(0) is defined via





ev⁡(0)​(γ):=γ​(0)\ev(0)(\gamma)\colon=\gamma(0)



(see also the beginning of Subsection 6.1).
Similarly, this holonomy map, denoted again by H(A)|01\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}, descends to a section of the associated bundle Ad⁡(ev⁡(0)∗​P)\Ad\!(\ev(0)^{*}P); this bundle is associated to ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P via the conjugation of GG.
More generally, dropping out the explicit dependence on connections, the holonomy map H(∙)|01\partr(\bullet)\arrowvert_{0}^{1} may be viewed as a map from the cartesian product of 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P}, the space of connections on PP, with the principal bundle ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P on the space of loops L​M\mathrm{L}M, to the group GG; this map satisfies, by Corollary 6.4, two kinds of equivariance, namely w.r.t. the GG-action on ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P and w.r.t. the 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}-action on 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P}.
Consider now a representation (V,ρ)(V,\rho) of GG, namely a (real or complex) vector space VV endowed with a group homomorphism ρ\rho from GG to the automorphism group of VV.
Composing the holonomy map with ρ\rho, one gets a map from the product 𝒜P×ev⁡(0)∗​P\mathcal{A}_{P}\times\ev(0)^{*}P to the linear group G​L​(V)GL(V); the aforementioned equivariance properties hold again, since ρ\rho is a group homomorphism.
Finally, one can take the trace in the endomorphism ring of VV of the composite map ρ∘H(∙)|01\rho\circ\partr(\bullet)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}; the result of these operations is denoted by





Wρ​(A,γ,p):=TrV⁡(ρ⁡(H⁡(A,γ,p))),W_{\rho}(A;\gamma;p)\colon=\tr_{V}\!\left(\rho(\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right))\right),



which is called Wilson loop map WρW_{\rho} w.r.t. the representation ρ\rho.
It is clearly a (real or complex, depending on the representation VV) function on 𝒜P×ev⁡(0)∗​P\mathcal{A}_{P}\times\ev(0)^{*}P.




The cyclicity of the trace TrV\tr_{V}, together with Corollary 6.4, implies the two following invariance properties of the Wilson loop map:




(6.4)

Wρ​(A,γ,p​g)\displaystyle W_{\rho}(A;\gamma;pg)
=Wρ​(A,γ,p),∀g∈G,\displaystyle=W_{\rho}(A;\gamma;p),\quad\forall g\in G,


(6.5)

Wρ​(Aσ,γ,p)\displaystyle W_{\rho}(A^{\sigma};\gamma;p)
=Wρ​(A,γ,p),∀σ∈𝒢.\displaystyle=W_{\rho}(A;\gamma;p),\quad\forall\sigma\in\mathcal{G}.



Let me spend a few words on the space of connections on PP, 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P}: as already seen at the end of Section 2, the gauge group 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} operates on the right on 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P}.
It would naturally be interesting to analyse the properties of the gauge group action of 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} on 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P} and to find suitable conditions for this action to be free; in fact, there could be in principle gauge transformations fixing connections, e.g. if the structure group GG possesses a nontrivial centre, there are constant gauge transformations fixing any given connection.
Therefore, in order to consider the moduli space of connections on PP, i.e. the quotient space 𝒜P/𝒢P\mathcal{A}_{P}/\mathcal{G}_{P}, one has to consider only the space 𝒜P∗\mathcal{A}_{P}^{*} of irreducible connections, which is the subset of 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P} on which the group 𝒢P∗\mathcal{G}_{P}^{*} (the quotient group of 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} by the centre of the structure group GG, which may be viewed as a normal subgroup of 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}).
Hence, it makes sense to consider the quotient space 𝒜P∗/𝒢P∗\mathcal{A}_{P}^{*}/\mathcal{G}_{P}^{*}, which one can view as the base space of a principal 𝒢P∗\mathcal{G}_{P}^{*}-bundle, whose total space is 𝒜P∗\mathcal{A}_{P}^{*}; it is not my plan to discuss the local structure of this quotient space, thus I do not enter into the details of the construction of suitable topologies on it.




Equations (6.4) and (6.5) imply immediately the following



Proposition 6.5.



For any linear representation (V,ρ)(V,\rho) of GG, the structure group of PP, the Wilson loop map





𝒜P∗×ev⁡(0)∗​P∋(A,γ,p)↦Wρ​(A,γ,p)\mathcal{A}_{P}^{*}\times\ev(0)^{*}P\ni(A;\gamma;p)\mapsto W_{\rho}(A;\gamma;p)



descends to a function WρW_{\rho} on the product of the moduli space of connections 𝒜P∗/𝒢P∗\mathcal{A}_{P}^{*}/\mathcal{G}_{P}^{*} with the space of loops L​M\mathrm{L}M.






6.1. Restriction to the boundary of I\mathrm{I}




I want to discuss how the restriction to the boundary of the “cylinder” L​M×I\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I} affects the isomorphism between ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P and ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P given by the parallel transport H(A)|0∙\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}; in fact, I will prove later that both restrictions give well-known gauge transformations of a bundle over the space of loops L​M\mathrm{L}M.




First of all, one can define the evaluation map at some point t∈It\in\mathrm{I}, which is denoted by ev⁡(t)\ev(t), as a map from the space of paths P​M\mathrm{P}\!M to MM by





ev⁡(t)​(γ):=γ​(t).\ev(t)(\gamma)\colon=\gamma(t).



It is clear that, taking restriction to L​M\mathrm{L}M, the evaluation maps at 00 and at 11 do coincide.
Restricting the bundles ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P and ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P to the subset L​M×{0}\mathrm{L}M\times\{0\} (which is clearly diffeomorphic to L​M\mathrm{L}M), one gets immediately





ev0∗⁡P|L​M×{0}\displaystyle\ev_{0}^{*}P|_{\mathrm{L}M\times\{0\}}
≅{(γ,t,p)∈ev0∗​P:t=0}≅ev⁡(0)∗​P;\displaystyle\cong\left\{(\gamma;t;p)\in\ev_{0}^{*}P\colon t=0\right\}\cong\ev(0)^{*}P;



ev∗⁡P|L​M×{0}\displaystyle\ev^{*}P|_{\mathrm{L}M\times\{0\}}
≅{(γ,t,p)∈ev∗⁡P:t=0}≅\displaystyle\cong\left\{(\gamma;t;p)\in\ev^{*}P\colon t=0\right\}\cong




≅{(γ,0,p)∈ev∗⁡P:γ⁡(0)=π⁡(p)}≅\displaystyle\cong\left\{(\gamma;0;p)\in\ev^{*}P\colon\gamma(0)=\pi(p)\right\}\cong




≅ev⁡(0)∗​P.\displaystyle\cong\ev(0)^{*}P.






The isomorphism ΦA\Phi_{A} respects fibres, whence it follows that it maps the restriction to L​M×{0}\mathrm{L}M\times\{0\} of ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P to the restriction to the same set of ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P, hence mapping ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P to itself.
By the very definition of ΦA\Phi_{A}, it follows:





ΦA|L​M×{0}​(γ,0,p)=(γ,0,q​H⁡(A,γ,0,p,q)),\Phi_{A}|_{\mathrm{L}M\times\{0\}}\left(\gamma;0;p\right)=(\gamma;0;q\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;0;p,q\right)),



where in this case π⁡(q)=γ⁡(0)=π⁡(p)\pi\left(q\right)=\gamma(0)=\pi(p).
One can choose q=pq=p, since the definition of ΦA\Phi_{A} above does not depend on the choice of qq as long as it remains in the same fibre, and thus it follows





γ~A,p​(0)=p,\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(0)=p,



whence H⁡(A,γ,0,p,p)=e\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;0;p,p\right)=e.
Hence, the restriction to L​M×{0}\mathrm{L}M\times\{0\} equals the identity gauge transformation of ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P.
On the other hand, taking restriction to the bundles ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P and ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P to L​M×{1}\mathrm{L}M\times\{1\} (which is again diffeomorphic to L​M\mathrm{L}M); in this case, one gets





ev0∗⁡P|L​M×{1}\displaystyle\ev_{0}^{*}P|_{\mathrm{L}M\times\{1\}}
={(γ,1,p)∈ev0∗​P:π⁡(p)=γ⁡(0)}≅ev⁡(0)∗​P;\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\gamma;1;p\right)\in\ev_{0}^{*}P\colon\pi(p)=\gamma(0)\right\}\cong\ev(0)^{*}P;



ev∗⁡P|L​M×{1}\displaystyle\ev^{*}P|_{\mathrm{L}M\times\{1\}}
={(γ,1,p)∈ev0∗​P:π⁡(p)=γ⁡(1)=γ⁡(0)}≅ev⁡(0)∗​P.\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\gamma;1;p\right)\in\ev_{0}^{*}P\colon\pi(p)=\gamma(1)=\gamma(0)\right\}\cong\ev(0)^{*}P.



So, restricting the two bundles ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P and ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P to the subset L​M×{1}\mathrm{L}M\times\{1\}, one gets the same bundle as before, namely ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P.
But the restriction of ΦA\Phi_{A} is not the identity gauge transformation, as the following computation shows:





ΦA|L​M×{1}​(γ,1,p)=(γ,1,q​H⁡(A,γ,1,p,q)),\Phi_{A}|_{\mathrm{L}M\times\{1\}}\left(\gamma;1;p\right)=\left(\gamma;1;q\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,q\right)\right),



where q∈Pq\in P obeys π⁡(q)=γ⁡(1)=γ⁡(0)=π⁡(p)\pi\left(q\right)=\gamma(1)=\gamma(0)=\pi(p).
Hence, one can again choose q=pq=p, and by definition





ΦA|L​M×{1}​(γ,1,p)=(γ,1,p​H⁡(A,γ,1,p,p)).\Phi_{A}|_{\mathrm{L}M\times\{1\}}\left(\gamma;1;p\right)=\left(\gamma;1;p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,p\right)\right).



The following identity holds:





p​H⁡(A,γ,1,p,p)=γ~A,p​(1)=p​H⁡(A,γ,p).p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,p\right)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(1)=p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right).



Since the action of GG on each fibre is free, it follows:





H⁡(A,γ,p)=H⁡(A,γ,1,p,p),∀γ∈L​M,p∈P.\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,p\right),\quad\forall\gamma\in\mathrm{L}M,p\in P.



Hence, the restriction of ΦA\Phi_{A} to L​M×{1}\mathrm{L}M\times\{1\} equals the gauge transformation of ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P defined by the holonomy H⁡(A,∙,∙)\partr\!\left(A;\bullet;\bullet\right) (which is denoted by H(A)|01\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}).






7. Some consequences of flatness related to holonomy and parallel transport




Motivated by Proposition 6.3 of Section 6, which allows us to parameterize particular generalized gauge transformations of the space of loops or paths in MM by connections on a given background bundle PP over MM in an equivariant way, I am interested now in the restriction of the equivariant map of Proposition 6.3 to the space of flat connections, on which the gauge group of PP also operates.
Before entering into the details, I need some technical facts about connections on the fibred product (see Section 4 for more details) of any two principal bundles.




7.1. Connections on the fibred product of two principal bundles




Assume PP, P~\widetilde{P} to be objects (a priori distinct) of the category 𝖡𝗎𝗇G,M\mathsf{Bun}_{G,M}, endowed with respective connections AA and A~\widetilde{A}.
A general tangent vector to the fibred product P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} at a point (p,p~)\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right) can be written as a pair (Xp,Xp~)\left(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}}\right), where XpX_{p}, resp. Xp~X_{\widetilde{p}}, is a tangent vector to PP at pp, resp. to P~\widetilde{P} at p~\widetilde{p}.
By the very definition of the projection π¯\overline{\pi} from P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} onto MM, the tangent vector (Xp,Xp~)\left(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}}\right) is vertical if and only if





T(p,p~)⁡π¯​(Xp,Xp~)=Tp⁡π⁡(Xp)=Tp~⁡π~​(Xp~)=0,\tange\!_{\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)}{\overline{\pi}}\left(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}}\right)=\tange\!_{p}{\pi}\left(X_{p}\right)=\tange\!_{\widetilde{p}}{\widetilde{\pi}}\left(X_{\widetilde{p}}\right)=0,



i.e.  if and only if both components of (Xp,Xp~)\left(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}}\right) are vertical.




As seen in Section 2, the connection AA, resp. A~\widetilde{A}, specifies a smooth splitting of the tangent bundle of PP, resp. P~\widetilde{P}, into vertical and AA-horizontal bundle, resp. A~\widetilde{A}-horizontal bundle.
Hence, one can write any tangent vector XpX_{p}, resp. Xp~X_{\widetilde{p}}, to PP at pp, resp. to P~\widetilde{P} at p~\widetilde{p}, as





Xp=Xpv+Xph,resp.Xp~=Xp~v+Xp~h.X_{p}=X_{p}^{v}+X_{p}^{h},\quad\text{resp.}\quad X_{\widetilde{p}}=X_{\widetilde{p}}^{v}+X_{\widetilde{p}}^{h}.



Thus, any tangent vector (Xp,Xp~)\left(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}}\right) to P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} at (p,p~)\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right) has a unique splitting




(7.1)

(Xp,Xp~)=(Xpv,Xp~v)+(Xph,Xp~h).\left(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}}\right)=\left(X_{p}^{v},X_{\widetilde{p}}^{v}\right)+\left(X_{p}^{h},X_{\widetilde{p}}^{h}\right).



It is clear from the definition of the action of G×GG\times G on P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} that the sets of all vectors of the form (Xph,Xp~h)\left(X_{p}^{h},X_{\widetilde{p}}^{h}\right) in (7.1), for any pair (p,p~)∈P⊙P~(p,\widetilde{p})\in P\odot\widetilde{P}, specify a G×GG\times G-invariant family of subspaces.
Therefore, by the arguments recalled in Section 2, to the splitting (7.1) belongs obviously the connection 11-form





(A⊙A~)(p,p~)​(Xp,Xp~):=(Ap​(Xp),A~p~​(Xp~)),\left(A\odot\widetilde{A}\right)_{\!\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)}\!\left(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}}\right)\colon=\left(A_{p}\!\left(X_{p}\right),\widetilde{A}_{\widetilde{p}}\!\left(X_{\widetilde{p}}\right)\right),



which is called the fibred product connection of the connections AA and A~\widetilde{A}; the fibred product connection A⊙A~A\odot\widetilde{A} is a 𝔤⊕𝔤\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}-valued form on P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P}.
Notice that the fibred product connection A⊙A~A\odot\widetilde{A} can be written alternatively as the restriction of the connection on the product P×P~P\times\widetilde{P} given by





(prP∗​A,prP~∗​A~),\left(\pr_{P}^{*}\!A,\pr_{\widetilde{P}}^{*}\!\widetilde{A}\right),



where prP\pr_{P}, resp. prP~\pr_{\widetilde{P}}, denotes the projection of P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} onto PP, resp. P~\widetilde{P}, to P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P}.




Recall that a connection AA on PP is flat, if it satisfies the equation




(7.2)

d​A+12​[A,A]=0.\mathrm{d}A+\frac{1}{2}{\left[{\,{A}\,,\,{A}\,}\right]}=0.



An equivalent characterisation of flatness of a connection AA, viewed as a way of splitting the tangent bundle of PP into vertical and AA-horizontal bundle, is that the AA-horizontal bundle specifies an integrable distribution over PP, i.e. the Lie bracket of any two AA-horizontal vectors is again horizontal.
From the latter characterisation of the fibred product connection A⊙A~A\odot\widetilde{A}, it follows that the fibred product connection is flat if and only if both AA and A~\widetilde{A} are flat; it is a consequence of Equation (7.2).




Given now a bundle morphism σ\sigma from PP to P~\widetilde{P} in the sense of Definition 4.1, one can construct a map σ⊙σ−1\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1} from P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} to P~⊙P\widetilde{P}\odot P as follows:





(σ⊙σ−1)​(p,p~):=(σ⁡(p),σ−1​(p~)),∀(p,p~)∈P⊙P~.\left(\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1}\right)\left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\colon=\left(\sigma(p),\sigma^{-1}\left(\widetilde{p}\right)\right),\quad\forall\ \left(p,\widetilde{p}\right)\in P\odot\widetilde{P}.





Lemma 7.1.



The fibred product map σ⊙σ−1\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1}, for any bundle morphism σ\sigma from PP to P~\widetilde{P}, is a bundle morphism from the fibred product P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} to P~⊙P\widetilde{P}\odot P (notice that both are G×GG\times G-bundles).




Proof.



First of all, since π~∘σ=π\widetilde{\pi}\circ\sigma=\pi, it follows immediately that σ⊙σ−1\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1} maps the fibred product P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} to P~⊙P\widetilde{P}\odot P.




It is clear that σ⊙σ−1\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1} is fibre-preserving; namely, the canonical projection from P~⊙P\widetilde{P}\odot P to MM is e.g. the projection π~\widetilde{\pi} from the first argument of any pair to MM, whence it follows





(π~∘σ)​(p)=π​(p),\left(\widetilde{\pi}\circ\sigma\right)(p)=\pi(p),



which equals the canonical projection from P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P}.




Finally, a direct computation following from GG-equivariance of σ\sigma, gives





(σ⊙σ−1)​((p,p~)​(g,h))\displaystyle\left(\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1}\right)\!\left((p,\widetilde{p})(g,h)\right)
=(σ⁡(p​g),σ−1​(p~​h))=\displaystyle=\left(\sigma(pg),\sigma^{-1}(\widetilde{p}h)\right)=




=(σ⁡(p)​g,σ−1​(p~)​h)=\displaystyle=\left(\sigma(p)g,\sigma^{-1}(\widetilde{p})h\right)=




=(σ⁡(p),σ−1​(p~))​(g,h)=\displaystyle=\left(\sigma(p),\sigma^{-1}(\widetilde{p})\right)(g,h)=




=((σ⊙σ−1)​(p,p~))​(g,h),\displaystyle=\left(\left(\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1}\right)\!(p,\widetilde{p})\right)(g,h),



proving G×GG\times G-equivariance.
∎





Recall that, by Lemma 4.11, any bundle morphism σ\sigma as above is invertible.
Since σ\sigma is an isomorphism, it is clear that σ⊙σ−1\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1} is also an isomorphism.
Moreover, the GG-equivariance of σ\sigma ensures that σ⊙σ−1\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1} is G×GG\times G-equivariant.




There is a fibred product connection A~⊙A\widetilde{A}\odot A on P~⊙P\widetilde{P}\odot P.
On the other hand, by pulling back the fibred product connection A~⊙A\widetilde{A}\odot A w.r.t. σ⊙σ−1\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1}, one gets a connection 11-form on P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P}, by the G×GG\times G-equivariance of σ⊙σ−1\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1}.



Lemma 7.2.



The pull-back of a fibred product connection A~⊙A\widetilde{A}\odot A on P~⊙P\widetilde{P}\odot P w.r.t. the fibred product map σ⊙σ−1\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1} equals a fibred product connection B⊙B~B\odot\widetilde{B} on P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P}, if and only if





{σ∗​A~=Bσ∗​B~=A.\begin{cases}\sigma^{*}\widetilde{A}&=B\\
\sigma^{*}\widetilde{B}&=A.\end{cases}






Proof.



An immediate computation gives the following identity




(7.3)

T(p,p~)⁡σ⊙σ−1​(Xp,Xp~)=(Tp⁡σ⁡(Xp),Tp~⁡σ−1​(Xp~)),\tange\!_{(p,\widetilde{p})}{\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1}}\!(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}})=\left(\tange\!_{p}{\sigma}(X_{p}),\tange\!_{\widetilde{p}}{\sigma^{-1}}(X_{\widetilde{p}})\right),



for any tangent vector (Xp,Xp~)(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}}) to P⊙P~P\odot\widetilde{P} at (p,p~)(p,\widetilde{p}).




It follows immediately from the definition of fibred product connection that





((σ⊙σ−1)∗​(A~⊙A))(p,p~)​(Xp,Xp~)\displaystyle\left(\left(\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1}\right)^{*}\!\left(\widetilde{A}\odot A\right)\right)_{(p,\widetilde{p})}\!\left(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}}\right)
=(B⊙B~)(p,p~)​(Xp,Xp~)⟺\displaystyle=\left(B\odot\widetilde{B}\right)_{(p,\widetilde{p})}\!\left(X_{p},X_{\widetilde{p}}\right)\Longleftrightarrow



(A~σ⁡(p)​(Tp⁡σ⁡(Xp)),Aσ−1​(p~)​(Tp~⁡σ−1​(Xp~)))\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{A}_{\sigma(p)}\!\left(\tange\!_{p}{\sigma}(X_{p})\right),A_{\sigma^{-1}(\widetilde{p})}\!\left(\tange\!_{\widetilde{p}}{\sigma^{-1}}(X_{\widetilde{p}})\right)\right)
=(Bp​(Xp),B~p~​(Xp~)CLOSE.\displaystyle=\left(B_{p}(X_{p}),\widetilde{B}_{\widetilde{p}}(X_{\widetilde{p}}\right).



The surjectivity of π\pi and π~\widetilde{\pi} yields then the claim.
∎





Hence, by Lemma 7.2, if the pull-back of A~⊙A\widetilde{A}\odot A w.r.t. σ⊙σ−1\sigma\odot\sigma^{-1} equals A⊙A~A\odot\widetilde{A}, the connection A~\widetilde{A} is the pull-back of AA w.r.t. σ\sigma.





7.2. Composition properties and inversion of the parallel transport




In this subsection, I state and prove two technical lemmata, which will be used later, when proving the two theorems on the consequences of flatness of the reference connection AA on both holonomy and parallel transport, viewed respectively as a gauge transformation of the pulled-back bundle ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P on L​M×I\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I} (or also ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P on L​M\mathrm{L}M) and as a generalized gauge transformation on L​M\mathrm{L}M or, more generally P​M\mathrm{P}\!M.




In fact, I will prove later how the parallel transport behaves w.r.t. the composition and inversion of paths.



Lemma 7.3.



Let γ1\gamma_{1} and γ2\gamma_{2} be two composable paths in MM, in the sense that γ1​(1)=γ2​(0)\gamma_{1}(1)=\gamma_{2}(0).
Additionally, let us consider three points p1p_{1}, p2p_{2} and p3p_{3} in PP, such that





γ1​(0)=π⁡(p1),γ1​(1)=γ2​(0)=π⁡(p2),γ2​(1)=π⁡(p3).\gamma_{1}(0)=\pi(p_{1}),\quad\gamma_{1}(1)=\gamma_{2}(0)=\pi(p_{2}),\quad\gamma_{2}(1)=\pi(p_{3}).



Define the composite curve of γ1\gamma_{1} and γ2\gamma_{2}, denoted byγ2∘γ1\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1}, as





(γ2∘γ1)​(t):={γ1​(2​t),t∈[0;12]γ2​(2​t−1),t∈[12;1].\left(\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1}\right)\!\left(t\right)\colon=\begin{cases}\gamma_{1}(2t),&t\in\left[0;\frac{1}{2}\right]\\
\gamma_{2}\left(2t-1\right),&t\in\left[\frac{1}{2};1\right]\end{cases}.



Clearly, the composite curve γ2∘γ1\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1} is piecewise smooth.




Then, the following identity holds




(7.4)

H⁡(A,γ2∘γ1,1,p1,p3)=H⁡(A,γ2,1,p2,p3)​H⁡(A,γ1,1,p1,p2).\partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1};1;p_{1},p_{3}\right)=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{2};1;p_{2},p_{3}\right)\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{1};1;p_{1},p_{2}\right).






Proof.



By Definition 3.5, one gets





γ2∘γ1~A,p1​(1)=p3​H⁡(A,γ2∘γ1,1,p1,p3),\widetilde{\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}(1)=p_{3}\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1};1;p_{1},p_{3}\right),



where γ2∘γ1~A,p1\widetilde{\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}} is the unique horizontal lift of γ2∘γ1\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1} based at p1p_{1}.




On the other hand, consider the composite curve γ2~A,γ1~A,p1​(1)∘γ1~A,p1\widetilde{\gamma_{2}}_{A,\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}(1)}\circ\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}} of horizontal curves:





(γ2~A,γ1~A,p1​(1)∘γ1~A,p1)​(t):={γ1~A,p1​(2​t),t∈[0,12]γ2~A,γ1~A,p1​(1)​(2​t−1),t∈[12;1].\left(\widetilde{\gamma_{2}}_{A,\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}(1)}\circ\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}\right)\left(t\right)\colon=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}(2t),&t\in\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right]\\
\widetilde{\gamma_{2}}_{A,\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}(1)}(2t-1),&t\in\left[\frac{1}{2};1\right].\end{cases}



By its very definition, it is clear that γ2~A,γ1~A,p1​(1)∘γ1~A,p1\widetilde{\gamma_{2}}_{A,\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}(1)}\circ\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}} lies over γ2∘γ1\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1} and that it is based at p1p_{1}.
Since it is the composition of two horizontal curves, it is also horizontal, whence it follows by Theorem 3.2 of Section 3





γ2~A,γ1~A,p1​(1)∘γ1~A,p1=γ2∘γ1~A,p1.\widetilde{\gamma_{2}}_{A,\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}(1)}\circ\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}=\widetilde{\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}.



Therefore, one gets





(γ2~A,γ1~A,p1​(1)∘γ1~A,p1)​(1)\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{\gamma_{2}}_{A,\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}(1)}\circ\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}\right)\!(1)
=γ2~A,γ1~A,p1​(1)​(1)=\displaystyle=\widetilde{\gamma_{2}}_{A,\widetilde{\gamma_{1}}_{A,p_{1}}(1)}(1)=




=γ2~A,p2​(1)​H⁡(A,γ1,1,p1,p2)=\displaystyle=\widetilde{\gamma_{2}}_{A,p_{2}}(1)\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{1};1;p_{1},p_{2}\right)=




=p3​H⁡(A,γ2,1,p2,p3)​H​(A,γ1,1,p,p1),\displaystyle=p_{3}\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{2};1;p_{2},p_{3}\right)\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{1};1;p,p_{1}\right),



whence the claim follows.
∎




Lemma 7.4.



Let γ\gamma be a path in MM; additionally, let pp, qq be two point in PP, such that





γ⁡(0)=π⁡(p),γ⁡(1)=π⁡(q).\gamma(0)=\pi(p),\quad\gamma(1)=\pi(q).



Define the inverse curve γ−1\gamma^{-1} of γ\gamma by





γ−1​(t):=γ⁡(1−t).\gamma^{-1}\!\left(t\right)\colon=\gamma\left(1-t\right).






Then, the following identity holds:




(7.5)

H⁡(A,γ−1,1,q,p)=H⁡(A,γ,1,p,q)−1.\partr\!\left(A;\gamma^{-1};1;q,p\right)=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,q\right)^{-1}.






Proof.



Consider the following curve:





γ~A,p​H⁡(A,γ,1,p,q)−1−1​(t):=γ~A,p​(1−t)​H⁡(A,γ,1,p,q)−1.\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,q\right)^{-1}}^{-1}(t)\colon=\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(1-t)\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,q\right)^{-1}.



It is immediate to see that this curve lies over γ−1\gamma^{-1}.
It is clearly based at qq, since





γ~A,p​(1)​H⁡(A,γ,1,p,q)−1\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p}(1)\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,q\right)^{-1}
=q​H⁡(A,γ,1,p,q)​H​(A,γ,1,p,q)−1=\displaystyle=q\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,q\right)\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,q\right)^{-1}=




=p~.\displaystyle=\widetilde{p}.



Since the connection AA defines a GG-invariant distribution, γ~A,p​H⁡(A,γ,1,p,q)−1−1\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,q\right)^{-1}}^{-1} is AA-horizontal, and by the uniqueness part of Theorem 3.2 of Section 3, one gets





γ~A,p​H⁡(A,γ,1,p,q)−1−1=γ−1~A,q,\widetilde{\gamma}_{A,p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;1;p,q\right)^{-1}}^{-1}=\widetilde{\gamma^{-1}}_{A,q},



whence the claim follows.
∎






7.3. Holonomy and flatness




Let AA be a flat connection on PP.
One of the most important features of flat connections is encoded in the following



Theorem 7.5.



If the connection AA is flat, the holonomy H⁡(A,γ,p)\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right) of a loop γ\gamma, based at p∈Pp\in P over γ⁡(0)\gamma(0), depends only on the homotopy class of the loop γ\gamma.





(See [7], Chapter 2, Sections 4, 7 and 8 for more details; let me just recall that it is a consequence of the Ambrose–Singer Theorem, which characterizes completely the Lie algebra of the holonomy group Φ⁡(p)\Phi(p) w.r.t. a given connection on a principal bundle in terms of its curvature form, pp being a reference point in PP.)
On the other hand (see e.g. [6], Chapter 1), it is well-known that a representation of the fundamental group π1​(M)\pi_{1}(M) of MM in GG implies the existence of a principal bundle PP with a flat connection, whose holonomy representation of the fundamental group of MM in GG coincides with the representation giving rise to the bundle with flat connection; this is the well-known correspondence between (gauge-equivalence classes of) flat connections and (conjugacy classes in GG of) representations of the fundamental group of MM.
Notice that π1​(M)\pi_{1}(M) is defined by considering continuous loops, whereas the holonomy w.r.t. a connection is defined in terms of piecewise smooth loops; the connection between the two settings is encoded in the so-called restricted holonomy group, for whose precise definition see [7], Chapter 2, Section 4.




As a consequence of Corollary 6.4 of Section 6, one can view the holonomy H⁡(A,0,1)\partr\!\left(A;0;1\right) w.r.t. AA as a map from the pulled-back bundle ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P to GG, equivariant w.r.t. conjugation in GG; the corresponding gauge-transformation of ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P is denoted by ΦA\Phi_{A}.
There is a natural map from ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P to PP, denoted by ev⁡(0)~\widetilde{\ev(0)}:





ev⁡(0)~​(γ,p):=p.\widetilde{\ev(0)}\left(\gamma;p\right)\colon=p.



Clearly, ev⁡(0)~\widetilde{\ev(0)} is GG-equivariant; thus, the pull-back of AA w.r.t. ev⁡(0)~\widetilde{\ev(0)} is a connection on ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P, which is denoted by ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A.




Now, I have all elements needed to state the following



Theorem 7.6.



The gauge transformation ΦA\Phi_{A} of ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P induced by the holonomy w.r.t. AA stabilizes the connection ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A, i.e. ΦA∗​ev⁡(0)∗​A=ev⁡(0)∗​A\Phi_{A}^{*}\ev(0)^{*}A=\ev(0)^{*}A, if and only if AA is flat.





Before proving Theorem 7.6, I need a few preliminary facts.
A general tangent vector at a point (γ;p)∈ev⁡(0)∗​P\left(\gamma;p\right)\in\ev(0)^{*}P can be written as a pair of tangent vectors (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right), where Xγ∈Tγ⁡L​MX_{\gamma}\in\tange\!_{\gamma}{\mathrm{L}M} (i.e. it is tangent to L​M\mathrm{L}M at γ\gamma, which is equivalent to saying that XγX_{\gamma} is a section of the tangent bundle of MM w.r.t. γ\gamma) and Xp∈Tp⁡PX_{p}\in\tange\!_{p}{P} (i.e. it is tangent to PP at pp).
The condition for the tangent vector (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right) to ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P at (γ;p)\left(\gamma;p\right) to be vertical is that





Xγ=0⇒Tp⁡π⁡(Xp)=Tγ​ev​(0)​(Xγ)=0,X_{\gamma}=0\Rightarrow\tange\!_{p}{\pi}(X_{p})=\tange\!_{\gamma}{\ev(0)}(X_{\gamma})=0,



since (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right) is tangent to ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P.
Therefore, any vertical vector to ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P at (γ;p)\left(\gamma;p\right) can be uniquely written as (0;Xp)\left(0;X_{p}\right), where XpX_{p} is vertical at pp.




On the other hand, the tangent vector (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right) at (γ;p)∈ev⁡(0)∗​P\left(\gamma;p\right)\in\ev(0)^{*}P is ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A-horizontal if and only if





(ev⁡(0)∗​A)(γ;p)​(Xγ,Xp)​=!​Ap​(Xp)=0.\left(\ev(0)^{*}A\right)_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)\overset{!}{=}A_{p}\!\left(X_{p}\right)=0.



Hence, a tangent vector (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right) at (γ;p)\left(\gamma;p\right) is ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A-horizontal if and only if its PP-component is AA-horizontal.






Since the connection AA specifies a splitting of the tangent bundle of PP into the vertical bundle and the AA-horizontal bundle, it is possible to split any tangent vector (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right) to ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P at (γ;p)\left(\gamma;p\right) into a unique sum



(7.6)

(Xγ;Xp)=(0;Xpv)+(Xγ;Xph),\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)=\left(0;X_{p}^{v}\right)+\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}^{h}\right),


where XpvX_{p}^{v}, resp. XphX_{p}^{h}, denotes the vertical, resp. AA-horizontal, part of the vector XpX_{p}.
The splitting (7.6) plays a pivotal rôle in the proof of Theorem 7.6, to which I come now.



Proof of Theorem 7.6.



Let me first prove that the flatness of AA implies that ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A is fixed by the gauge transformation ΦA\Phi_{A}; Lemma 7.7 implies the converse.
Consider a general tangent vector (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right) to ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P at a general point (γ;p)\left(\gamma;p\right), and assume XpX_{p} to be AA-horizontal at pp.
One can assume that (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right) is the tangent vector at (γ;p)\left(\gamma;p\right) of a path (γs;ps)\left(\gamma_{s};p_{s}\right) (s∈Is\in\mathrm{I}), where γs\gamma_{s} is a path in L​M\mathrm{L}M and psp_{s} is an AA-horizontal curve in PP.
Moreover, since (γs;ps)\left(\gamma_{s};p_{s}\right) belongs to ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P, the curve psp_{s} lies over γs​(0)\gamma_{s}(0), for any s∈Is\in\mathrm{I}.




Define a curve γ¯s\overline{\gamma}_{s} in MM, for any s∈Is\in\mathrm{I}, by the rule




(7.7)

γ¯s​(t):=γs​t​(0).\overline{\gamma}_{s}\!\left(t\right)\colon=\gamma_{st}(0).






Construct now a map Γ\Gamma on the unit square I×I\mathrm{I}\times\mathrm{I} by means of the family of curves (7.7) as follows:





Γ⁡(t,s):={γ¯s​(3​t),t∈[0;13]γs​(3​t−1),t∈[13;23]γ¯s​(3−3​t),t∈[23,1].\Gamma\left(t,s\right)\colon=\begin{cases}\overline{\gamma}_{s}\!\left(3t\right),&t\in\left[0;\frac{1}{3}\right]\\
\gamma_{s}\!\left(3t-1\right),&t\in\left[\frac{1}{3};\frac{2}{3}\right]\\
\overline{\gamma}_{s}\!\left(3-3t\right),&t\in\left[\frac{2}{3},1\right].\end{cases}



First of all, for any s∈Is\in\mathrm{I}, Γ⁡(∙,s)\Gamma(\bullet,s) is a closed curve: in fact,





Γ⁡(0,s)=γ¯s​(0)=γ0​(0)=γ⁡(0),Γ⁡(1,s)=γ¯s​(0)=γ⁡(0).\Gamma(0,s)=\overline{\gamma}_{s}(0)=\gamma_{0}(0)=\gamma(0),\quad\Gamma(1,s)=\overline{\gamma}_{s}(0)=\gamma(0).



It follows also that Γ⁡(∙,s)\Gamma(\bullet,s) is based in γ⁡(0)\gamma(0), for any ss in the unit interval.
Finally, it is clear that Γ\Gamma is a homotopy of γ\gamma.




Since any Γ⁡(∙,s):=Γs\Gamma(\bullet,s):=\Gamma_{s} is homotopic to γ\gamma and the connection AA is by assumption flat, one gets, by Theorem 7.5,





H⁡(A,Γs,p)=H⁡(A,γ,p),∀s∈I.\partr\!\left(A;\Gamma_{s};p\right)=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right),\quad\forall s\in\mathrm{I}.



On the other hand, for any s∈Is\in\mathrm{I}, the curve Γs\Gamma_{s} may be written as the composite curve γ¯s−1∘γs∘γ¯s\overline{\gamma}_{s}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{s}\circ\overline{\gamma}_{s}.
Then, Lemma 7.3 and 7.4 imply together





H⁡(A,γ,p)\displaystyle\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)
=H⁡(A,Γs,p)=\displaystyle=\partr\!\left(A;\Gamma_{s};p\right)=




=H⁡(A,γ¯s,1,p,ps)−1​H⁡(A,γs,ps)​H​(A,γ¯s,1,p,ps).\displaystyle=\partr\!\left(A;\overline{\gamma}_{s};1;p,p_{s}\right)^{-1}\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{s};p_{s}\right)\ \partr\!\left(A;\overline{\gamma}_{s};1;p,p_{s}\right).






Define, for any s∈Is\in\mathrm{I}, the curve p¯s​(t)\overline{p}_{s}(t) by the rule





p¯s​(t):=pt​s,\overline{p}_{s}\!\left(t\right)\colon=p_{ts},



where tt is also in the unit interval.




The curve p¯s\overline{p}_{s} is clearly AA-horizontal, as it is a reparametrization of an AA-horizontal curve.
Moreover, it lies over γ¯s\overline{\gamma}_{s}:





π⁡(p¯s​(t))=π⁡(ps​t)=γs​t​(0)=γ¯s​(t),∀t∈I,\pi\left(\overline{p}_{s}\!(t)\right)=\pi\left(p_{st}\right)=\gamma_{st}(0)=\overline{\gamma}_{s}(t),\quad\forall t\in\mathrm{I},



and is based at pp, since p¯s​(0)=p0=p\overline{p}_{s}(0)=p_{0}=p.




Hence, the curve p¯s\overline{p}_{s}, for any s∈Is\in\mathrm{I}, is the unique AA-horizontal lift of the curve γ¯s\overline{\gamma}_{s}, thus it follows





H⁡(A,γ¯s,1,p,ps)=e,∀s∈I.\partr\!\left(A;\overline{\gamma}_{s};1;p,p_{s}\right)=e,\quad\forall s\in\mathrm{I}.



As a consequence, one gets the following identity:





H⁡(A,γ,p)=H⁡(A,γs,ps),∀s∈I,\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{s};p_{s}\right),\quad\forall s\in\mathrm{I},



which is equivalent to the fact that the holonomy of the family of curves (γs;ps)\left(\gamma_{s};p_{s}\right) in ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P is constant, and this yields in turn





T(γ;p)H(A)|01(Xγ;Xp)=0,if Xp is A-horizontal.\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)=0,\quad\text{if $X_{p}$ is $A$-horizontal}.






An explicit computation gives




(7.8)

T(γ;p)⁡ΦA​(Xγ,Xp)\displaystyle\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\Phi}_{A}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)
=(Xγ;Tp⁡RH⁡(A,γ,p)⁡(Xp)+CLOSE\displaystyle=\left(X_{\gamma};\tange\!_{p}{\raction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}}\left(X_{p}\right)\right.+




+TH⁡(A,γ,p)Lp[T(γ;p)H(A)|01(Xγ;Xp)]),\displaystyle\left.+\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}{\laction_{p}}\left[\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)\right]\right),



for any tangent vector (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right) on ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P at (γ;p)\left(\gamma;p\right).




Therefore, if XpX_{p} is AA-horizontal, the above equation simplifies to





T(γ;p)⁡ΦA​(Xγ,Xp)=(Xγ;Tp⁡RH⁡(A,γ,p)⁡(Xp)).\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\Phi}_{A}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)=\left(X_{\gamma};\tange\!_{p}{\raction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}}\left(X_{p}\right)\right).



Finally, one gets by direct computations





ΦA∗​(ev⁡(0)∗​A)(γ;p)​(Xγ,Xp)\displaystyle\Phi_{A}^{*}\left(\ev(0)^{*}A\right)_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)
=(ev⁡(0)∗​A)(γ;p​H⁡(A,γ,p))​[(Xγ;Tp⁡RH⁡(A,γ,p)⁡(Xp))]=\displaystyle=\left(\ev(0)^{*}A\right)_{\left(\gamma;p\!\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)\right)}\left[\left(X_{\gamma};\tange\!_{p}{\raction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}}\left(X_{p}\right)\right)\right]=




=Ap​H⁡(A,γ,p)​[Tp⁡RH⁡(A,γ,p)⁡(Xp)]=\displaystyle=A_{p\!\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}\left[\tange\!_{p}{\raction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}}\left(X_{p}\right)\right]=




=Ad⁡(H⁡(A,γ,p)−1)​[Ap​(Xp)]=\displaystyle=\Ad\left(\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)^{-1}\right)\left[A_{p}\left(X_{p}\right)\right]=




=0,\displaystyle=0,



if (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right) is ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A-horizontal; in the third equality, I made use of the equivariance of AA.






Hence, any ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A-horizontal vector at (γ;p)\left(\gamma;p\right) is also ΦA∗​(ev⁡(0)∗​A)\Phi_{A}^{*}\left(\ev(0)^{*}A\right)-horizontal.





It follows immediately that ΦA∗​(ev⁡(0)∗​A)=ev⁡(0)∗​A\Phi_{A}^{*}\left(\ev(0)^{*}A\right)=\ev(0)^{*}A, since at any point (γ;p)∈ev⁡(0)∗​P\left(\gamma;p\right)\in\ev(0)^{*}P a tangent vector (Xγ;Xp)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right) can be decomposed in a unique way into a vertical piece and an ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A-horizontal piece; by the above computations, ΦA∗​(ev⁡(0)∗​A)\Phi_{A}^{*}\left(\ev(0)^{*}A\right) and ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A agree on any tangent vector, and thus they must be identical.
∎





As mentioned before, to prove the converse claim one needs the following



Lemma 7.7.



The condition that the gauge transformation ΦA\Phi_{A} associated to the holonomy w.r.t. AA fixes the connection ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A can be alternatively encoded into the following horizontality equation:





dev⁡(0)∗​AH(A)|01=0.\mathrm{d}_{\ev(0)^{*}A}\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}=0.






Proof.



Theorem 7.5 implies that, for any couple (γ;p)∈ev⁡(0)∗​P(\gamma;p)\in\ev(0)^{*}P and any tangent vector (Xγ;Xp)(X_{\gamma};X_{p}) to ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P at (γ;p)(\gamma;p), the identity holds:





(ΦA∗​(ev⁡(0)∗​A))(γ;p)​(Xγ,Xp)=ev⁡(0)∗​A(γ;p)​(Xγ,Xp).\left(\Phi_{A}^{*}\!(\ev(0)^{*}A)\right)_{(\gamma;p)}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{p})=\ev(0)^{*}A_{(\gamma;p)}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{p}).






Equation (7.8) gives an explicit expression for the tangent map of the holonomy; one can split the right-hand side of Equation (7.8) into two pieces:





T(γ;p)⁡ΦA​(Xγ,Xp)\displaystyle\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\Phi}_{A}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)
=(Xγ;Tp⁡RH⁡(A,γ,p)⁡(Xp))+\displaystyle=\left(X_{\gamma};\tange\!_{p}{\raction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}}\left(X_{p}\right)\right)+




+(0;TH⁡(A,γ,p)Lp[T(γ;p)H(A)|01(Xγ;Xp)]).\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\left(0;\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}{\laction_{p}}\left[\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)\right]\right).



The second term of the pair on the right-hand side may be also rewritten as





TH⁡(A,γ,p)Lp[T(γ;p)H(A)|01(Xγ;Xp)]=\displaystyle\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}{\laction_{p}}\left[\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)\right]=
TeLp​H⁡(A,γ,p){TH⁡(A,γ,p)LH⁡(A,γ,p)−1\displaystyle\tange\!_{e}{\laction_{p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}}\!\left\{\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}{\laction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)^{-1}}}\right.




[T(γ;p)H(A)|01(Xγ;Xp)]},\displaystyle\left.\left[\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)\right]\right\},



which turns out to be a vertical vector in ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P.




Thus, it follows by the very definition of pull-back:





(ΦA∗​(ev⁡(0)∗​A))(γ;p)​(Xγ,Xp)\displaystyle\left(\Phi_{A}^{*}\!(\ev(0)^{*}A)\right)_{(\gamma;p)}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{p})
=ev⁡(0)∗​A(γ;p​H⁡(A,γ,p))​(Xγ,Tp⁡RH⁡(A,γ,p)⁡(Xp))+\displaystyle=\ev(0)^{*}A_{(\gamma;p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right))}\!\left(X_{\gamma};\tange\!_{p}{\raction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}}\left(X_{p}\right)\right)+




+ev⁡(0)∗​A(γ;p​H⁡(A,γ,p))​(0;TH⁡(A,γ,p)⁡LpCLOSE\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\ev(0)^{*}A_{(\gamma;p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right))}\!\left(0;\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}{\laction_{p}}\right.




[T(γ;p)H(A)|01(Xγ;Xp)])=\displaystyle\phantom{=+}\left.\left[\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)\right]\right)=




=Ad⁡(H⁡(A,γ,p)−1)​ev​(0)∗​A(γ;p)​(Xγ,Xp)+\displaystyle=\Ad\left(\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)^{-1}\right)\ev(0)^{*}A_{(\gamma;p)}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{p})+




+TH⁡(A,γ,p)LH⁡(A,γ,p)−1[T(γ;p)H(A)|01(Xγ;Xp)],\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}{\laction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)^{-1}}}\left[\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)\right],



where in the third equality, I made use of the GG-equivariance of ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A to get the first term and of verticality of ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A to get the second term.




By writing down explicitly the adjoint action, the equation at the beginning of the proof, stating the fact that ΦA\Phi_{A} fixes the connection ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A, takes the form




(7.9)

T(γ;p)H(A)|01(Xγ;Xp)+TeRH⁡(A,γ,p)[Ap(Xp)]−TeLH⁡(A,γ,p)[Ap(Xp)]=0,\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;p\right)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right)+\tange\!_{e}{\raction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}}\!\left[A_{p}(X_{p})\right]-\tange\!_{e}{\laction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;p\right)}}\!\left[A_{p}(X_{p})\right]=0,



and recall that, by the very definition of ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A, one has





Ap​(Xp)=ev⁡(0)∗​A(γ;p)​(Xγ,Xp).A_{p}(X_{p})=\ev(0)^{*}A_{(\gamma;p)}\!\left(X_{\gamma};X_{p}\right).



This is exactly the horizontality equation for the holonomy.
∎





Lemma 7.7 implies that the gauge transformation ΦA\Phi_{A}, viewed as a GG-equivariant map from ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P to GG, is constant along ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A-horizontal curves in ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P: namely, consider a ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A-horizontal curve γ~​(s)=(Γs,ps)\widetilde{\gamma}(s)=\left(\Gamma_{s},p_{s}\right) in ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P, where Γs\Gamma_{s} is a curve in L​M\mathrm{L}M, i.e. a curve of loops, whereas psp_{s} denotes a AA-horizontal curve in PP, such that the projection of psp_{s} onto MM equals Γs​(0)\Gamma_{s}(0), the initial (and final) point of the loop Γs\Gamma_{s}.
Since γ~\widetilde{\gamma} is ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A-horizontal, it follows that





ev⁡(0)∗​Aγ~​(dd​s⁡γ~)=0,\ev(0)^{*}A_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\!\left(\ddss\widetilde{\gamma}\right)=0,



whence it follows, by the very definition of the tangent map and by (7.9),





Tγ~H(A)|01(dd​sγ~)=dd​sH(A)|01(γ~)=!0.\tange\!_{\widetilde{\gamma}}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}}\!\left(\ddss\widetilde{\gamma}\right)=\ddss\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}(\widetilde{\gamma})\overset{!}{=}0.



This is equivalent to the condition that H(A)|01\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1} is constant along the path γ~\widetilde{\gamma}; this is the main argument in the proof of the “only if” part of Theorem 7.6.



End of the proof of Theorem 7.6.



By what was recalled shortly after the statement of Theorem 7.5, given a connection AA, it suffices to prove that the holonomy representation w.r.t. AA based at some point p∈Pp\in P over some x∈Mx\in M,





LM∋γ↦H(A)|01(γ,p)∈G,\mathrm{L}M\ni\gamma\mapsto\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}\!\left(\gamma,p\right)\in G,



descends to a representation of the fundamental group π1​(M)\pi_{1}(M).
To be more precise (see [7], Chapter 2, Section 4, for more details), one has to consider the holonomy as a representation of the fundamental group π1​(M)\pi_{1}(M) (consisting of all continuous loops in MM) into the quotient Lie group Φ​(p)/Φ0​(p)\Phi(p)/\Phi^{0}(p), where, borrowing the notations from [7], Φ⁡(p)\Phi(p), resp. Φ0​(p)\Phi^{0}(p), denotes the holonomy group w.r.t. AA with reference point pp, resp. the restricted holonomy group w.r.t AA with reference point pp; they are respectively subgroups of GG consisting of all elements which can be written in the form H(A)|01(γ,p)\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}(\gamma,p), for γ\gamma a (piecewise smooth) loop in MM, resp. a (piecewise smooth) loop in MM homotopic to the constant loop.
Hence, in this framework, to prove that the holonomy gives truly a representation of π1​(M)\pi_{1}(M) to GG, one has to show that the restricted holonomy group Φ0​(p)\Phi^{0}(p) with reference point pp is trivial.
To see this, consider a (piecewise smooth) loop γ\gamma based at xx, homotopic to the constant loop exe_{x} based at xx; a base point pp over xx is chosen.
A homotopy between γ\gamma and exe_{x} is denoted by Γ\Gamma, or, in other words,





Γ⁡(t,0)=γ⁡(t),Γ⁡(t,1)=ex​(t)=x,Γ⁡(0,s)=x,∀(s,t)∈I×I,\Gamma(t,0)=\gamma(t),\quad\Gamma(t,1)=e_{x}(t)=x,\quad\Gamma(0,s)=x,\quad\forall(s,t)\in\mathrm{I}\times\mathrm{I},



whence it follows that





π⁡(p)=Γ⁡(0,s),∀s∈I.\pi(p)=\Gamma(0,s),\quad\forall s\in\mathrm{I}.



Using the so called Factorization Lemma (see [7], Appendix 7, for the proof), one can assume that the homotopy Γ\Gamma is also piecewise smooth.
Thus, one can consider the curve





I∋s↦γ~​(s):=(Γ⁡(∙,s),p);\mathrm{I}\ni s\mapsto\widetilde{\gamma}(s)\colon=\left(\Gamma(\bullet,s),p\right);



it is obvious that γ~\widetilde{\gamma} lies in ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P and, by its very construction, γ~\widetilde{\gamma} is ev⁡(0)∗​A\ev(0)^{*}A-horizontal.
Therefore, the gauge transformation ΦA\Phi_{A} is constant along the curve γ~\widetilde{\gamma}, i.e.





H(A)|01(Γ(∙,0),p)=H(A)|01(γ,p)=!H(A)|01(Γ(∙,1),p)=H(A)|01(ex,p)=e,\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}\!\left(\Gamma(\bullet,0),p\right)=\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}\!\left(\gamma,p\right)\overset{!}{=}\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}\!\left(\Gamma(\bullet,1),p\right)=\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}\!\left(e_{x},p\right)=e,



whence the claim follows.




Thus, the quotient group Φ​(p)/Φ0​(p)\Phi(p)/\Phi^{0}(p) equals Φ⁡(p)\Phi(p), which is a subgroup of GG; it follows that AA is flat, since the corresponding holonomy gives a representation of the fundamental group of MM in GG.
∎




7.3.1. Flatness and the Wilson loop map




In this subsubsection, I want to discuss the consequences of Theorem 7.5 and Lemma 7.7 for the Wilson loop map, introduced and discussed in Subsubsection 6.0.1.




Recall that the Wilson loop map WρW_{\rho}, defined upon the choice of a linear representation (V,ρ)(V,\rho) of the structure group GG, is a function on the product of the moduli space of (irreducible) connections 𝒜P∗/𝒢P∗\mathcal{A}_{P}^{*}/\mathcal{G}_{P}^{*} and the space of loops L​M\mathrm{L}M.
Moreover, the Wilson loop function WρW_{\rho} comes from a 𝒢P∗×G\mathcal{G}_{P}^{*}\times G-invariant function on the product bundle 𝒜P∗×ev⁡(0)∗​P\mathcal{A}_{P}^{*}\times\ev(0)^{*}P, which is simply the trace of the holonomy map H(∙)|01\partr(\bullet)\arrowvert_{0}^{1} in the representation ρ\rho.




Notice that, by the very definition of flatness, the (restricted) gauge group 𝒢P∗\mathcal{G}_{P}^{*} operates on the subspace of 𝒜P∗\mathcal{A}_{P}^{*} consisting of flat connections; hence, one can consider the Wilson loop map WρW_{\rho} on the moduli space of flat connections ℳP\mathcal{M}_{P} on PP, which is simply





ℳP:={A∈𝒜P∗:FA=0}/𝒢P∗.\mathcal{M}_{P}\colon=\left\{A\in\mathcal{A}_{P}^{*}\colon F_{A}=0\right\}/\mathcal{G}_{P}^{*}.



Lemma 7.7 yields the following



Proposition 7.8.



The Wilson loop map WρW_{\rho} on the product ℳP×L​M\mathcal{M}_{P}\times\mathrm{L}M is closed w.r.t. the De Rham differential, for any class of flat connections; equivalently, the map





ℳP∋[A]↦Wρ​(A),\mathcal{M}_{P}\ni[A]\mapsto W_{\rho}(A),



assigning to every class of flat connections the Wilson loop map w.r.t. [A][A] on L​M\mathrm{L}M, is map from the moduli space of (irreducible) flat connections to the 00-th De Rham cohomology group of L​M\mathrm{L}M.




Proof.



It suffices to prove that, for any (irreducible) flat connection AA on PP, the function





ev⁡(0)∗​P∋(γ;p)↦Wρ​(A)​(γ,p):=Wρ​(A,γ,p)\ev(0)^{*}P\ni(\gamma;p)\mapsto W_{\rho}(A)(\gamma;p)\colon=W_{\rho}(A;\gamma;p)



on the bundle ev⁡(0)∗​P\ev(0)^{*}P is d\mathrm{d}-closed.




The horizontality equation of Lemma 7.7 takes the following form in the linear group GL⁡(V)\GL(V):





T(γ;p)ρ(H(A)|01)(Xγ;Xp)+ρ¯(Ap(Xp))ρ(H(A)|01)(γ;p)−ρ(H(A)|01)(γ;p)ρ¯(Ap(Xp))=0,\tange\!_{(\gamma;p)}{\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{p})+\overline{\rho}(A_{p}(X_{p}))\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})(\gamma;p)-\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})(\gamma;p)\overline{\rho}(A_{p}(X_{p}))=0,



where ρ¯:=Te⁡ρ\overline{\rho}\colon=\tange\!_{e}{\rho} is the derived Lie algebra-representation of 𝔤\mathfrak{g} coming from the GG-representation ρ\rho.




By its very definition, the following equation for the exterior derivative of the Wilson loop map Wρ​(A)W_{\rho}(A) holds:





d​Wρ​(A)(γ;p)​(Xγ,Xp)\displaystyle\mathrm{d}W_{\rho}(A)_{(\gamma;p)}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{p})
=dTrV(ρ(H(A)|01)(γ;p)(Xγ;Xp)=\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\tr_{V}\left(\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1}\right)_{(\gamma;p)}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{p})=




=TrV(T(γ;p)ρ(H(A)|01)(Xγ;Xp))=\displaystyle=\tr_{V}\left(\tange\!_{(\gamma;p)}{\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{p})\right)=




=TrV(T(γ;p)ρ(H(A)|01)(Xγ;Xp))+\displaystyle=\tr_{V}\left(\tange\!_{(\gamma;p)}{\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{p})\right)+




+TrV(ρ¯(Ap(Xp))ρ(H(A)|01)(γ;p))−\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\tr_{V}\left(\overline{\rho}(A_{p}(X_{p}))\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})(\gamma;p)\right)-




−TrV(ρ(H(A)|01)(γ;p)ρ¯(Ap(Xp)))=\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\tr_{V}\left(\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})(\gamma;p)\overline{\rho}(A_{p}(X_{p}))\right)=




=0,\displaystyle=0,



where the third identity is a consequence of the cyclicity of the trace, and the fourth identity follows directly from the horizontality equation of Lemma 7.7.
∎





Hence, to any orbit in the moduli space ℳP\mathcal{M}_{P}, the Wilson loop map Wρ​(A)W_{\rho}(A) associates an element of the 00-th De Rham cohomology group, i.e. a locally constant function on the space of loops.






7.4. Flatness and parallel transport




Let me return to the principal bundles ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P and ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P on the cylinder L​M×I\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I} or P​M×I\mathrm{P}\!M\times\mathrm{I}.
Denote by ev~\widetilde{\ev}, resp. ev0~\widetilde{\ev_{0}}, the natural map from ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P, resp. ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P, to PP, given by





ev~​(γ,t,p):=p,resp.ev0~​(γ,t,q):=q.\widetilde{\ev}\left(\gamma;t;p\right)\colon=p,\quad\text{resp.}\quad\widetilde{\ev_{0}}\left(\gamma;t;q\right)\colon=q.



Denote also by ev∗⁡A\ev^{*}A, resp. ev0∗​A\ev_{0}^{*}A, the pull-back of AA w.r.t. ev~\widetilde{\ev}, resp. ev0~\widetilde{\ev_{0}}; ev∗⁡A\ev^{*}A, resp. ev0∗​A\ev_{0}^{*}A, is clearly a connection on ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P, resp. ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P, since both maps ev~\widetilde{\ev} and ev0~\widetilde{\ev_{0}} are GG-equivariant.
Consider the fibred product of ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P and ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P, and a point (γ,t,p,q)\left(\gamma;t;p,q\right) in it.
Denote by the 44-tuple (Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p},X_{q}\right) a general tangent vector to ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P at a point (γ,t,p,q)\left(\gamma;t;p,q\right), where OPENi)i) XγX_{\gamma} is tangent to L​M\mathrm{L}M at the curve γ\gamma, OPENi​i)ii) XtX_{t} is tangent to I\mathrm{I} at tt, and OPENi​i​i)iii) XpX_{p}, resp. XqX_{q}, is tangent to PP at pp, resp. qq.




The condition on the tangent vector (Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p},X_{q}\right) to be vertical may be translated into the set of equations





Xγ=0,Xt=0⇒{Tp⁡π⁡(Xp)=T(γ,t)⁡ev0⁡(Xγ;Xt)=0,Tq⁡π⁡(Xq)=T(γ,t)⁡ev⁡(Xγ;Xt)=0,X_{\gamma}=0,\quad X_{t}=0\Rightarrow\ \begin{cases}\tange\!_{p}{\pi}(X_{p})&=\tange\!_{\left(\gamma,t\right)}{\ev_{0}}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t}\right)=0,\\
\tange\!_{q}{\pi}\left(X_{q}\right)&=\tange\!_{\left(\gamma,t\right)}{\ev}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t}\right)=0,\end{cases}



where the first identities follow from the definition of the bundle projection from ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P onto L​M×I\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I}, and the remaining are consequences of (Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p},X_{q}\right) being tangent to ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P.Hence, a tangent vector (Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p},X_{q}\right) to the fibred product ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P at a point (γ,t,p,q)\left(\gamma;t;p,q\right) is vertical if and only if its PP-pieces are vertical and its L​M×I\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I}-piece vanishes.
As already known, the connection AA specifies a splitting of the tangent bundle of PP.
Therefore, any tangent vector XpX_{p} to PP at pp can be written uniquely as Xp=Xpv+XphX_{p}=X_{p}^{v}+X_{p}^{h}, where XpvX_{p}^{v}, resp. XphX_{p}^{h}, denotes the vertical part, resp. the horizontal part, of XpX_{p}.






Thus, it is possible to write in a unique way




(Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)=(0,0,Xpv,Xqv)+(Xγ,Xt,Xph,Xqh),\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p},X_{q}\right)=\left(0;0;X_{p}^{v},X_{q}^{v}\right)+\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p}^{h},X_{q}^{h}\right),


for any tangent vector (Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p},X_{q}\right) to ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P at (γ,t,p,q)\left(\gamma;t;p,q\right).
The above splitting specifies in an obvious way a G×GG\times G-invariant distribution on ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P; the corresponding connection 11-form is simply the fibred product connection





(ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A)(γ,t,p,q)​(Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq):=(Ap​(Xp),Aq​(Xq)).\left(\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A\right)_{\left(\gamma;t;p,q\right)}\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p},X_{q}\right):=\left(A_{p}\left(X_{p}\right),A_{q}\left(X_{q}\right)\right).



Recall by Subsection 7.1, that the fibred product connection ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A is flat if and only if both its components are flat; hence, if AA is flat, then ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A is also flat.
Analogous results hold for ev∗⁡P⊙ev0∗​P\ev^{*}P\odot\ev_{0}^{*}P; denote the connection constructed from AA on the fibred product ev∗⁡P⊙ev0∗​P\ev^{*}P\odot\ev_{0}^{*}P by ev∗⁡A⊙ev0∗​A\ev^{*}A\odot\ev_{0}^{*}A.
Consider the fibred product map of ΦA\Phi_{A}, which gives by Lemma 7.1 of Subsection 7.1, a bundle (iso)morphism from ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P to ev∗⁡P⊙ev0∗​P\ev^{*}P\odot\ev_{0}^{*}P:





ΦA⊙ΦA−1​(γ,t,p,q):=(γ,t,q​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q),p​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)−1).\Phi_{A}\odot\Phi_{A}^{-1}\left(\gamma;t;p,q\right):=\left(\gamma;t;q\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right),p\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)^{-1}\right).






After this discussion, one can state and prove the following



Theorem 7.9.



If AA flat, the following identity holds:




(7.10)

(ΦA⊙ΦA−1)∗​(ev∗⁡A⊙ev0∗​A)=ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A.\left(\Phi_{A}\odot\Phi_{A}^{-1}\right)^{*}\left(\ev^{*}A\odot\ev_{0}^{*}A\right)=\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A.






Proof.



It suffices to show that any ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A-horizontal vector in ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P is also horizontal w.r.t. the pull-back of ev∗⁡A⊙ev0∗​A\ev^{*}A\odot\ev_{0}^{*}A by the fibred product map ΦA⊙ΦA−1\Phi_{A}\odot\Phi_{A}^{-1}.
One can view a general ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A-horizontal vector (Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}\right), where the subscripts are related to the base points of the respective tangent vectors, as the initial tangent direction of an ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A-horizontal curve, which is denoted by (γs,t¯s,ps,qs)\left(\gamma_{s};\overline{t}_{s};p_{s},q_{s}\right); one can also assume that psp_{s} and qsq_{s} are AA-horizontal curves in PP.
Here, t¯s\overline{t}_{s} denotes a curve in I\mathrm{I} starting at t¯0=t\overline{t}_{0}=t.

π⁡(ps)=γs​(0),π⁡(qs)=γs​(ts),∀s∈I.\pi(p_{s})=\gamma_{s}(0),\quad\pi(q_{s})=\gamma_{s}(t_{s}),\quad\forall s\in\mathrm{I}.



Furthermore, define the (piecewise smooth) family of loops in MM, which is denoted by Γ\Gamma, via the assignment





Γ⁡(t,s):={γ¯s​(4​t),t∈[0;14]γs​((4​t−1)​t¯s),t∈[14,12]γ^s​(3−4​t),t∈[12;34]γ⁡((4−4​t)​t¯0),t∈[34,1].\Gamma(t,s)\colon=\begin{cases}\overline{\gamma}_{s}(4t),&t\in\left[0;\frac{1}{4}\right]\\

H⁡(A,γs,t¯s,ps,qs)=H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q).\partr\!\left(A;\gamma_{s};\overline{t}_{s};p_{s},q_{s}\right)=\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right).

T(γ,t,p,q)H(A)|0∙(Xγ;Xt;Xp;Xq)=0,\tange\!_{(\gamma;t;p;q)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}}\!\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}\right)=0,



if the tangent vector (Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}\right) is ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A-horizontal.




An explicit computation gives, for a general tangent vector (Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}) to ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P at a general point (γ,t,p,q)(\gamma;t;p;q)






T(γ,t,p,q)⁡(ΦA⊙ΦA−1)​(Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)=(Xγ;Xt;Tq⁡R~H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)​(Xq)+CLOSE\displaystyle\tange\!_{(\gamma,t;p,q)}{\left(\Phi_{A}\odot\Phi_{A}^{-1}\right)}\!\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}\right)=\big(X_{\gamma};X_{t};\tange\!_{q}{\widetilde{\raction}_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)}}\!(X_{q})+\big.




+TH⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)Lq(T(γ,t,p,q)H(A)|0∙(Xγ;Xt;Xp;Xq));TpRH⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)−1(Xp)+\displaystyle\big.+\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)}{\laction_{q}}\!\left(\tange\!_{(\gamma;t;p;q)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}}\!\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}\right)\right);\tange\!_{p}{\raction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)^{-1}}}\!(X_{p})+\big.




+TH⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)−1Lp(T(γ,t,p,q)(H(A)|0∙)−1(Xγ;Xt;Xp;Xq))).\displaystyle\big.+\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)^{-1}}{\laction_{p}}\!\left(\tange\!_{(\gamma;t;p;q)}{\left(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}\right)^{-1}}\!\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}\right)\right)\big).






The previous expression simplifies remarkably, assuming the tangent vector (Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}) to be ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A-horizontal:





T(γ,t,p,q)⁡(ΦA⊙ΦA−1)​(Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)\displaystyle\tange\!_{(\gamma,t;p,q)}{\left(\Phi_{A}\odot\Phi_{A}^{-1}\right)}\!\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}\right)
=(Xγ;Xt;Tq⁡R~H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)​(Xq)CLOSE;\displaystyle=\big(X_{\gamma};X_{t};\tange\!_{q}{\widetilde{\raction}_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)}}\!(X_{q});\big.




OPENTp⁡RH⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)−1⁡(Xp))\displaystyle\big.\phantom{=}\quad\tange\!_{p}{\raction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)^{-1}}}\!(X_{p})\big)



Hence, a straightforward computation similar in spirit to the final computation in the proof of Theorem 7.5, where I make use of the GG-equivariance of the connection AA, implies:






Any ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A-horizontal vector in the fibred product ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P is also (ΦA⊙ΦA−1)∗​(ev∗⁡A⊙ev0∗​A)\left(\Phi_{A}\odot\Phi_{A}^{-1}\right)^{*}\!\left(\ev^{*}A\odot\ev_{0}^{*}A\right)-horizontal.





The claim is then a consequence of arguments very similar to those used in the final steps of the proof of Theorem 7.6.
∎





By Lemma 7.2, Equation (7.10) in Theorem 7.9 may be rewritten as follows:





ΦA∗​ev∗​A=ev0∗​A.\Phi_{A}^{*}\ev^{*}A=\ev_{0}^{*}A.



On the other hand, one can rewrite Equation (7.10) as a “horizontality equation”, with help of the following



Corollary 7.10.



The condition that the isomorphism ΦA⊙ΦA−1\Phi_{A}\odot\Phi_{A}^{-1} associated to the parallel transport w.r.t. AA intertwines the fibred product connections ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A and ev∗⁡A⊙ev0∗​A\ev^{*}A\odot\ev_{0}^{*}A may be written as a horizontality equation for the parallel transport:





dH(A)|0∙+ev∗AH(A)|0∙−H(A)|0∙ev0∗A=0,\mathrm{d}\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}+\ev^{*}A\ \partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}-\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}\ \ev_{0}^{*}A=0,



where, by abuse of notation, I denoted by ev0∗​A\ev^{*}_{0}A and ev∗⁡A\ev^{*}A the respective pull-backs w.r.t. the projections pr1\pr_{1} and pr2\pr_{2} from ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P onto ev0∗​P\ev_{0}^{*}P and ev∗⁡P\ev^{*}P, respectively.
By abuse of notation, I have also simply denoted as products in the above formula what I should have written as tangent maps of left- and right multiplication; the explicit formulae in the proof will point out to the precise notation I have skipped above.




Proof.



Theorem 7.9 implies that, for any point (γ,t,p,q)(\gamma;t;p,q) in ev0∗​P⊙ev∗⁡P\ev_{0}^{*}P\odot\ev^{*}P and any tangent vector (Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p},X_{q}) at this point to the fibred product bundle, the identity holds:





((ΦA⊙ΦA−1)∗​(ev∗⁡A⊙ev0∗​A))(γ,t,p,q)​(Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq)==(ev0∗​A⊙ev∗⁡A)(γ,t,p,q)​(Xγ,Xt,Xp,Xq).\left((\Phi_{A}\odot\Phi_{A}^{-1})^{*}\!(\ev^{*}A\odot\ev_{0}^{*}A)\right)_{(\gamma;t;p,q)}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p},X_{q})=\\
=(\ev_{0}^{*}A\odot\ev^{*}A)_{(\gamma;t;p,q)}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p},X_{q}).






Recall the expression for the tangent map of the isomorphism ΦA⊙ΦA−1\Phi_{A}\odot\Phi_{A}^{-1}:

+TH⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)−1Lp(T(γ,t,p,q)(H(A)|0∙)−1(Xγ;Xt;Xp;Xq))).\displaystyle\big.+\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)^{-1}}{\laction_{p}}\!\left(\tange\!_{(\gamma;t;p;q)}{\left(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}\right)^{-1}}\!\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}\right)\right)\big).



Notice that the right-hand side of Equation (7.12) can be splitted in two tangent vectors, namely

The tangent vector TH⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)Lq(T(γ,t,p,q)H(A)|0∙(Xγ;Xt;Xp;Xq))\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)}{\laction_{q}}\!\left(\tange\!_{(\gamma;t;p;q)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}}\!\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}\right)\right) can be then rewritten as

=TeLq​H⁡(A,γ,t,p,q){TH⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)LH⁡(A,γ,t,p,q)−1\displaystyle=\tange\!_{e}{\laction_{q\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)}}\!\left\{\tange\!_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)}{\laction_{\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t;p,q\right)^{-1}}}\right.

Thus, the second vector in the previous splitting of Equation (7.12) is vertical, whence one can deduce from the definition of fibred product connection and from the first equation at the beginning of the proof the pair of equations:





Ap​(Xp)\displaystyle A_{p}(X_{p})

+TH(A)|0∙(γ;t;p,q)L(H(A)|0∙)−1(γ;t;p,q)[T(γ,t,p,q)H(A)|0∙(Xγ;Xt;Xp;Xq)]\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\tange\!_{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}\!(\gamma;t;p,q)}{\laction_{(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet})^{-1}\!(\gamma;t;p,q)}}\!\left[\tange\!_{(\gamma;t;p;q)}{\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{\bullet}}\!\left(X_{\gamma};X_{t};X_{p};X_{q}\right)\right]
