Observe that I used the GG-equivariance and the verticality of AA in the second identities.




It is not difficult to see that both equations are equivalent, and each one is in turn equivalent to the horizontality equation for the parallel transport, thus yielding the claim.
∎







8. Applications of the generalized gauge transformation associated to the parallel transport: iterated Chen integrals




In this section, I want to discuss an application of the computations performed in Section 7, which was already mentioned in the Introduction and which was the main motivation for this paper, namely iterated Chen integrals in the spirit of [2] and [4], where I discussed so-called generalized holonomies as differential forms on the space of loops L​M\mathrm{L}M.
These differential forms are constructed via iterated Chen integrals in the sense of [5]; they are obtained by taking push-forward (i.e. integration along the fibre of trivial fibre bundles, whose fibres are simplices △n\triangle_{n}, for any n∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}) of traces in some representation of the Lie algebra 𝔤\mathfrak{g} of the structure group GG of products of differential forms on L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g} (or, to be more precise, in the universal enveloping algebra of 𝔤\mathfrak{g}), which are in turn pull-backs of differential forms on MM with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g} w.r.t. evaluation maps at different points of the simplex △n\triangle_{n}.
As it is well-known, differential forms on MM with values in the Lie algebra 𝔤\mathfrak{g} of GG are in one-to-one correspondence with differential forms on MM with values in the vector bundle associated to the trivial principal GG-bundle over MM via the adjoint representation of GG.




The main question is: is it possible to consider analogues of the aforementioned Chen-type integrals, when considering a nontrivial principal GG-bundle PP, and consequently differential forms on MM with values in the associated bundle ad⁡P\ad P to PP via the adjoint representation of GG?
The answer is positive,




8.1. Parallel transport and simplices




I am therefore interested in the following more general situation: instead of considering the “cylinder” L​M×I\mathrm{L}M\times\mathrm{I}, consider the more general space L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n}, where △n\triangle_{n} denotes the standard nn-dimensional simplex





△n:={(t1,…,tn)∈In:0≤t1≤⋯≤tn≤1}.\triangle_{n}\colon=\left\{\left(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\right)\in\mathrm{I}^{n}\colon 0\leq t_{1}\leq\cdots\leq t_{n}\leq 1\right\}.



Notice that I=△1\mathrm{I}=\triangle_{1}.




There are n+1n+1 natural maps from L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} into MM, given by





evi,n:=ev∘πi,n,1≤i≤n,ev⁡(0)n:=ev⁡(0)∘πn,\ev_{i,n}\colon=\ev\circ\pi_{i,n},\quad 1\leq i\leq n,\quad\ev(0)_{n}\colon=\ev(0)\circ\pi_{n},



where πi,n​(γ,t1,…,tn):=(γ;ti)\pi_{i,n}\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\right)\colon=\left(\gamma;t_{i}\right) and πn​(γ,t1,…,tn):=γ\pi_{n}\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\right)\colon=\gamma.




Accordingly to the definitions of Section 6, define the pulled-back bundles evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P and ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P over L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n}, as well as their fibred product ev⁡(0)n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P and evi,n∗​P⊙evj,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}P, for any two indices 1≤i<j≤n1\leq i<j\leq n; the natural maps from ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P and evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P to PP are denoted by ev⁡(0)n~\widetilde{\ev(0)_{n}} and evi,n~\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}} respectively.




Given a connection AA on PP, natural connections on the fibred products ev⁡(0)n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P and evi,n∗​P⊙evj,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}P are given by the fibred product connections ev⁡(0)n∗​A⊙evi,n∗​A\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}A and evi,n∗​A⊙evj,n∗​A\ev_{i,n}^{*}A\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}A; denote by evi,n∗​A⊙ev⁡(0)n∗​A\ev_{i,n}^{*}A\odot\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A the corresponding fibred product connection on evi,n∗​P⊙ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\odot\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P.
(As before, I changed slightly the notation: in fact, I pulled the connection AA w.r.t. the corresponding bundle maps evi,n~\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}. resp. ev⁡(0)n~\widetilde{\ev(0)_{n}}.)



Proposition 8.1.



For any connection AA on PP, the parallel transport map H(A)|0i\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{i} defined by





ev⁡(0)n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P∋(γ,t1,…,tn,p0,pi)↦H⁡(A,γ,ti,p0,pi)∈G,\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\ni\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n};p_{0},p_{i}\right)\mapsto\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t_{i};p_{0},p_{i}\right)\in G,



is a generalized gauge transformation between the bundle ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P and evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P.
Moreover, the triple (H(∙)|0i,ΦP,i,φP,i)\left(\partr(\bullet)\arrowvert_{0}^{i},\Phi_{P,i},\varphi_{P,i}\right), where H(∙)|0i\partr(\bullet)\arrowvert_{0}^{i} is viewed as a map from the space of connections on PP to the generalized gauge transformations between ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P and evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P, is an equivariant map from the space of connections, viewed as a 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}-space, to the groupoid 𝒢G,L​M×△n\mathcal{G}_{G,\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n}} of generalized gauge transformations on L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n}.
Here, the morphism (ΦP,i,φP,i)\left(\Phi_{P,i},\varphi_{P,i}\right) is defined as follows





ΦP,i​(σ):=(σev⁡(0)n,σevi,n),φP,i​(P):=(ev⁡(0)n∗​P,evi,n∗​P),\Phi_{P,i}(\sigma)\colon=\left(\sigma_{\ev(0)_{n}},\sigma_{\ev_{i,n}}\right),\quad\varphi_{P,i}(P)\colon=\left(\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P,\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\right),



where I used the same notations of Lemma 6.1 and Lemma 6.2 of Section 6.




Proof.



The proof is a consequence of the very same arguments used in the proof of Proposition 6.3.
∎





As a consequence of Proposition 8.1 together with Theorem 4.10, the bundle ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P is isomorphic to evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P, for any index ii, via





ΦA,i,n​(γ,t1,…,tn,p):=(γ,t1,…,tn,q​H⁡(A,γ,ti,p,q)),\Phi_{A,i,n}\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n};p\right)\colon=\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n};q\ \partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t_{i};p,q\right)\right),



where π⁡(p)=γ⁡(0)\pi(p)=\gamma(0), and qq is any point in PP over γ⁡(ti)\gamma(t_{i}).




Consider now two indices 1≤i<j≤n1\leq i<j\leq n and the corresponding bundles evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P, evj,n∗​P\ev_{j,n}^{*}P, and their fibred products evi,n∗​P⊙evj,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}P and evj,n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev_{j,n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P.




For a connection AA on PP, define the following parallel transport map, which is denoted by H(A)|ij\partr(A)\arrowvert_{i}^{j},




(8.1)

evi,n∗​P⊙evj,n∗​P∋(γ,t1,…,tn,pi,pj)↦H⁡(A,γ,ti,tj,pi,pj)∈G.\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}P\ni\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n};p_{i},p_{j}\right)\mapsto\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t_{i},t_{j};p_{i},p_{j}\right)\in G.



Corollary 3.8 implies the following



Proposition 8.2.



For any connection AA on PP, the parallel transport map H(A)|ij\partr(A)\arrowvert_{i}^{j} defined by Equation (8.1), for any two indices 1≤i<j≤n1\leq i<j\leq n, is a generalized gauge transformation between the bundles evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P and evj,n∗​P\ev_{j,n}^{*}P.
Moreover, the triple (H(∙)|ij,ΦP,i,j,φP,i,j)\left(\partr(\bullet)\arrowvert_{i}^{j},\Phi_{P,i,j},\varphi_{P,i,j}\right), where H(∙)|ij\partr(\bullet)\arrowvert_{i}^{j} is viewed as a map from the space of connections on PP to the generalized gauge transformations between evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P and evj,n∗​P\ev_{j,n}^{*}P, is an equivariant map from the space of connections as a 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}-space, to the groupoid 𝒢G,L​M×△N\mathcal{G}_{G,\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{N}} of generalized gauge transformations on L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} w.r.t. generalized conjugation.
The morphism (ΦP,i,j,φP,i,j)\left(\Phi_{P,i,j},\varphi_{P,i,j}\right) is defined in this case via





ΦP,i,j​(σ):=(σevi,n,σevj,n),φP,i,j​(P):=(evi,n∗​P,evj,n∗​P),\Phi_{P,i,j}(\sigma)\colon=\left(\sigma_{\ev_{i,n}},\sigma_{\ev_{j,n}}\right),\quad\varphi_{P,i,j}(P)\colon=\left(\ev_{i,n}^{*}P,\ev_{j,n}^{*}P\right),



where I used the same notations of Lemma 6.1 and Lemma 6.2 of Section 6.





According to Theorem 4.10, there is a bundle (iso)morphism ΦA,i,j,n\Phi_{A,i,j,n} from evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P to evj,n∗​P\ev_{j,n}^{*}P, defined via





evi,n∗​P∋(γ,t1,…,tn,p)​↦ΦA,i,j,n​(γ,t1,…,tn,q​H⁡(A,γ,ti,tj,p,q))∈evj,n∗​P,\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\ni\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n};p\right)\overset{\Phi_{A,i,j,n}}{\mapsto}\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n};q\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t_{i},t_{j};p,q\right)\right)\in\ev_{j,n}^{*}P,



where qq is any point obeying π⁡(q)=γ⁡(tj)\pi(q)=\gamma(t_{j}).





8.2. Flatness and parallel transport on simplices




In this subsection, I want to discuss analogues of Theorem 7.9 of Subsection 7.4 for the isomorphisms ΦA,i,n\Phi_{A,i,n} and ΦA,i,j,n\Phi_{A,i,j,n} introduced and discussed in the previous subsection.
I begin therefore with a discussion of verticality and ev⁡(0)n∗​A⊙evi,n∗​A\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}A-horizontality of tangent vectors to the fibred product ev⁡(0)n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P, resp. evi,n∗​P⊙evj,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}P, for any two indices 1≤i<j≤n1\leq i<j\leq n.




A tangent vector to ev⁡(0)n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P at the point (γ,t1,…,tn,p,q)\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n};p,q\right) is given by a n+3n+3-tuple (Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p},X_{q}\right), where OPENi)i) XγX_{\gamma} is a tangent vector to L​M\mathrm{L}M at γ\gamma (hence, a vector field on the pull-back of the tangent bundle of MM w.r.t. γ\gamma), OPENi​i)ii) XiX_{i} a tangent vector to I\mathrm{I} at tit_{i}, which is the projection on the ii-th factor of the nn-simplex △n\triangle_{n}, and OPENi​i​i)iii) XpX_{p}, resp. XqX_{q}, is a tangent vector to PP at pp, resp. qq.




The condition on (Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p},X_{q}\right) to be vertical, by the very definition of the bundle projection onto L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n}, is encoded in the following equation:





Xγ=0,Xi=0,∀1≤i≤n⇒{Tp⁡π⁡(Xp)=T(γ,t1,…)​ev​(0)n​(Xγ,X1,…)=0,Tq⁡π⁡(Xq)=T(γ,t1,…)⁡evi,n⁡(Xγ,X1,…)=0.X_{\gamma}=0,\ X_{i}=0,\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq n\Rightarrow\begin{cases}\tange\!_{p}{\pi}\!(X_{p})&=\tange\!_{(\gamma;t_{1},\dots)}{\ev(0)_{n}}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots)=0,\\
\tange\!_{q}{\pi}\!(X_{q})&=\tange\!_{(\gamma;t_{1},\dots)}{\ev_{i,n}}\!(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots)=0.\end{cases}



Hence, a general tangent vector (Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p},X_{q}\right) to ev⁡(0)n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P at the point (γ,t1,…,tn,p,q)\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n};p,q\right) is vertical if and only if its L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n}-piece vanishes and both its PP-pieces are vertical in PP.




The choice of a connection AA on PP determines a unique splitting of any tangent vector to PP in its vertical part and AA-horizontal part; hence, the two PP-pieces of any tangent vector (Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p},X_{q}\right) to ev⁡(0)n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P at the point (γ,t1,…,tn,p,q)\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n};p,q\right) may be decomposed in a unique way as





Xp=Xpv+Xph,Xq=Xqv+Xqh.X_{p}=X_{p}^{v}+X_{p}^{h},\quad X_{q}=X_{q}^{v}+X_{q}^{h}.








Thus, one has the unique decomposition of (Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p},X_{q}\right):




(Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xp,Xq)=(0,0,…,0,Xpv,Xqv)+(Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xph,Xqh).\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p},X_{q}\right)=\left(0;0,\dots,0;X_{p}^{v},X_{q}^{v}\right)+\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p}^{h},X_{q}^{h}\right).


It is easy to verify, by the very definition of fibred product connection ev⁡(0)n∗​A⊙evi,n∗​A\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}A, that the tangent vector (Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xph,Xqh)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p}^{h},X_{q}^{h}\right) is ev⁡(0)n∗​A⊙evi,n∗​A\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}A-horizontal.





Analogously, when considering the fibred product evi,n∗​P⊙evj,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}P, one gets






Given a connection AA on PP, there is a unique splitting of any tangent vector (Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xp,Xq)\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p},X_{q}\right) to evi,n∗​P⊙evj,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}P:




(Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xp,Xq)=(0,0,…,0,Xpv,Xqv)+(Xγ,X1,…,Xn,Xph,Xqh),\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p},X_{q}\right)=\left(0;0,\dots,0;X_{p}^{v},X_{q}^{v}\right)+\left(X_{\gamma};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};X_{p}^{h},X_{q}^{h}\right),


where XphX_{p}^{h}, resp. XqhX_{q}^{h}, is an AA-horizontal vector to PP at pp, resp. qq.
The second piece in the above identity is obviously evi,n∗​A⊙evj,n∗​A\ev_{i,n}^{*}A\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}A-horizontal, by the very definition of fibred product connection.





Consider the fibred product map ΦA,i,n⊙ΦA,i,n−1\Phi_{A,i,n}\odot\Phi_{A,i,n}^{-1}, resp. ΦA,i,j,n⊙ΦA,i,j,n−1\Phi_{A,i,j,n}\odot\Phi_{A,i,j,n}^{-1}, which is an isomorphism from ev⁡(0)n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P to evi,n∗​P⊙ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\odot\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P, resp. from evi,n∗⊙evj,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}P to evj,n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev_{j,n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P; explicitly, these maps take the form





(ΦA,i,n⊙ΦA,i,n−1)​(γ,t1,…,p,q)\displaystyle\left(\Phi_{A,i,n}\odot\Phi_{A,i,n}^{-1}\right)\!\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots;p,q\right)
=(γ,t1,…,q​H⁡(A,γ,ti,p,q),p​H⁡(A,γ,ti,p,q)−1)\displaystyle=\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots;q\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t_{i};p,q\right),p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t_{i};p,q\right)^{-1}\right)



(ΦA,i,j,n⊙ΦA,i,j,n−1)​(γ,t1,…,p,q)\displaystyle\left(\Phi_{A,i,j,n}\odot\Phi_{A,i,j,n}^{-1}\right)\!\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots;p,q\right)
=(γ;t1,…;q​H⁡(A,γ,ti,tj,p,q)CLOSE,\displaystyle=\Big(\gamma;t_{1},\dots;q\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t_{i},t_{j};p,q\right),\Big.




OPENp​H⁡(A,γ,ti,tj,p,q)−1).\displaystyle\qquad\Big.p\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t_{i},t_{j};p,q\right)^{-1}\Big).






The same arguments used in the proof of Theorem 7.9 yield the following



Theorem 8.3.



If AA is flat, the following identities hold:





(ΦA,i,n⊙ΦA,i,n−1)∗​(evi,n∗​A⊙ev⁡(0)n∗​A)\displaystyle\left(\Phi_{A,i,n}\odot\Phi_{A,i,n}^{-1}\right)^{*}\!\left(\ev_{i,n}^{*}A\odot\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A\right)
=ev⁡(0)n∗​A⊙evi,n∗​A,\displaystyle=\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}A,



(ΦA,i,j,n⊙ΦA,i,j,n−1)∗​(evj,n∗​A⊙evi,n∗​A)\displaystyle\left(\Phi_{A,i,j,n}\odot\Phi_{A,i,j,n}^{-1}\right)^{*}\!\left(\ev_{j,n}^{*}A\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}A\right)
=evi,n∗​A⊙evj,n∗​A.\displaystyle=\ev_{i,n}^{*}A\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}A.







Theorem 8.3 together with Lemma 7.2 implies the identities:





ΦA,i,n∗​(evi,n∗​A)\displaystyle\Phi_{A,i,n}^{*}\left(\ev_{i,n}^{*}A\right)
=ev⁡(0)n∗​A,\displaystyle=\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A,



ΦA,i,j,n∗​(evj,n∗​A)\displaystyle\Phi_{A,i,j,n}^{*}\left(\ev_{j,n}^{*}A\right)
=evi,n∗​A,\displaystyle=\ev_{i,n}^{*}A,



if AA is flat.
Hence, Theorem 8.3 shows that the isomorphism ΦA,i,n\Phi_{A,i,n}, resp. ΦA,i,j,n\Phi_{A,i,j,n}, intertwines the connections evi,n∗​A\ev_{i,n}^{*}A and ev⁡(0)n∗​A\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A, resp. evj,n∗​P\ev_{j,n}^{*}P and evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P.





8.3. Iterated Chen-type integrals




I consider now a differential form ω\omega on MM with values in the associated bundle ad⁡P=P×G𝔤\ad P=P\times_{G}\mathfrak{g} of PP, where 𝔤\mathfrak{g}, the Lie algebra of GG, becomes a representation of GG w.r.t. the adjoint action; by the same label I denote the unique basic form on PP with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g} corresponding to ω\omega.
One can take the pull-back of ω\omega w.r.t. the bundle map evi,n~\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}} and, as a result, one gets a basic form on evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P with values in the Lie algebra 𝔤\mathfrak{g}, which is denoted (by abuse of notations) by evi,n∗​ω\ev_{i,n}^{*}\omega.
Taking then the pull-back of evi,n∗​ω\ev_{i,n}^{*}\omega w.r.t. ΦA,i,n\Phi_{A,i,n}, one gets a basic form on ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g}, which in turn descends to a form on L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} with values in the associated bundle ad⁡ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ad\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P.
Denote the result of these three operations by ω^A,i,n\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n}, for 1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n.
In other words, as a basic form on ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g}, one has





ω^A,i,n:=(evi,n~∘ΦA,i,n)∗​ω.\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n}\colon=\left(\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\circ\Phi_{A,i,n}\right)^{*}\omega.



I want now to analyze in detail, using the tools developed in the previous sections about the isomorphisms induced from parallel transport, the properties of the pulled-back forms ω^A,i,n\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n}, i.e. its dependence on the space of connections 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P}, which may be summarised in the following



Proposition 8.4.




i) 



The assignment






𝒜P×Ω∗​(M,ad⁡P)∋(A,ω)​↦Ψ0,i,n\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{P}\times\Omega^{*}\!\left(M,\ad P\right)\ni(A,\omega)\overset{\Psi_{0,i,n}}{\mapsto}



↦Ψ0,i,n\displaystyle\overset{\Psi_{0,i,n}}{\mapsto}
(ev⁡(0)n∗​A,ω^A,i,n)∈𝒜ev⁡(0)n∗​P×Ω∗​(L​M×△n,ad⁡ev⁡(0)n∗​P)\displaystyle(\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A,\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n})\in\mathcal{A}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P}\times\Omega^{*}\!\left(\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n},\ad\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\right)



is 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}-equivariant; the gauge group 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} operates on the space of forms on L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} with values in the associated bundle ad⁡ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ad\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P by composing the action of 𝒢ev⁡(0)n∗​P\mathcal{G}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P} with the group homomorphism from 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} to 𝒢ev⁡(0)n∗​P\mathcal{G}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P} described in Lemma 6.1 of Section 6.


ii) 



There is a natural map from 𝒜P×Ω∗​(M,ad⁡P)\mathcal{A}_{P}\times\Omega^{*}\!\left(M,\ad P\right) to Ω∗​(M,ad⁡P)\Omega^{*}\!\left(M,\ad P\right), resp. from the product 𝒜ev⁡(0)n∗​P×Ω∗​(L​M×△n,ad⁡ev⁡(0)n∗​P)\mathcal{A}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P}\times\Omega^{*}\!\left(\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n},\ad\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\right) to Ω∗​(L​M×△n,ad⁡ev⁡(0)n∗​P)\Omega^{*}\!\left(\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n},\ad\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\right), given by





(A,ω)​↦c​o​vP​dA​ω,resp.(B,η)​↦c​o​vev⁡(0)n∗​P​dB​η.(A,\omega)\overset{\mathnormal{cov}_{P}}{\mapsto}\mathrm{d}_{A}\omega,\quad\text{resp.}\quad(B,\eta)\overset{\mathnormal{cov}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P}}{\mapsto}\mathrm{d}_{B}\eta.



Restricting to the subspace F−1​({0})F^{-1}(\left\{0\right\}) of flat connections, the following diagram commutes:





F−1​({0})×Ω∗​(M,ad⁡P)→Ψ0,i,nF−1​({0})×Ω∗​(L​M×△n,ad⁡ev⁡(0)n∗​P)pr𝒜P×c​o​vP↓↓pr𝒜ev⁡(0)n∗​P×c​o​vev⁡(0)n∗​PF−1​({0})×Ω∗​(M,ad⁡P)→Ψ0,i,nF−1​({0})×Ω∗​(L​M×△n,ad⁡ev⁡(0)n∗​P).\begin{CD}F^{-1}(\left\{0\right\})\times\Omega^{*}\!\left(M,\ad P\right)@>{\Psi_{0,i,n}}>{}>F^{-1}(\left\{0\right\})\times\Omega^{*}\!\left(\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n},\ad\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\right)\\
@V{\pr_{\mathcal{A}_{P}}\times\mathnormal{cov}_{P}}V{}V@V{}V{\pr_{\mathcal{A}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P}}\times\mathnormal{cov}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P}}V\\
F^{-1}(\left\{0\right\})\times\Omega^{*}\!\left(M,\ad P\right)@>{\Psi_{0,i,n}}>{}>F^{-1}(\left\{0\right\})\times\Omega^{*}\!\left(\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n},\ad\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\right)\end{CD}\quad.






As a consequence of the commutativity of the previous diagram, one can consider the “cohomology bundle” H⁡(P,𝔤)\coh\!(P,\mathfrak{g}) over the moduli space of (irreducible) flat connections, whose fibre at a class [A][A] consists of all cohomology classes with values in the associated bundle ad⁡P\ad P at any degree of the covariant differential dA\mathrm{d}_{A}, and the “cohomology bundle” H⁡(ev⁡(0)n∗​P,𝔤)\coh\!(\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P,\mathfrak{g}), with fibre at [B][B] consisting of cohomology classes with values in the associated bundle ad⁡ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ad\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P w.r.t. the covariant differential dB\mathrm{d}_{B}; then, the map Ψ0,i,n\Psi_{0,i,n} descends to a bundle morphism between the cohomology bundles.
(To be more precise, the cohomology bundles may be defined over any open subset of ℳP\mathcal{M}_{P}, where the dimensions of the aforementioned “covariant” cohomology groups are constant; therefore, one has to consider a family of cohomology bundles over all such open sets, where they make sense.)






Proof.



The gauge group 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} of PP defines by pull-back a right action on Ω∗​(M,ad⁡P)\Omega^{*}\left(M,\ad P\right), viewed as the space of basic differential forms on PP with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g}:





ωσ:=σ∗​(ω),σ∈𝒢\omega^{\sigma}\colon=\sigma^{*}\!\left(\omega\right),\quad\sigma\in\mathcal{G}



Proposition 6.3 implies, using again the same notations of Section 6,





Ψ0,i,n​((A,ω)σ)\displaystyle\Psi_{0,i,n}\left((A,\omega)^{\sigma}\right)
=ωσ^Aσ,i,n=\displaystyle=\widehat{\omega^{\sigma}}_{A^{\sigma},i,n}=




=(σ∘evi,n~∘ΦAσ,i,n)∗​ω=\displaystyle=\left(\sigma\circ\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\circ\Phi_{A^{\sigma},i,n}\right)^{*}\omega=




=(evi,n~∘σevi,n∘ΦAσ,i,n)∗​ω=\displaystyle=\left(\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\circ\sigma_{\ev_{i,n}}\circ\Phi_{A^{\sigma},i,n}\right)^{*}\omega=




=(evi,n~∘ΦA,i,n∘σev⁡(0)n)∗​ω=\displaystyle=\left(\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\circ\Phi_{A,i,n}\circ\sigma_{\ev(0)_{n}}\right)^{*}\omega=




=ω^A,i,nσev⁡(0)n=\displaystyle=\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n}^{\sigma_{\ev(0)_{n}}}=




=(Ψ0,i,n​(A,ω))σev⁡(0)n,\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{0,i,n}(A,\omega)\right)^{\sigma_{\ev(0)_{n}}},



from which the first assertion of the Proposition follows.




As for the second assertion, assume the connection AA on PP to be flat; then one gets:





(c​o​vev⁡(0)n∗​P∘Ψ0,i,n)​(A,ω)\displaystyle\left(\mathnormal{cov}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P}\circ\Psi_{0,i,n}\right)\!(A,\omega)
=dev⁡(0)n∗​A​ω^A,i,n=\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A}\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n}=




=d​ω^A,i,n+[(ev⁡(0)n∗​A),ω^A,i,n]=\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n}+{\left[{\,{\left(\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A\right)}\,,\,{\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n}}\,}\right]}=




=d​ω^A,i,n+[(ev⁡(0)n∗​A),(ΦA,i,n∘evi,n~)∗​ω]=\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathrm{d}\omega}_{A,i,n}+{\left[{\,{\left(\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A\right)}\,,\,{\left(\Phi_{A,i,n}\circ\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\right)^{*}\omega}\,}\right]}=




=d​ω^A,i,n+(ΦA,i,n∘evi,n~)∗​[A,ω]=\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathrm{d}\omega}_{A,i,n}+\left(\Phi_{A,i,n}\circ\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\right)^{*}{\left[{\,{A}\,,\,{\omega}\,}\right]}=




=dA​ω^A,i,n=\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathrm{d}_{A}\omega}_{A,i,n}=




=(pr2∘Ψ0,i,n∘(pr𝒜P×c​o​vP))​(A,ω),\displaystyle=\left(\pr_{2}\circ\Psi_{0,i,n}\circ(\pr_{\mathcal{A}_{P}}\times\mathnormal{cov}_{P})\right)\!(A,\omega),



where pr2\pr_{2} is the projection onto the second factor of 𝒜ev⁡(0)n∗​P\mathcal{A}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P} with the forms on L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} with values in the associated bundle End⁡(V)\End(V).
The third equality is a direct consequence of Theorem 8.3, if AA is flat.
∎





Let me state and prove a Lemma useful for explicit computations.



Lemma 8.5.



For any connection AA in 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P}, for any basic form ω\omega on PP with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g} and for any index 1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n, the following formula holds:





pr1∗(ω^A,i,n)=Ad((H(A)|0i)−1)pr2∗(evi,n∗ω),\pr_{1}^{*}\!\left(\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n}\right)=\Ad\!\left((\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{i})^{-1}\right)\pr_{2}^{*}\!\left(\ev_{i,n}^{*}\omega\right),



where prj\pr_{j} denotes the jj-projection from ev⁡(0)n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P onto its jj-factor, for j=1,2j=1,2.




Proof.



Assuming ω\omega to be of degree rr, consider rr tangent vectors Xj:=(Xγj,X1j,…,Xpj,Xqj)X^{j}\colon=\left(X_{\gamma}^{j};X_{1}^{j},\dots;X_{p}^{j},X_{q}^{j}\right) to ev⁡(0)n∗​P⊙evi,n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{i,n}^{*}P at a general point.




Thus, by definition of pull-back, one has





pr1∗​(ω^A,i,n)(γ,t1,…,p,q)​(X1,…)\displaystyle\pr_{1}^{*}\!\left(\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n}\right)_{\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots;p,q\right)}\!\left(X^{1},\dots\right)
=(ω^A,i,n)(γ,t1,…,p)​(T(γ,t1,…,p,q)⁡pr1⁡(X1),…)=\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n}\right)_{\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots;p\right)}\!\left(\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots;p,q\right)}{\pr_{1}}\!\left(X^{1}\right),\dots\right)=




=ωq​H⁡(A,γ,ti,p,q)​(T(γ,t1,…,p,q)⁡(evi,n~∘ΦA,i,n∘pr1)​(X1)CLOSE,\displaystyle=\omega_{q\partr\!\left(A;\gamma;t_{i};p,q\right)}\!\left(\tange\!_{\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots;p,q\right)}{\left(\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\circ\Phi_{A,i,n}\circ\pr_{1}\right)}\!\left(X^{1}\right),\right.

Since ω\omega is basic, it follows

(evi,n~∘pr2)∗​ω(γ,t1,…,p,q)​(X1,…),\displaystyle\phantom{=\quad}\left(\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\circ\pr_{2}\right)^{*}\omega_{\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots;p,q\right)}\!\left(X^{1},\dots\right),



where in the first identity I used the horizontality of ω\omega to get rid of the vertical parts of any argument, and in the second identity I used the GG-equivariance of ω\omega.
∎




Remark 8.6.



The formula displayed in Lemma 8.5 simplifies remarkably under the assumption PP trivial; it gives in this case the well-known formula used in [4] for the construction of the generalized holonomy.





On the other hand, as was already proved in Proposition 8.2, the parallel transport map H(A)|ij\partr(A)\arrowvert_{i}^{j}, for a given connection AA on PP, defines an isomorphism between the bundles evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P and evj,n∗​P\ev_{j,n}^{*}P; thus, taking a basic differential form ω\omega on PP with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g}, representing a form on MM with values in the bundle ad⁡P\ad P, one can take its pull-backs w.r.t. evi,n~\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}} and evj,n~\widetilde{\ev_{j,n}} respectively, which is denoted by evi,n∗​ω\ev_{i,n}^{*}\omega and evj,n∗​ω\ev_{j,n}^{*}\omega.
Taking now evj,n∗​ω\ev_{j,n}^{*}\omega as a basic form on evj,n∗​P\ev_{j,n}^{*}P with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g}, one can consider its pull-back w.r.t. the isomorphism ΦA,i,j,n\Phi_{A,i,j,n}, induced by the parallel transport map H(A)|ij\partr(A)\arrowvert_{i}^{j}, thus one has a basic form on evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g}, which is denoted by





ω^A,i,j,n:=(evj,n~∘ΦA,i,j,n)∗​ω.\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,j,n}\colon=\left(\widetilde{\ev_{j,n}}\circ\Phi_{A,i,j,n}\right)^{*}\omega.



Is there some formula, similar to that displayed in Lemma 8.5, relating ω^A,i,j,n\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,j,n} to evj,n∗​ω\ev_{j,n}^{*}\omega?
This is encoded in the following



Lemma 8.7.



For any connection AA in 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P}, for any basic form ω\omega on PP with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g} and for any two indices 1≤i<j≤n1\leq i<j\leq n, the following formula holds:





pr1∗(ω^A,i,j,n)=Ad((H(A)|ij)−1)pr2∗(evj,n∗ω),\pr_{1}^{*}\!\left(\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,j,n}\right)=\Ad\!\left((\partr(A)\arrowvert_{i}^{j})^{-1}\right)\pr_{2}^{*}\!\left(\ev_{j,n}^{*}\omega\right),



where prk\pr_{k} denotes the kk-projection from evi,n∗​P⊙evj,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P\odot\ev_{j,n}^{*}P onto its kk-factor, for k=1,2k=1,2.




Proof.



The proof is similar to that of Lemma 8.5; one only has to change the notations accordingly.
∎






8.4. Restrictions to boundary faces of simplices




I need now some brief comments about the isomorphisms ΦA,i,n\Phi_{A,i,n} and their restrictions on the faces of the simplices △n\triangle_{n}; in fact, the following computations will be used later.




Denoting by ια,n\iota_{\alpha,n}, for 0≤α≤n0\leq\alpha\leq n, the inclusion of L​M×△n−1\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n-1} into L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} given by





ια,n​(γ,t1,…,tn−1):={(γ,0,t1,…,tn−1),α=0(γ,t1,…,tα,tα,…,tn−1),1≤α≤n−1(γ,t1,…,tn−1,1),α=n,\iota_{\alpha,n}\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1}\right)\colon=\begin{cases}\left(\gamma;0,t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1}\right),&\alpha=0\\
\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha},\dots,t_{n-1}\right),&1\leq\alpha\leq n-1\\
\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1},1\right),&\alpha=n,\end{cases}



let me also denote by ι~α,n\widetilde{\iota}_{\alpha,n} the natural map from evi,n−1∗​P\ev_{i,n-1}^{*}P into evi,n∗​P\ev_{i,n}^{*}P.




Now, given two manifolds MM and NN, a principal GG-bundle PP over NN and a smooth map ff from MM to NN, if one takes the pull-back of any form on NN with values in the associated bundle P×GVP\times_{G}V of PP, for some representation (ρ,V)\left(\rho,V\right) of GG, the resulting form on MM takes its values in the pull-back bundle f∗​(P×GV)f^{*}\left(P\times_{G}V\right), which is canonically isomorphic to f∗​P×GVf^{*}P\times_{G}V.
Denoting by f~\widetilde{f} the natural map from f∗​Pf^{*}P to PP, by ω\omega a general form on NN with values in the associated bundle P×GVP\times_{G}V, it is well-known that the basic form on f∗​Pf^{*}P with values in VV corresponding to f∗​ωf^{*}\omega is simply the pull-back w.r.t. f~\widetilde{f} of the basic form on PP with values in VV corresponding to ω\omega.




Therefore, in order to compute the result of the restriction by ια,n\iota_{\alpha,n} of the form ω^A,i,n\widehat{\omega}_{A,i,n} as a form on L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} with values in ev⁡(0)n∗​P×G𝔤\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\times_{G}\mathfrak{g}, it suffices to compute the result of the following compositions of bundle maps, which is very easy; one can therefore write down only the results:




(8.2)

evi,n~∘ΦA,i,n∘ι~0,n\displaystyle\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\circ\Phi_{A,i,n}\circ\widetilde{\iota}_{0,n}
={ev⁡(0)n−1~,i=1evi−1,n−1~∘ΦA,i−1,n−1,2≤i≤n,\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\ev(0)_{n-1}},&i=1\\
\widetilde{\ev_{i-1,n-1}}\circ\Phi_{A,i-1,n-1},&2\leq i\leq n\end{cases},



evi,n~∘ΦA,i,n∘ι~α,n\displaystyle\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\circ\Phi_{A,i,n}\circ\widetilde{\iota}_{\alpha,n}
={evi,n−1~∘ΦA,i,n−1,1≤i≤αevα,n−1~∘ΦA,α,n−1,i=α+1evi−1,n−1~∘ΦA,i−1,n−1,α+2≤i≤n,\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\ev_{i,n-1}}\circ\Phi_{A,i,n-1},&1\leq i\leq\alpha\\
\widetilde{\ev_{\alpha,n-1}}\circ\Phi_{A,\alpha,n-1},&i=\alpha+1\\
\widetilde{\ev_{i-1,n-1}}\circ\Phi_{A,i-1,n-1},&\alpha+2\leq i\leq n\\
\end{cases},



evi,n~∘ΦA,i,n∘ι~n,n\displaystyle\widetilde{\ev_{i,n}}\circ\Phi_{A,i,n}\circ\widetilde{\iota}_{n,n}
={evi,n−1~∘ΦA,i,n−1,1<i≤n−1ev⁡(0)~∘ΦA∘π~n,i=1.\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\ev_{i,n-1}}\circ\Phi_{A,i,n-1},&1<i\leq n-1\\
\widetilde{\ev(0)}\circ\Phi_{A}\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{n},&i=1.\end{cases}







8.5. The generalized holonomy and Chen’s iterated integrals




Finally, let me define, for a general principal bundle PP over MM, the so-called generalized holonomy via Chen’s iterated integrals.




First of all, consider so-called B​FBF-couples, i.e.  assuming the manifold MM to be of dimension mm, consider a general couple (A,B)(A,B), where OPENi)i) AA is a connection on the principal bundle PP and OPENi​i)ii) BB is a basic form on PP with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g} of any degree; equivalently, BB is a form on MM of degree with values in the adjoint bundle ad⁡P\ad P.
(For applications in TQFT, especially higher-dimensional B​FBF theories, I will restrict the degree of BB to m−2m-2, mm being the dimension of the manifold MM.)
Take additionally some representation (V,ρ)(V,\rho) of the structure group GG; the derivative at the identity of the Lie group morphism ρ\rho defines a Lie algebra representation of 𝔤\mathfrak{g} in VV, which is denoted by ρ^\widehat{\rho}.




For any positive integer nn and 1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n, define the following differential form:





B^A,i,n,ρ:=ρ^​(B^A,i,n),\widehat{B}_{A,i,n,\rho}:=\widehat{\rho}\!\left(\widehat{B}_{A,i,n}\right),



borrowing the notations from Subsection 8.3.
Hence, for any choice of indices nn (and ii), B^A,i,n,ρ\widehat{B}_{A,i,n,\rho} defines a basic differential form on the pulled-back bundle ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P with values in the Lie algebra of endomorphisms of VV; equivalently, it may be viewed as a differential form on L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} with values in the associated bundle ev⁡(0)n∗​P×GEnd⁡(V)\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\times_{G}\End(V), where GG acts on End⁡(V)\End(V) by a twisted conjugation w.r.t. ρ\rho.
If one considers then the expression on ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P with values in End⁡(V)\End(V) given by





(B^A,1,n,ρ∧⋯∧B^A,n,n,ρ)ρ(H(A)|01)−1,\left(\widehat{B}_{A,1,n,\rho}\land\cdots\land\widehat{B}_{A,n,n,\rho}\right)\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1},



where the wedge product means not only the usual wedge product of the form-piece but also multiplication in the ring End⁡(V)\End(V), it is easy to verify that it defines also a basic form on ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P with values in End⁡(V)\End(V) of degree n​deg⁡Bn\deg B; thus, its trace in the endomorphism ring End⁡(V)\End(V) defines a basic differential form on ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P of degree n⁡(m−2)n(m-2), hence a differential form on L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n}.
Integrating this differential form along the fiber w.r.t. the projection from L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} onto L​M\mathrm{L}M, forgetting the simplex, one gets a differential form on the space of loops L​M\mathrm{L}M of degree n⁡(m−3)n(m-3).



Definition 8.8.



Define the generalized Wilson loop w.r.t. the B​FBF-pair (A,B)(A,B) in the representation ρ\rho as the formal series




(8.3)

𝒲ρ(A,B):=Wρ(A)+∑n≥1πn∗TrV[(B^A,1,n,ρ∧⋯∧B^A,n,n,ρ)ρ(H(A)|01)−1],\mathcal{W}_{\rho}(A,B):=W_{\rho}(A)+\sum_{n\geq 1}\pi_{n*}\tr_{V}\left[\left(\widehat{B}_{A,1,n,\rho}\land\cdots\land\widehat{B}_{A,n,n,\rho}\right)\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right],



where Wρ​(A)W_{\rho}(A) denotes the Wilson loop function introduced in Subsubsection 6.0.1 and by πn∗\pi_{n*} is denoted the push-forward (or integration along the fiber) w.r.t. the forgetful projection from L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} onto L​M\mathrm{L}M.




Remark 8.9.



Restricting to B​FBF-pairs with deg⁡B=m−2\deg B=m-2, the generalized Wilson loop 𝒲ρ​(A,B)\mathcal{W}_{\rho}(A,B) is a formal series, whose terms are differential forms of increasing degree, with the exception of the case m=1,2m=1,2, where all push-forwards vanish, because the degree of any integrand is strictly smaller than the dimension of the fiber along which one integrates the terms of the generalized Wilson loop up to order 11, hence reducing it to the usual Wilson loop.
The dimension m=3m=3 presents a different phenomenon: namely, since the degree of the summand indexed by the positive integer nn of the generalized Wilson loop is n⁡(m−3)n(m-3), if m=3m=3, the degree of the aforementioned summand is 00, hence for any summand.
Thus, the generalized Wilson loop 𝒲ρ​(A,B)\mathcal{W}_{\rho}(A,B) defines, for every 33-dimensional manifold MM, a formal function on the space of loops L​M\mathrm{L}M.





Now, recall that the gauge group 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} operates from the right on the space of connections 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P} on PP and on basic forms on PP with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g} by pull-back; therefore, 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} operates also on the space of B​FBF-pairs (A,B)(A,B) from the right.
There is also an operation of Ωdeg⁡B−1​(M,ad⁡P)\Omega^{\deg B-1}(M,\ad P) on the space of B​FBF-pairs, namely





((A,B),τ)↦(A,B+dA​τ).\left((A,B),\tau\right)\mapsto(A,B+\mathrm{d}_{A}\tau).



It turns out that both actions, of 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P} and of Ωdeg⁡B−1​(M,ad⁡P)\Omega^{\deg B-1}(M,\ad P), combine into a unique right action of the semidirect product group 𝒢P⋉Ωdeg⁡B−1​(M,ad⁡P)\mathcal{G}_{P}\ltimes\Omega^{\deg B-1}(M,\ad P):





((A,B),(σ,τ))↦(Aσ,Bσ+dAσ​τ).\left((A,B),(\sigma,\tau)\right)\mapsto(A^{\sigma},B^{\sigma}+\mathrm{d}_{A^{\sigma}}\tau).





Remark 8.10.



Before entering into the details (and under the assumption deg⁡B=m−2\deg B=m-2, let me point out another peculiarity of the case m=3m=3: the space of connections 𝒜P\mathcal{A}_{P} is an affine space modelled on the space of basic 11-forms on PP with values in 𝔤\mathfrak{g}.
Hence, a B​FBF-pair (A,B)(A,B) in dimension 33 may be also viewed as a connection on PP, decomposed w.r.t. to the chosen “background” connection AA.
Notice that the connection A+BA+B, corresponding to the B​FBF-pair (A,B)(A,B), is flat, assuming AA flat, if and only if BB satisfies the AA-covariant Maurer–Cartan Equation





dA​B+12​[B,B]=0;\mathrm{d}_{A}B+\frac{1}{2}{\left[{\,{B}\,,\,{B}\,}\right]}=0;



this identity will play a key-rôle in subsequent computations.





The following theorem contains a list of the most important properties of the generalized Wilson loop 𝒲ρ​(A,B)\mathcal{W}_{\rho}(A,B).



Theorem 8.11.



The following properties hold for the generalized Wilson loop function 𝒲ρ\mathcal{W}_{\rho}:


a) 



in any dimension mm, the map 𝒲ρ\mathcal{W}_{\rho}, assigning to any B​FBF-pair (A,B)(A,B) the generalized Wilson loop 𝒲ρ​(A,B)\mathcal{W}_{\rho}(A,B), is invariant w.r.t. the action of the gauge group 𝒢P\mathcal{G}_{P}:





𝒲ρ​((A,B)σ)=𝒲ρ​(A,B),∀σ∈𝒢P.\mathcal{W}_{\rho}\left((A,B)^{\sigma}\right)=\mathcal{W}_{\rho}(A,B),\quad\forall\sigma\in\mathcal{G}_{P}.




b) 



If one considers the subspace of B​FBF-pairs obeying




(8.4)

FA=0,dA​B=0,F_{A}=0,\quad\mathrm{d}_{A}B=0,



then the corresponding generalized Wilson loop 𝒲ρ​(A,B)\mathcal{W}_{\rho}(A,B) is a closed form, assuming deg⁡B\deg B to be odd and such that 2​deg⁡B>m2\deg B>m; assuming deg⁡B\deg B even and such that 2​deg⁡B>m2\deg B>m, then the form made only of the oddly-indexed summands in the generalized Wilson loop is a closed form.


c) 



Assuming deg⁡B=1\deg B=1 and the background connection AA to be flat and BB obeys the AA-covariant Maurer–Cartan Equation, then the generalized Wilson loop is a locally constant function on the space of loops in MM.


d) 



If one considers the space of B​FBF-pairs (A,B)(A,B) obeying Equation (8.4), then the infinitesimal action of Ωdeg⁡B−1​(M,ad⁡P)\Omega^{\deg B-1}(M,\ad P) changes the generalized Wilson loop 𝒲ρ​(A,B)\mathcal{W}_{\rho}(A,B) by an exact form, assuming 2​deg⁡B>m+12\deg B>m+1.






Proof.




a) 



It suffices to show the claim for any summand of the generalized Wilson loop; for this purpose, let me introduce the shorthand notation





𝒲ρ,n(A,B):=πn∗TrV[(B^A,1,n,ρ∧⋯∧B^A,n,n,ρ)ρ(H(A)|01)−1].\mathcal{W}_{\rho,n}(A,B)\colon=\pi_{n*}\tr_{V}\left[\left(\widehat{B}_{A,1,n,\rho}\land\cdots\land\widehat{B}_{A,n,n,\rho}\right)\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right].



Now, recall Part OPENi)i) of Proposition 8.4 and that, in the notation used there,





𝒲ρ,n(A,B)=πn∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧Ψ0,n,n(A,B)ρρ(H(A)|01)−1],\mathcal{W}_{\rho,n}(A,B)=\pi_{n*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\Psi_{0,n,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right],



where the subscript ρ\rho denotes composition with the tangent map at the identity of the homomorphism ρ\rho; by abuse of notation, I have written





Ψ0,i,n​(A,B)\Psi_{0,i,n}(A,B)



instead of





pr2⁡(Ψ0,i,n​(A,B)),\pr_{2}\!\left(\Psi_{0,i,n}(A,B)\right),



where pr2\pr_{2} denotes the projection onto the second factor.
Therefore, it holds





𝒲ρ,n​((A,B)σ)\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\rho,n}\!\left((A,B)^{\sigma}\right)
=πn∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n((A,B)σ)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(Aσ)|01)−1]=\displaystyle=\pi_{n*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}\!\left((A,B)^{\sigma}\right)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A^{\sigma})\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]=




=πn∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n((A,B))ρσev⁡(0)n∧⋯∧ρ(H(Aσ)|01)−1]=\displaystyle=\pi_{n*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}\!\left((A,B)\right)_{\rho}^{\sigma_{\ev(0)_{n}}}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A^{\sigma})\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]=




=𝒲ρ,n​(A,B),\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}_{\rho,n}(A,B),



where I made use of the fact that a general gauge transformation operates on a basic form by conjugation, because of the horizontality and of the equivariance, of Proposition 6.3 of Section 6 and of the cyclicity of the trace.


b) 



First of all, notice that, under the assumption 2​deg⁡B>m2\deg B>m, the form Bρ∧BρB_{\rho}\land B_{\rho}, which has obviously degree 2​deg⁡B2\deg B vanishes due to dimensional reason.




Now, I compute exemplarily the exterior derivative of the nn-th summand 𝒲ρ,n​(A,B)\mathcal{W}_{\rho,n}(A,B) of the generalized Wilson loop; for this purpose, I need the generalized Stokes’ Theorem (see [4] for more details and to the Appendix for the orientation choices for the nn-th simplex):





d​𝒲ρ,n​(A,B)\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{W}_{\rho,n}(A,B)
=(−1)nπn∗dTrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]−\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\pi_{n*}\mathrm{d}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]-




−(−1)nπ∂n∗ι∂n∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]=\displaystyle\phantom{=}-(-1)^{n}\pi_{\partial_{n}*}\iota_{\partial_{n}}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]=




=(−1)nπn∗TrV{dev⁡(0)n∗​Aρ[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]}−\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\pi_{n*}\tr_{V}\left\{\mathrm{d}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A_{\rho}}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]\right\}-




−(−1)n∑α=0n(−1)α+1πn−1∗ια,n∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧\displaystyle\phantom{=}-(-1)^{n}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}(-1)^{\alpha+1}\pi_{n-1*}\iota_{\alpha,n}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\right.




∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]=\displaystyle\phantom{=}\left.\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]=




=∑i=1n(−1)n+(i−1)​(m−2)πn∗TrV[⋯∧dev⁡(0)n∗​AρΨ0,i,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{n+(i-1)(m-2)}\pi_{n*}\tr_{V}\left[\cdots\land\mathrm{d}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A_{\rho}}\Psi_{0,i,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\right.




∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]+\displaystyle\phantom{=}\left.\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]+




+(−1)n+n⁡(m−2)πn∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧dev⁡(0)n∗​Aρρ(H(A)|01)−1]−\displaystyle\phantom{=}+(-1)^{n+n(m-2)}\pi_{n*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\mathrm{d}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A_{\rho}}\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]-




−∑α=0n(−1)n+α+1πn−1∗ια,n∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]=\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}(-1)^{n+\alpha+1}\pi_{n-1*}\iota_{\alpha,n}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]=




=∑i=1n(−1)n+(i−1)​(m−2)πn∗TrV[⋯∧dA​B^A,i,n,ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]−\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{n+(i-1)(m-2)}\pi_{n*}\tr_{V}\left[\cdots\land\widehat{\mathrm{d}_{A}B}_{A,i,n,\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]-




−∑α=0n(−1)n+α+1πn−1∗ια,n∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]=\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}(-1)^{n+\alpha+1}\pi_{n-1*}\iota_{\alpha,n}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]=




=−∑α=0n(−1)n+α+1πn−1∗ια,n∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1],\displaystyle=-\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}(-1)^{n+\alpha+1}\pi_{n-1*}\iota_{\alpha,n}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right],



where I made use of Part OPENi​i)ii) of Proposition 6.3 of Section 6 and of Lemma 7.7 of Section 7, together with Equation (8.4.
By the notation dev⁡(0)n∗​Aρ\mathrm{d}_{\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A_{\rho}} is meant the covariant derivative on forms on ev⁡(0)n∗​P\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P with values in the associated bundle ev⁡(0)n∗​P×GEnd⁡(V)\ev(0)_{n}^{*}P\times_{G}\End(V) obtained by twisting the pull-back connection ev⁡(0)n∗​A\ev(0)_{n}^{*}A by the Lie-algebra representation ρ^\widehat{\rho}, the tangent map at the identity of ρ\rho.




It therefore remains only to compute the boundary contributions; for this purpose, I will make use of the set of equations (8.2).
In fact, one gets the following three types of boundary contributions:





ι0,n∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]\displaystyle\iota_{0,n}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]
=TrV[ev⁡(0)n~∗Bρ∧Ψ0,1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧\displaystyle=\tr_{V}\left[\widetilde{\ev(0)_{n}}^{*}B_{\rho}\land\Psi_{0,1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\right.




∧⋯∧Ψ0,n−1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1];\displaystyle\phantom{=}\left.\land\cdots\land\Psi_{0,n-1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right];



ια,n∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]\displaystyle\iota_{\alpha,n}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]
=TrV[Ψ0,1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧\displaystyle=\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\right.




∧(Bρ∧Bρ)^A,α,n−1∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1],\displaystyle\phantom{=}\left.\land\widehat{\left(B_{\rho}\land B_{\rho}\right)}_{A,\alpha,n-1}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right],




1≤α≤n−1;\displaystyle\phantom{=}1\leq\alpha\leq n-1;



ιn,n∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]\displaystyle\iota_{n,n}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]
=TrV[Ψ0,1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧⋯\displaystyle=\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\right.




∧Ψ0,n−1,n−1​(A,B)ρ∧\displaystyle\phantom{=}\left.\land\Psi_{0,n-1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\right.




∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1∧ev⁡(0)n~∗Bρ].\displaystyle\phantom{=}\left.\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\land\widetilde{\ev(0)_{n}}^{*}B_{\rho}\right].



If 2​deg⁡B>m2\deg B>m, the right-hand side of the second term in the above identities vanishes, for any index 1≤α≤n−11\leq\alpha\leq n-1.
Thus, one has only to compare the right-hand sides of the first and the last term; for this purpose, I make use of the cyclicity of the trace, so as to get:





−∑α=0n(−1)n+α+1πn−1∗ια,n∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]==(−1)nπn−1∗TrV[ev⁡(0)n~∗Bρ∧Ψ0,1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧∧⋯∧Ψ0,n−1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]++TrV[Ψ0,1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧Ψ0,n−1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1∧ev⁡(0)n~∗Bρ]==[(−1)n+(−1)(n−1)​(m−2)]πn−1∗TrV[ev⁡(0)n~∗Bρ∧Ψ0,1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧∧⋯∧Ψ0,n−1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1].-\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}(-1)^{n+\alpha+1}\pi_{n-1*}\iota_{\alpha,n}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]=\\
=(-1)^{n}\pi_{n-1*}\tr_{V}\left[\widetilde{\ev(0)_{n}}^{*}B_{\rho}\land\Psi_{0,1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\right.\\
\phantom{=}\left.\land\cdots\land\Psi_{0,n-1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]+\\
+\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\Psi_{0,n-1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\right.\\
\left.\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\land\widetilde{\ev(0)_{n}}^{*}B_{\rho}\right]=\\
=\left[(-1)^{n}+(-1)^{(n-1)(m-2)}\right]\pi_{n-1*}\tr_{V}\left[\widetilde{\ev(0)_{n}}^{*}B_{\rho}\land\Psi_{0,1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\right.\\
\phantom{=}\left.\land\cdots\land\Psi_{0,n-1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right].



Assuming additionally that deg⁡B\deg B is odd, the sum of sign terms before the last expression in the above chain of equalities vanishes, hence yielding the claim for deg⁡B\deg B odd.
On the other hand, if mm is even, the aforementioned sum of sign terms equals, for a general nn,





(−1)n+1,(-1)^{n}+1,



which vanishes if and only if nn is odd.
Thus, in even dimensions, the series made of the oddly-indexed summands of the generalized Wilson loop is a closed form, if the B​FBF-pair obeys Equation (8.4).


c) 



The proof of the claim goes in principle as the proof of Part OPENb)b), namely I make use of the generalized Stokes’ Theorem.
Without repeating all the computations made in Part OPENb)b) and assuming the background connection AA to be flat, since deg⁡B=1\deg B=1 is odd, the remaining boundary terms for the nn-th summand of the generalized Wilson loop are






∑i=1n−1(−1)n+iπn−1∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧Bρ∧Bρ^A,i,n−1∧⋯\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{n+i}\pi_{n-1*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\widehat{B_{\rho}\land B_{\rho}}_{A,i,n-1}\land\cdots\right.




∧Ψ0,n−1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]=\displaystyle\left.\land\Psi_{0,n-1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]=




=∑i=1n−1(−1)n+iπn−1∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧Ψ0,i,n−1(A,12[B,B])ρ∧⋯\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{n+i}\pi_{n-1*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\Psi_{0,i,n-1}\!\left(A,\frac{1}{2}{\left[{\,{B}\,,\,{B}\,}\right]}\right)_{\rho}\land\cdots\right.




∧Ψ0,n−1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1],\displaystyle\left.\land\Psi_{0,n-1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right],



where I made use of the fact that BB is a 11-form.




On the other hand, the exterior derivative of the n−1n-1-th summand produces a term containing covariant derivatives w.r.t. AA of BB, which takes the form:






∑i=1n−1(−1)n+iπn−1∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧Ψ0,i,n−1(A,dAB)ρ∧⋯∧\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{n+i}\pi_{n-1*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\Psi_{0,i,n-1}(A,\mathrm{d}_{A}B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\right.




∧Ψ0,n−1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1].\displaystyle\left.\land\Psi_{0,n-1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right].



It is immediate to see that the boundary term coming from the nn-summand and the previous term sum up to give






∑i=1n−1(−1)n+iπn−1∗TrV[Ψ0,1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧⋯∧Ψ0,i,n−1(A,dAB+12[B,B])ρ∧⋯∧\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{n+i}\pi_{n-1*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\Psi_{0,i,n-1}(A,\mathrm{d}_{A}B+\frac{1}{2}{\left[{\,{B}\,,\,{B}\,}\right]})_{\rho}\land\cdots\land\right.




∧Ψ0,n−1,n−1(A,B)ρ∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1],\displaystyle\left.\land\Psi_{0,n-1,n-1}(A,B)_{\rho}\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right],



which vanishes assuming the connection A+BA+B to be flat, whence the claim follows.


d) 



I will show the claim exemplarily for the second summand in the generalized Wilson loop, namely





𝒲2,ρ(A,B)=π1∗TrV[B^A,1,1,ρ∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1].\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho}(A,B)=\pi_{1*}\tr_{V}\left[\widehat{B}_{A,1,1,\rho}\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right].



Assuming the B​FBF-pair to satisfy Equation (8.4), then one has





𝒲2,ρ​(A,B+dA​τ)=𝒲2,ρ​(A,B)+𝒲2,ρ​(A,dA​τ);\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho}(A,B+\mathrm{d}_{A}\tau)=\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho}(A,B)+\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho}(A,\mathrm{d}_{A}\tau);



I want to show that the second term on the right-hand side is d​ddd-exact.
Namely, again by the generalized Stokes’ Theorem, one gets





dπ1∗TrV[Ψ0,1,1(A,τ)∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\pi_{1*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,1}(A,\tau)\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]
=−π1∗dTrV[Ψ0,1,1(A,τ)∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]+\displaystyle=-\pi_{1*}\mathrm{d}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,1}(A,\tau)\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]+




+ι1,1∗TrV[Ψ0,1,1(A,τ)∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]−\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\iota_{1,1}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,1}(A,\tau)\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]-




−ι0,1∗TrV[Ψ0,1,1(A,τ)∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]=\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\iota_{0,1}^{*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,1}(A,\tau)\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]=




=−π1∗TrV[Ψ0,1,1(A,dAτ)∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]+\displaystyle=-\pi_{1*}\tr_{V}\left[\Psi_{0,1,1}(A,\mathrm{d}_{A}\tau)\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]+




+TrV[ρ(H(A)|01)−1∧ev(0)∗τ]−\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\tr_{V}\left[\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\land\ev(0)^{*}\tau\right]-




−TrV[ev(0)∗τ∧ρ(H(A)|01)−1]=\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\tr_{V}\left[\ev(0)^{*}\tau\land\rho(\partr(A)\arrowvert_{0}^{1})^{-1}\right]=




=𝒲2,ρ​(A,dA​τ),\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho}(A,\mathrm{d}_{A}\tau),



by cyclicity of the trace again.
Thus, if the B​FBF-pair obeys Equation (8.4), a variation of the BB-form by an AA-covariant exact form produces a change of the second summand of the generalized Wilson loop, which is itself a closed form, independently of the dimension of mm (as the previous computation shows immediately by repeating it almost verbatim), by a d\mathrm{d}-exact form:





𝒲2,ρ​(A,B+dA​τ)=𝒲2,ρ​(A,B)+d​𝒲~2,ρ​(A,τ).\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho}(A,B+\mathrm{d}_{A}\tau)=\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho}(A,B)+\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2,\rho}(A,\tau).



The second term of the generalized Wilson loop, 𝒲2,ρ​(A,B)\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho}(A,B) is, by the above arguments, a “toy” example of a closed form on the space of loops in MM, because there are no restrictions on the dimension of the manifold MM.




The problems arise when considering the higher summands; the generalized Stokes’ Theorem plays again a fundamental rôle, and one can repeat the arguments used in the proof of Part OPENb)b) almost verbatim, keeping in mind that, due to dimensional reason, the products





Bρ∧τρorτρ∧BρB_{\rho}\land\tau_{\rho}\quad\text{or}\quad\tau_{\rho}\land B_{\rho}



vanish, whenever 2​deg⁡B>m+12\deg B>m+1.




Notice that the fact that I consider the infinitesimal action of Ωdeg⁡B−1​(M,ad⁡P)\Omega^{\deg B-1}(M,\ad P) simplifies considerably the computations, because the action on the nn-th summand decomposes it into a sum of nn contributions, each containing at some place between 11 and nn a term dA​τ\mathrm{d}_{A}\tau instead of a form BB.
Notice also that I have to restrict ourselves to odd dimensions, because of the two boundary contributions coming from the collapse of first coordinate of the simplex to 00 or of the last one to 11: namely, it arises a similar phenomenon as for the last part of the proof of Part OPENb)b).
Similarly, considering also the case of even dimensions, then one has to restrict the generalized Wilson loop only to the summands indexed by odd indices.



∎







Appendix A The orientation of the nnth simplex △n\triangle_{n}




One of the fundamental ingredients in the construction of the generalized Wilson loop of Definition 8.8 of Section 8 is the nn-th simplex △n\triangle_{n}.




In order to compute the push-forward w.r.t. the forgetful projection πn\pi_{n}, I need to specify an orientation of the simplex.
This can be done by choosing an orientation form, which is the restriction to △n\triangle_{n} of the standard orientation form on ℝn{\mathbb{R}}^{n}, i.e.




(A.1)

d​vol△n:=d​t1∧⋯∧d​tn.\mathrm{d}\!\text{vol}_{\triangle_{n}}\colon=\mathrm{d}t_{1}\land\cdots\land\mathrm{d}t_{n}.



Thus, one can integrate nn-forms on △n\triangle_{n} by the rule: if ω=f​d​t1∧⋯∧d​tn\omega=f\mathrm{d}t_{1}\land\cdots\land\mathrm{d}t_{n} is a form of highest degree on △n\triangle_{n}, where ff is a smooth function on △n\triangle_{n}, its integral is given by





∫△nω:=∫01⋯∫0t2f(t1,…,tn)dt1⋯dtn.\int_{\triangle_{n}}\omega\colon=\int_{0}^{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t_{2}}f\left(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{n}.



Clearly, by Fubini’s theorem, one can also write e.g.





∫△nω=∫01∫ti1⋯∫titi+2∫0ti⋯∫0t2f(t1,…,tn)dt1⋯dti−1dti+1⋯dtndti,\int_{\triangle_{n}}\omega=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{t_{i}}^{1}\cdots\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+2}}\int_{0}^{t_{i}}\cdots\int_{0}^{t_{2}}f\left(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{i-1}\mathrm{d}t_{i+1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{n}\mathrm{d}t_{i},



for any 1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n.




Now that a particular orientation of △n\triangle_{n} is specified, one can define the push-forward w.r.t. the projection πn\pi_{n} from L​M×△n\mathrm{L}M\times\triangle_{n} onto L​M\mathrm{L}M as




(A.2)

πn∗[f(γ;t1,…)dvol△n∧πn∗(ω)]:=[∫△nf(γ;t1,…)dvol△n]∧ω,\pi_{n*}\left[f\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots\right)\mathrm{d}\!\text{vol}_{\triangle_{n}}\land\pi_{n}^{*}\left(\omega\right)\right]\colon=\left[\int_{\triangle_{n}}f\left(\gamma;t_{1},\dots\right)\mathrm{d}\!\text{vol}_{\triangle_{n}}\right]\land\omega,



otherwise, I set the push-forward to be 00.
In equation (A.2), ff is a smooth function on L×△n\mathrm{L}\times\triangle_{n}, while ω\omega is a smooth form on L​M\mathrm{L}M.
One can verify directly that equation (A.2) is a special case of our definition of push-forward.




Now, it is not difficult to compute the orientations of n+1n+1 boundary faces of the nn-simplex △n\triangle_{n}, which I used explicitly on the proof of Theorem 8.11 of Section 8.




The boundary ∂△n\partial\triangle_{n} of the simplex can be decomposed into a union of n+1n+1 different sets as follows:





∂△n=⋃α=0n(∂△n)α,\partial\triangle_{n}=\bigcup_{\alpha=0}^{n}\left(\partial\triangle_{n}\right)_{\alpha},



where





(∂△n)0\displaystyle\left(\partial\triangle_{n}\right)_{0}
:={(t1,…,tn)∈△n:t1=0};\displaystyle\colon=\left\{\left(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\right)\in\triangle_{n}\colon t_{1}=0\right\};



(∂△n)α\displaystyle\left(\partial\triangle_{n}\right)_{\alpha}
:={(t1,…,tn)∈△n:tα=tα+1},1≤α≤n−1;\displaystyle\colon=\left\{\left(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\right)\in\triangle_{n}\colon t_{\alpha}=t_{\alpha+1}\right\},\quad 1\leq\alpha\leq n-1;



(∂△n)n\displaystyle\left(\partial\triangle_{n}\right)_{n}
:={(t1,…,tn)∈△n:tn=1}.\displaystyle\colon=\left\{\left(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\right)\in\triangle_{n}\colon t_{n}=1\right\}.



It is clear that any (∂△n)α\left(\partial\triangle_{n}\right)_{\alpha} is isomorphic to the n−1n-1-simplex △n−1\triangle_{n-1}; the orientation form on △n−1\triangle_{n-1} is d​voln−1\mathrm{d}\!\text{vol}_{n-1}.




Clearly, the 00-th boundary face (∂△n)0\left(\partial\triangle_{n}\right)_{0} has −∂∂t1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}} as outward-directed (normalized) vector field (since ti≥0t_{i}\geq 0, for all 1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n).
Since the orientation form of the boundary ∂M\partial M of a smooth, oriented manifold MM is induced from the orientation form of MM by the rule





d​vol∂M:=ιv​d​volM,\mathrm{d}\!\text{vol}_{\partial M}\colon=\iota_{v}\mathrm{d}\!\text{vol}_{M},



where ιv\iota_{v} denotes contraction of the orientation form of MM by a given vector-field vv, which has to be outward-directed from ∂M\partial M, it follows immediately that the 00-th boundary face of △n\triangle_{n} has orientation form −d​voln−1-\mathrm{d}\!\text{vol}_{n-1}, hence one can say that it has orientation −1-1.
In a similar way, by direct computations, it can be proved that any face (∂△n)α\left(\partial\triangle_{n}\right)_{\alpha} has orientation form (−1)α+1​d​voln−1(-1)^{\alpha+1}\mathrm{d}\!\text{vol}_{n-1}, hence the orientation of the n+1n+1 faces of △n\triangle_{n} is given by the rule





or⁡(∂△n)α=(−1)α+1.\orient\left(\partial\triangle_{n}\right)_{\alpha}=(-1)^{\alpha+1}.

 S. Kobayashi, Differential geometry of complex vector bundles, Publications of the Mathematical Society of Japan, 15. Kano Memorial Lectures, 5, Princeton University Press, Princeton, NJ; Iwanami Shoten, (Tokyo, 1987)

Foundations of Differential Geometry. Vol. I,

Instructions for reporting errors
    
We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below:
    
      
Click the "Report Issue" (
            
          ) button, located in the page header.
    
    
Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
    
Our team has already identified the following issues. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all.
    
Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion, and welcome developer contributions.
